That leaves **`pdfium-render` 0.9.3** (MIT OR Apache-2.0, wrapping Google's PDFium, BSD-3-Clause — the engine Chrome uses), and the decisive property is how the native library arrives: `libloading` is a non-optional dependency and no `-sys` crate appears in its normal dependencies, so it **loads PDFium dynamically at run time**. No `links` key, no C++ toolchain at build time — the trap that disqualified `tesseract` for OCR. The deployment supplies `libpdfium`, which is the same mounted-at-runtime shape already chosen for the OCR weights and the embedding model (`ENC-534`), so the operator story is one story rather than three. Still to decide before implementing: whether a page tree is parsed inside the D17 sandbox (it is a parser eating hostile input, which is why `raster.rs` refuses PDFs today) and what a per-page render budget looks like |
| ENC-538 | The vector path's "excerpt" is the whole chunk | P2 | DONE | Named by `ENC-529`. `milvus.rs` passes Milvus's `text` field straight through, so the same document is quoted at ≤240 characters when search is degraded and at up to 3 200 when it is healthy. Defensible in principle — a dense match has no matched *span* to cut around, so it is not the same object — but the inconsistency is caller-visible and should be settled deliberately rather than by which path answered. **Settled 2026-08-21, and not by making them the same for tidiness.** Both paths now cut to the same 240-character budget with the same word boundaries and elision marks, and differ in exactly one respect — which window — because only one of them has an anchor to choose one with. The reasoning: the caller-visible defect this row names is the **size**, and size has nothing to do with anchoring; 3 200 against 240 was not a considered difference, it was `milvus::decode` not cutting. And a head cut is **not** the `ts_headline` failure `ENC-529` refuses: that failure quoted from *outside* the matched span, whereas here the matched unit **is the chunk** — the embedding was computed over all of it and all of it scored — so every window inside it is inside the match. The head is not a guess about where the match is; it is what "this match has no narrower location" looks like. Three alternatives rejected on the record: locating the query's words where they happen to appear (its meaning would depend on an accident, nothing in the response would say which rule produced a given string, and it is backwards — dense retrieval earns its keep on queries whose words are *absent*, so it would excerpt best exactly where dense added least); the whole chunk with an explicit contract (relabels the size difference rather than removing it); and nothing at all on the dense path (a **healthy** search would then disclose strictly less than a degraded one, inverting what `degraded: true` describes). Verified by deliberate violation seven ways, including restoring the passthrough against live Milvus — "the decoder passed the chunk through: 3036 characters against a budget of 240" — and a `None`-everything decoder caught by a short-document positive control, which is `docs/12 §1.2`'s rule doing its job. Follow-ups split out rather than carried here. Also unresolved: excerpts carry no highlighting (`docs/05 §11` shows `<em>`; closing it means carrying matched offsets alongside the text so the API layer marks up without parsing content), and an excerpt is a *fragment*, so bidi state balanced in a whole document can be unbalanced in the quotation — an unterminated U+202E reverses surrounding UI text, and the remedy is `unicode-bidi: isolate` at render rather than stripping, which would break verbatimness |
| ENC-539 | Eight vacuous tests in one session, so the discipline is now written down | P1 | DONE | Not a bug but a pattern, and it kept recurring: a concurrency test that passed 3/3 against a naive implementation because the pool was capped at two connections; `S3`/`S4` staying green with the denylist deleted, because a revocation removes the ACL too; a `D24` test that passed with its empty-document guard removed, because a *different* guard caught it while the comment named the wrong one; an image-bomb test that passed twice for two different wrong reasons; `tests/degraded.rs` asserting `excerpt == None` against a generator that wrote `None` unconditionally; and two source-scanning gates that matched their own source. `docs/12 §1.2` now states the rule — a test is not believed until it has been watched to fail — and names **the recurring shape: an assertion about an absence passes for free.** `excerpt == None`, "no cross-tenant row", "no `set_status` here", "these bytes were never read" all hold trivially against an implementation that does nothing. Two habits close it: include a **positive control** (without a third document that *does* receive its excerpt, every assertion passes against code returning `None` for everything), and when a deliberate violation fails to break a test, treat that as a finding and record which mechanism the test actually proves rather than quietly rewording it |
| ENC-540 | main went red: the OCR tests were `#[ignore]`d and CI ran them anyway | P0 | DONE | **The third time this repo has learned that an `#[ignore]` is not insulation from CI**, after the ClamAV steps (which say it twice in their own comments) and the Milvus tests. `crates/indexing/tests/ocr.rs` was written correctly — `#[ignore]`d with the requirement named at each test, in the shape the PostgreSQL, Milvus and ClamAV suites use — but nothing supplied `ENCLAVE_OCR_MODELS`, so `--include-ignored` ran all four and they failed on a missing mount. Merged before the `test` job finished, which is how it reached `main` rather than a PR. Fixed by provisioning the models in CI the way every other dependency is provisioned: a fetch step separated from the assertions, retried three times, whose failure message says *network* rather than naming OCR. **Fetched, never committed** — the published models are CC-BY-SA-4.0, so downloading them into a throwaway runner is use while baking them into an image or the tree would be redistribution. Two things caught while writing it: `[ "$attempt" -lt 3 ] && sleep` is a bug under `set -e`, killing the step on the last attempt before its own error message prints; and the URLs and the tests were verified locally rather than assumed — 12 MB total, all seven tests pass against the real weights in 21s |
| ENC-541 | Dense-path S6: a metadata-only caller gets no excerpt from a Milvus candidate | P2 | DONE | Named by `ENC-538` rather than absorbed into it. The gate is literally the same `PostFilter::confirm` the lexical S6 test already exercises, so this is coverage rather than a hole — but `ENC-538` made the dense path produce excerpts that are *new objects*, and the assertion that they are withheld from a `MetadataRead`-only caller should run against real Milvus candidates rather than being inferred from the lexical case. Roughly fifteen lines beside `s5_an_over_permissive_index_decides_nothing`. **Done 2026-08-21**, and the shape is what `docs/12 §1.2` asks for: three files, one withheld, one *unquotable* (indexed with an empty `text` — the dense analogue of a name-only match, and a state `milvus::decode` already documents as legitimate because a metadata-only update leaves the body alone), and one readable as the **positive control**. It also asserts the generator's half **first** — the candidate must *carry* an excerpt — so "withheld" cannot be satisfied by "never produced". Three deliberate violations each failed by name, including a decoder returning `None` for everything, which the generator-side guard caught |
| ENC-542 | Excerpt highlighting offsets, and bidi in a fragment | P2 | DONE | `ENC-529`'s follow-up, scoped by `ENC-538`. `docs/05 §11` shows `<em>`; closing it means carrying the matched offsets alongside the text so the API layer marks up **without parsing content**. The dense path has no offsets — nothing matched at a position — so the field is optional and a dense excerpt arrives unmarked, which is a consequence of `ENC-538`'s decision rather than an oversight. Bidi belongs here too: an excerpt is a *fragment*, so state balanced across a whole document can be unbalanced in the quotation, and an unterminated U+202E reverses the surrounding UI text. The remedy is `unicode-bidi: isolate` at render rather than stripping the characters, which would break the verbatimness the excerpt rule is built on. **Shipped 2026-08-21, and the placement is the decision.** Offsets ride **inside** a new `Excerpt` value rather than beside it: a `highlights` field next to `Confirmed::excerpt` breaks the `None`-means-two-things property on the very first withheld response — `excerpt: null` carrying offsets says *there is a passage here you may not see* — and inside the value there is no such response to write. The dense path's absence of offsets is a **variant** (`Highlights::Unlocated`), not an empty `Vec`, because an empty list conflates "the locator found nothing" with "there was never a narrower span", and only the second is what `ENC-538` decided a dense match is; the variant's documentation also tells a renderer to mark **nothing**, closing the misreading that would `<em>` the whole passage. Rule 10 extends to the offsets themselves — a span says *a matched term of this length occurs at this position*, so printing it beside a redacted body gives back part of what the redaction removed — with `postfilter`'s field-level redaction kept as an independent second defence, confirmed independent by breaking each and watching only its own tests fail. Bidi is pinned by a test that fails **if a sanitizer is added**, with the renderer's half in `docs/14 §7`. Seven deliberate violations, each failing by name, including an offset shift that selected `"he tanta"` where `"tantalum"` was meant. **Found a real bug on the way**: the first implementation dropped matched tokens that overran the rendered body, and since `window_for` deliberately cuts mid-token when one token exceeds the whole budget, that left no span, the constructor refused, and the hit lost its excerpt **entirely** — caught by an existing budget test, fixed by clamping to what is visible |
| ENC-543 | The composite-FK gate reports `pass` and has never enforced anything | P1 | DONE | Found while closing `ENC-502`, and it is the sharpest instance of this session's recurring theme. `.github/workflows/structural-gates.yml` guards its assertion on `crates/db/tests/composite_fk_coverage.rs` existing; the file does not exist, so the job emits `GATE PENDING … NOT ENFORCED YET` and **exits 0**. In the checks list a reviewer reads *"RULE: every FK between tenant-scoped tables includes tenant_id — pass"*, in green, for a gate that has never looked at a foreign key. `CLAUDE.md` rule 4 names composite FKs as something "CI fails otherwise" on, and today it does not. The consequence is exactly `ENC-502`: RLS and grant coverage enumerate tenant-scoped **tables**, nothing enumerates their **keys**, so a missing composite key sat as a source comment for a milestone. Note the gate is *honest* in its log — this is unbuilt rather than silently broken — but the check name is what people read. The marker says it "lands with `ENC-106`", which shipped the RLS gate and is `DONE`, so the pending note points at closed work and reads as scheduled rather than dropped. **Written 2026-08-22, and it found three real violations on its first run** — all in the auth tables, all from migration `0001`: `user_credentials(user_id) -> users(id)`, `user_mfa_methods(user_id) -> users(id)` and `refresh_tokens(parent_id) -> refresh_tokens(id)`. The `UNIQUE (tenant_id, id)` convention that makes a composite key *expressible* only arrives in `0005`, so these predate the pattern rather than having ignored it, which is exactly the drift a gate catches and review does not. What they permitted, concretely: a `user_credentials` row — password hash, lockout counter — attached to **another tenant's user**; a `user_mfa_methods` row carrying a TOTP secret reference or WebAuthn public key, likewise; and a refresh token naming a parent in another tenant's family, which reuse detection then walks. RLS closes none of it, because PostgreSQL runs referential-integrity checks with row security **deliberately not enforced**, so the key's own lookup sees every tenant's rows. Migration `0016` adds the two missing `UNIQUE (tenant_id, id)` targets and recreates all three keys compositely; the gate now passes on 26 keys. The escape hatch is gone too: the RLS and composite-FK jobs used to `exit 0` when their test file was missing, so deleting a test silently disarmed its gate — the **grant** gate already had this right ("a deleted gate is a silent green, not a pass"), and the fix was simply never applied to the other two. Verified both directions: a missing test file exits 1 naming the gate, a present one enforces |
| ENC-544 | `lists.slug` and `libraries.slug` have no uniqueness; `workspaces.slug` does | P3 | TODO | Raised by `ENC-502` and scoped out rather than absorbed. `docs/04 §10` states no unique constraint for `lists`, and migration 0015 added none, because making `lists` stricter than the sibling it is shaped after — with no document saying so — is a rule invented in a migration. But `uq_workspace_slug` exists on `workspaces (tenant_id, slug) WHERE deleted_at IS NULL`, so two of the three URL-segment columns disagree with the third. Either slugs address things or they do not; the answer belongs in `docs/04 §10`, and whichever way it goes, one of the three migrations is currently wrong |
| ENC-545 | A scanned PDF is `SKIPPED`, because nothing extracts `application/pdf` | P1 | DONE | The M3 exit-criterion blocker, found by `ENC-537` and named rather than glossed. `NoExtractor` answers for PDF, so `Pipeline::prepare` returns `Outcome::Unsupported` → `SKIPPED` / `unsupported_media_type` — and `OcrRetry` only fires on `NoText`, so **the rasteriser and the OCR engine are both reachable and neither is ever reached**. Needs a PDF text extractor producing per-page segments and, crucially, `TextlessSource::pages_without_text` for the pages that yielded nothing; `docs/07 §3` already names `pdfium` for this and PDFium's text API is now in the graph. Carries a version-marker gap: the *rasteriser* decides what pixels OCR sees, the marker is composed from the OCR extractor alone, and `ExtractorVersion` is `&'static str`, so a mounted library cannot be hashed into it — swapping PDFium is an operator action that needs a manual bump, exactly as with the OCR weights |
| ENC-546 | Nothing constructs `OcrRetry` outside tests | P1 | DONE | The other half of the exit criterion, from `ENC-537`. `crates/worker` builds `Pipeline::new(PlainTextExtractor, …)`; no production code constructs `OcrExtractor`, `OcrRetry` or `PdfiumPages`, and no configuration names `ENCLAVE_OCR_MODELS` or `ENCLAVE_PDFIUM`. The deployment story is now **three mounted volumes** — embedding model, OCR weights, PDFium — and it needs writing where an operator reads it, including `RTEN_NUM_THREADS` beside the worker's CPU limit, since `rten` pulls `rayon` unconditionally (`ENC-535`) |
| ENC-547 | Nothing writes `retrieval_denylist.indexed_seq`, because nothing writes Milvus at all | P2 | DONE | The surviving half of `ENC-528`, re-scoped after checking rather than carried forward on its old premise. Not "the removal path does not exist yet" but **no write path of any kind exists**: `crates/search/src/milvus.rs` can create the collection, search it, count it and ping it, and that is the whole surface. So the earlier framing — "the producer is whoever eventually deletes chunks" — understated it by one step. The producer arrives with whatever performs the store write, and must call `confirm_indexed` **after** the store call using the `SuppressionSeq` that `suppress` already returns for exactly this handoff — never a seq re-read afterwards, which would silently absorb a suppression that landed during the write. Until then `NULL` means unknown and every row is honest. Nothing in the read path may become conditional on it; `neither_the_search_read_nor_the_lift_consults_the_catch_up_columns` is the standing guard |
| ENC-548 | The worker process has no metrics socket, so its gauges reach nobody | P2 | DONE | Named by `ENC-528` rather than glossed, and it is the *first* time this bites because no worker pass had ever published a metric before. The instruments are process-wide statics and the only exposition is `crates/api/src/metrics_listener.rs`, so a coverage pass scheduled in the worker publishes into a registry nothing scrapes — the same shape as `ENC-521`, where metrics existed for a while with no listener and read as zero forever. Two options, and it is a placement decision the scheduler owns: give the worker binary its own listener, which wants `serve`/`router` moved below `enclave-api` since worker → api is backwards, or schedule the coverage pass in the API process. `docs/11 §5.7` step 5 now says to check this when the gauges are absent. **Closed 2026-08-22, and wider than scoped, because the socket was the smaller half:** nothing called *any* of the four passes — `index_pass`, `invalidation::sweep`, `coverage::probe_pass` and the epoch reconciler each had exactly one caller and it was its own test. `crates/worker/src/main.rs` is now a real binary and was run end to end against the dev stack: migrations applied, `/metrics` scraped `200`, and SIGTERM exited 0 **in under a millisecond against a 300-second interval**, which is the wake-up path working in the real process rather than in a test. The worker got its **own** listener, with `serve`/`router` moved to `enclave_observability::exposition` behind an optional feature so a bottom-of-graph crate does not oblige the workspace to compile axum; the alternative would have moved a BYPASSRLS enumerator and a slow external census into the request-serving process. `crates/api/src/metrics_listener.rs` remains the API's binding point, so `xtask policy-routing` keeps exactly one permitted file — verified by adding `/metrics` to the API router and watching the gate refuse. The tenant enumerator lives **inside `crates/db`**, so `platform_connection` still has zero callers elsewhere and one grep is still a complete list of where RLS is bypassed. Deny-by-default is structural rather than argued: a pass whose dependency is absent is **not scheduled at all**, because `claim` commits before the first byte is read, so a pass pointed at a dead store would burn the `attempts` budget that quarantines poisoned documents on a deployment whose only problem is a missing bucket. Eleven controls broken on purpose, each failing by name |
| ENC-549 | A section renumber broke five citations, and I did the renumbering | P3 | DONE | Inserting `docs/12 §1.1` ("test our integration, not somebody else's correctness") pushed the deliberate-violation rule to `§1.2`, and five files cite it by number — two test files, two source files and the tracker. A concurrent session caught it and reported it rather than half-fixing it, which was right: a partial sweep is worse than none, because the surviving citations still look correct. Swept in one edit. The general lesson is `docs/README.md`'s cross-reference rule doing exactly what it is for, in the direction people forget — **the danger is not the reference going stale, it is the section moving underneath a reference that still resolves to something plausible.** A reader following `§1.1` after this change would have landed on a real rule, just not the one being cited |
| ENC-550 | main went red again: the OCR round-trip tests were timing-dependent | P0 | DONE | `a_scanned_text_free_pdf_is_read_back_as_text` and `a_scanned_pdf_of_blank_pages_fails_rather_than_indexing_as_empty` both failed on CI with `Refused(Timeout)` while passing locally, because `RenderBudget::DEFAULT`'s 30-second clock is comfortable on an Apple Silicon laptop — the suite runs in about 22 seconds — and is not on a hosted runner. **A test whose verdict depends on how fast the machine is will keep doing that, intermittently, and every occurrence teaches somebody that the OCR path is flaky** — which is how a real failure eventually gets waved through. The fix is the repo owner's own rule (`docs/12 §1.1`) applied to a budget: these two assert *our wiring* — a scanned PDF reaches `READY` with its page recorded, a blank one does not — and how long `ocrs` takes to do that is the engine's business. They now run under an `UNTIMED` budget whose only difference from `DEFAULT` is the clock. The twelve tests that assert the budget **itself** keep `DEFAULT` and tightened values, because there the bound is the thing under test, and `tests/bounds.rs` still proves `Refusal::Timeout` against a deliberately stuck extractor — so nothing about the timeout path went untested |
| ENC-551 | Concurrent PDFium document work crashes the process — and `ENC-537` shipped it | P0 | DONE | Found by `ENC-545` while building the text extractor, and it is a memory-safety incident inside another item rather than anything that row's title would lead you to. `pdfium-render`'s `thread_safe` feature locks each FFI **call**; two threads interleaving two **documents** share globals PDFium never made re-entrant. Measured, not inferred: two threads reading text off font-carrying PDFs died **7 of 8 runs** with `SIGTRAP`/`SIGABRT`/`SIGSEGV`, and a temporary probe running four concurrent *rasterisations* of a font-carrying PDF died **5 of 6** — so the `ENC-537` rasteriser already on `main` has the same defect, and only its fixtures hid it, because image-only pages never touch the font machinery. A worker indexing two PDFs at once would have died. Fixed with a process-wide `DOCUMENTS` mutex held for a document's whole life, taken *after* the sniff and input cap so a non-PDF cannot queue behind a real one; both modules take it, and 0 crashes in 10 consecutive parallel runs. Poisoning is recovered from rather than propagated, argued at the constructor: one hostile file must not permanently disable PDF handling and then have that reported as a verdict about every innocent document after it. `docs/12 §4.8` **G10**. The cost is real and stated — PDFium work in this process is now serial per document, which is a throughput bound the D17 sandbox would remove by putting each document in its own process |
| ENC-552 | Nothing routes a media type to an extractor, so a deployment can serve one format | P1 | DONE | Named by `ENC-545`, and it blocks the M3 exit criterion as squarely as that row did. `Pipeline<E>` holds a **single** `Extractor`, and only tests construct one — so `PlainTextExtractor` and `PdfTextExtractor` cannot coexist in a running worker, and `ENC-545`'s extractor is proven and unreachable. `Extractor::supports` already exists and is consulted before any bytes reach a parser, which is the hook a router would dispatch on. Coordinate with `ENC-546`, which owns the worker's construction of the OCR stage. **Built 2026-08-22** as `MediaTypeRouter`, dispatching on the `Extractor::supports` hook that was already consulted before any bytes reached a parser — and it is *itself* an `Extractor`, so `Pipeline` and every existing test are unchanged. Three decisions argued rather than defaulted: an ambiguous type is refused **at construction** rather than resolved by declaration order, because an order that decides which parser reads every PDF is one somebody changes while tidying a list; an unrouted type stays `SKIPPED` structurally; and the version marker is **declared by the deployment and verified against every member's**, per `+`-component rather than by `contains` — `text/1` is a substring of `pdf-text/1`, which is the positive control. `router/1` alone was rejected as the dangerous option, since bumping a member would leave `docs/07 §3`'s reindex trigger comparing an unchanged string: a trigger that silently stops triggering. Costs stated rather than discovered: it over-reindexes, and the manifest no longer names which extractor ran. Ten deliberate violations, each failing by name |
| ENC-553 | A mixed PDF's scanned pages are never OCR'd | P2 | TODO | D24's shape at a smaller scale, named by `ENC-545` rather than hidden. A ninety-page report with three scanned exhibits is `Extracted` — because `TextDocument::is_empty` is false — so it reaches `READY` over eighty-seven pages and **nothing on any surface says the exhibits are missing**. The alternative was worse and was rejected deliberately: returning `NoText` would make `OcrRetry` rebuild the document from the pages it recognised *and nothing else*, discarding ten typed pages to recover three scanned ones. Closing it needs an outcome carrying **both** a document and a work list, plus a merge in `OcrRetry` — not a loosening of its pass-through, which is the thing that stops OCR turning "this document failed" into "this document is empty". The empty per-page segments already carry the page numbers such a merge would need |
| ENC-554 | `ENCLAVE_TEST_*` collided with the config prefix and stopped processes starting | P1 | DONE | `ConfigLoader::new()` reads the whole process environment, so `ENCLAVE_TEST_S3_SECRET_ACCESS_KEY` arrived as a field named `test_s3_secret_access_key`, was correctly classed as a credential at 57 bits against a 48-bit threshold, and **any process started from a developer's shell refused to start, naming a field nobody had written**. Fixed by renaming the family out of the reserved prefix, including a fourth site the brief did not name — `ENCLAVE_TEST_CLAMD_ADDR` — which would have made the new rule false. The loader carve-out was rejected for the reason that matters: **a skipped prefix makes the loader *drop* an override an operator set**, and a process that starts on the wrong value is worse than one that refuses to start; it also has to grow into a list, each entry a config path permanently unreachable. A rename has one failure mode — a missed site — and it is loud: exporting only the old names made all twelve MinIO tests fail naming `TEST_S3_ENDPOINT`. `crates/config/tests/ambient_environment.rs` loads configuration from the real process environment so the class cannot return silently, proved both directions, with the credential value assembled at run time per rule 11 |
| ENC-555 | `idx_lists_workspace` is redundant with `uq_list_slug` | P3 | TODO | `0015` created `(tenant_id, workspace_id) WHERE deleted_at IS NULL`; `0017`'s `uq_list_slug` is the same predicate with a third column, so it already serves that listing. Deliberately not dropped in the same migration: a correctness fix for slugs and an availability change to a read path do not belong together, because reverting either would revert both |
| ENC-556 | `ENCLAVE_DEV_*` still squats in the reserved config prefix | P2 | TODO | About eighteen compose variables, `ENCLAVE_DEV_DB_PASSWORD` and `ENCLAVE_DEV_MINIO_PASSWORD` among them. Normally read by `docker compose` from a `.env` rather than exported, so they do not reach a loading process — but exported, they hit the scanner by exactly `ENC-554`'s route. Renaming touches `deploy/compose/dev.yml` and every developer's local `.env`, which is why it is its own row. Two smaller instances in the same family: `crates/config`'s own unit tests `set_var` two `ENCLAVE_TEST_*` names into the live test process, harmless until a test in that binary calls `ConfigLoader::new()`; and `deploy/compose/dev.yml` defaults MinIO to `enclave-dev-minio` while CI and `deploy/README.md` use `enclave-dev-secret`, so a developer following the README against the compose stack gets 403s |
| ENC-557 | The indexing worker does not write the vector store | P1 | DONE | `ENC-547` built `VectorWriter` and nothing calls it. `crates/worker/src/indexing.rs` writes `chunk_text` and the manifest; nothing embeds a chunk or calls `upsert_chunks`, so the collection is empty in a real deployment, every dense search returns nothing, and `indexed_seq` still reads unasserted everywhere. The reason has changed rather than gone — from "there is nothing to confirm" to "the producer exists and is not wired". Needs `enclave-embeddings` wired in, which also lands `ENC-533`'s compile-time width agreement, since the worker would then be the crate that legitimately depends on both |
| ENC-558 | A re-index to a new version orphans the old version's chunks | P2 | TODO | Named by `ENC-547`. `chunk_id` is `uuid_v5` over `version_id`, so re-indexing a file at a **new** version upserts a new set and leaves the previous one in place; `remove_file` is whole-file only, and the missing operation is version-scoped (`file_id == {file} and version_id != {keep}`). Not a leak — the post-filter still resolves every candidate — but permanent over-fetch carrying stale `acl_tokens` that nothing knows to refresh, which is `ENC-513`'s duplicate case made durable |
| ENC-559 | `docs/07 §6.3`'s metadata-only update has no implementation | P2 | TODO | A permission or classification change should rewrite a chunk's scalars **without re-embedding** — that is the whole point of §6.3, and it is what makes a subtree ACL change O(chunks) rather than O(re-embedding). It needs the existing vectors read back as output fields, or the vector cache §6.3 describes, keyed by `chunk_id`. `upsert_chunks` takes full records, so today the only way to refresh `acl_tokens` is a complete re-index of the file |
| ENC-560 | `page_number` cannot express "no page" | P3 | TODO | The collection declares it `Int32` non-nullable, so an unpaginated document writes `0` and pages are 1-based. That is precisely the sentinel the `varchar`/`required_varchar` split exists to avoid, on the integer axis: "absent" and "page zero" become one value, and a filter written against either silently matches the other. Fixing it is a `docs/07 §4` schema decision plus a collection rebuild, which is why it was documented on the field rather than changed under a row about writing |
| ENC-561 | Nothing stops a `NO_INDEX` chunk being written to the vector store | P3 | TODO | `docs/07 §4` says those classifications never reach the collection. There is no `NO_INDEX` concept anywhere in the Rust tree — only `ClassificationRank(i32)` — so the writer has nothing to check, and inventing the vocabulary inside the search crate would put a classification decision in the crate furthest from classification. Belongs with whichever crate first gives a classification an *indexing disposition* |
| ENC-562 | `enclave-config` models no `storage:` section, so nothing can build a `BlobStore` | P1 | DONE | The fourth blocker on M3's last exit criterion, and it is *configuration* rather than wiring. `crates/worker` is wired for indexing and returns `None` at one function because there is nothing to build an `S3BlobStore` from. **It cannot be closed from the config side as things stand**: `enclave-storage` depends on `enclave-config`, so the `S3Config` an operator writes cannot be modelled by the crate that parses it without a dependency cycle — the shape is a decision for whoever lands `docs/08 §15`'s storage section. Until then the indexing pass is not scheduled and says so at start-up, `chunk_text` stays empty, and search stays lexical-only. Note what was deliberately *not* done: pointing the pass at an unconfigured store would still `claim` — a committed transaction — before failing the read, burning the `attempts` budget on every file it touched. **Closed 2026-08-22 using the shape this repo already documents**: `enclave-config` holds `StorageConfig`/`S3StorageConfig` — what an *operator writes* — and `enclave-storage` converts, exactly as `db_config_from` does for the database. The two alternatives are recorded at the type rather than left implicit: inverting the dependency would put `aws-sdk-s3` behind **every** crate in the workspace, since everything depends on `enclave-config`; and an untyped subtree gives up the property the crate exists for, that `load` reports every problem in one pass rather than one at a time inside a binary's start-up. The config struct is deliberately *smaller* than `S3Config` — no multipart pacing, which carries no correctness — which is what makes the duplication a decision rather than an accident, and `S3Flavor` is not duplicated at all: it moved to `config` and `storage` re-exports it. A bucket that was **named and cannot be reached** is an error rather than a `None`, because only one of those two absences is an operator's decision. Proved end to end against the dev MinIO: YAML text → `ConfigLoader` → `S3Config` → signed PUT → range read, with credentials as `env://` references |
| ENC-563 | No `search:` section, so the coverage probe still has no census | P2 | DONE | The other half of `ENC-562`, one section along, and what stops `ENC-548` from fully clearing `SearchIndexCoverageUnreported`. `MilvusIndex` is the only `IndexCensus` and `MilvusConfig::new(uri, dimension)` has no configured source. A fabricated default would be **worse than the absence**: a census pointed at a URI nobody set fails, every tenant counts `unreadable`, and the gauges report a fleet-wide outage that is really a missing config key. The listener now exists and serves counters at zero; the producer needs configuration. **Closed 2026-08-22 alongside `ENC-562`**, and with one deliberate omission: **the embedding width is not a configuration key.** `index_census` reads `enclave_embeddings::model::ACTIVE.dimension`, because a configurable width is a way to write down a mistake that errors at neither end — Milvus accepts the width its collection was created with, the model emits the width it was trained at, and retrieval quietly degrades. That is `ENC-533`'s single-source-of-truth arriving by a better route than a cross-crate assertion. Milvus unreachable **degrades** rather than refusing start-up, unlike storage, and both sides of that asymmetry are argued in place |
| ENC-564 | `platform_connection` documented a lint that did not exist | P2 | DONE | Its own doc comment said *"this method is on the deny-list of the ENC-110 routing lint. Same family as `ENC-543`: a control that reads as enforced and is not. `.github/scripts/no_raw_pool.py` now fails on any `.platform_connection(` outside `crates/db`, verified by adding one to `crates/worker` and watching it fail. The rule is "not outside `crates/db`" rather than a caller allowlist, deliberately: it keeps one grep a complete list of where tenant isolation is bypassed, and puts every cross-tenant `WHERE` clause where a single review covers them |
| ENC-565 | A suspended tenant's coverage gauges freeze at their healthiest value | P3 | TODO | Surfaced by `ENC-548` rather than introduced by it. `docs/11 §12` pauses background processing for `SUSPENDED`, so the enumerator excludes them — which means their coverage gauges stop being *refreshed* rather than being removed, and a tenant suspended while healthy leaves a healthy-looking series behind indefinitely. `metrics::search::forget_tenant` exists for deletion and has no suspension analogue. Recorded in `docs/11 §3.1` and §12 rather than silently accepted; the fix is either forgetting the series on suspension, or exporting tenant status as a series an operator can join against |
| ENC-566 | The API and the worker read the same `metrics_port`, so one of them fails to bind | P1 | DONE | Found by reading the two binaries side by side after `ENC-548` gave the worker a listener. Both take `config.server.metrics_port`, and `deploy/config/enclave.example.yaml` sets it to `9464` — so on a host running both from one `enclave.yaml`, **whichever starts second dies with `Address already in use`**, and it dies at start-up rather than degrading, because the bind is a `?`. Worse, `deploy/monitoring/prometheus.yml` already scrapes **9464 for the API and 9465 for the worker**, so the monitoring config encodes two ports that the configuration cannot express. Nobody hit it because the worker was a `println!` until today. The fix is a config-shape decision rather than a patch, which is why this is a row: either the exposition port moves under each process's own section (so `server.metrics_port` becomes wrong for both), or the binaries take it from somewhere process-specific — and note that `server.*` is the wrong home either way, since a worker serves no HTTP API and has no `bind`, `public_url` or `trusted_proxies`. Whichever way it goes, `prometheus.yml` and the example config have to end up agreeing, because right now they do not |
| ENC-567 | Four "exists but nothing calls it" incidents in one session, so it is now a report | P2 | DONE | Not a bug but a pattern, and it cost four separate incidents: the composite-FK gate whose test file did not exist, so the job exited 0 and reported **pass** in green for a milestone (`ENC-543`); `render_prometheus()` producing an exposition nothing served, so the metrics read as zero forever and no alert could fire (`ENC-521`); the four worker passes each called only by their own test while the binary was a `println!` (`ENC-548`); and `platform_connection` documenting itself as covered by a lint that did not exist, unnoticed because it had **zero** callers (`ENC-564`). `.github/scripts/unwired_report.py` lists every public function whose only callers are tests. **A report, not a gate, and deliberately so** — it lists 46 on `main`, most of them legitimate: `crates/api` serves six surfaces, so `create_file`, `rename`, `redeem` and the rest are unwired *on purpose*, and failing a build on that would be failing it on the project's own phasing. The distinction that matters is not unwired versus wired but **visibly** unwired versus **invisibly** unwired: a repository method with no endpoint is obvious to everyone, whereas all four incidents had something *asserting* they were connected — a green check, an alert rule, a doc comment — so the absence read as presence. The value is therefore the diff: a name appearing here *after* something starts claiming it is reachable is the signal. It already demonstrates its own use — 50 before `#54`, 46 after, the difference being the worker passes finally getting a caller |
| ENC-569 | main red again: an OCR test asserted the engine's accuracy, not our wiring | P0 | DONE | `a_scanned_page_is_read_back_and_carries_the_page_it_came_from` asserted the recognised text contained `INVOICE`. On CI's Linux runner `ocrs` read **`"NVOCE\nTOTAL"`** off the same rendered page — it dropped the leading `I` — and the test failed while passing on macOS. That is the *engine's* accuracy against a platform's font rasterisation, which is exactly what the repo owner ruled out of scope and what `docs/12 §1.1` was written to say: **we test our integration, not a third party's correctness.** Same shape as `ENC-550`'s timeout — a verdict that depends on which machine ran it, and every recurrence teaches somebody that the OCR path is flaky until a real failure gets waved through. The assertion now requires that *something* readable was recovered, never *what*, and the load-bearing assertions are unchanged: the outcome is `READY`, and the chunk carries the page it came from. Verified still load-bearing rather than assumed — stubbing the stage to pass its input through fails it by name with `NoText(TextlessSource { pages_without_text: [1] })`. Worth noting the rule was written this morning and the test predates it by hours: writing a rule down does not retroactively fix the code that violates it |
| ENC-568 | The canonical field-count guard is a `debug_assert`, so a release build has none | P2 | DONE | Found while auditing every doc comment that *claims* a gate enforces it — the sweep `ENC-564` prompted, after `platform_connection` documented a lint that did not exist. Two of the three claims in the tree are honest: `crates/api`'s policy-routing lint is real and was verified today, and `crates/core`'s compile-fail note says plainly that `trybuild` "is deliberately not wired up yet". The third is true **in debug only**. `canonical.rs` says "a field added without a version bump fails the build rather than the chain", and what enforces it is `debug_assert_eq!(w.fields, CANONICAL_FIELD_COUNT)` inside the encoder — compiled out in release. The unit test beside the constant is `assert_eq!(CANONICAL_FIELD_COUNT, 23)`, which is close to a tautology on its own: it fires when somebody *changes the constant*, not when somebody adds a field and forgets to. So the property holds today because CI runs `cargo test` in debug, and would vanish silently the day anyone adds a release-mode test job — a control that stops being a control under a different build profile, with nothing saying so. **Fixed rather than merely recorded**: the encoder's check is now `assert_eq!`, and the test beside the constant encodes a real event instead of comparing the constant with itself. The branch costs one integer comparison against a SHA-256 of the whole event, which is the right trade for this invariant — a mismatch means the bytes are not canonical v1, so every hash computed from them is meaningless, and the chain is what proves the log has not been edited. Writing a wrong link into that chain is worse than refusing to write one. Proved by simulating the actual mistake — a field added to the encoder with the constant left alone: it now fails in **release** as well as debug, where before it passed silently |
| ENC-570 | The worker's extractor is not wrapped in `BoundedExtractor` | P1 | DONE | Found by `ENC-552` while composing the router, and it is a **live safety gap in the only deployment that ships**. `crates/worker/src/main.rs` passes `PlainTextExtractor` into the pipeline runner unwrapped, so the wall clock, the input cap, the output cap **and D24's empty-document conversion** — all of which `BoundedExtractor` applies *from outside* — are absent in production. Every test in `crates/indexing` runs wrapped, which is exactly why nothing catches it: the bounds are exercised constantly and the shipped composition is not. One line to fix, and it belongs with the wiring that is rewriting that call site anyway. Worth noting what it means: a hostile document reaching the shipped worker today is parsed under no bound at all. **Fixed in the same edit that assembled the router**, which is where the call site was being rewritten anyway |
| ENC-571 | The manifest cannot say which extractor read a document | P2 | TODO | Fallout of `ENC-552`, named rather than hidden. `index_manifests.extractor_version` now records the *router's* marker for every file, so a reader cannot tell whether a document was read by the text extractor or by PDFium — and bumping one member reindexes files the other produced. The truth exists: `TextDocument::extractor_version` carries it and `Prepared` drops it, and the worker compounds that by reading one marker at construction and recording it for the whole pass. Closing it is a field on `Prepared` plus a per-document `BuildVersions`, which touches the worker and so was deliberately not smuggled in behind a router |
| ENC-572 | `text.rs` indexes an ASCII PDF declared `text/plain` as prose | P3 | TODO | Found while writing `ENC-552`'s module documentation and **confirmed by running it**: `PlainTextExtractor` has no signature to match, so a PDF whose objects are all ASCII decodes cleanly and its syntax is chunked as paragraphs — the extractor returned `"%PDF-1.4\n1 0 obj\n…"` as prose. Bounded, since every segment is charged against the output cap, and it **predates the router** — a router changes which extractor a *correctly* declared source reaches and nothing about a misdeclared one. But a router is where the next person will look for the check, which is why it is recorded here. The fix is a negative sniff in `text.rs`: refuse bytes carrying another format's signature. That changes an extractor's own verdicts, so it is its own row |
| ENC-573 | `docs/08 §15` documented a `storage.profile` key that resolved to nothing | P3 | DONE | Found by `ENC-562` while modelling the section. The example carried `storage.profile: "tenant-default"`, naming a row in `docs/04 §4`'s `storage_profiles` table — and **no migration creates that table**, so an operator following the documented example was writing a key that referred to nothing. Removed, with the removal argued in the document rather than silently dropped, and `§4`'s per-tenant override restated as landing later *in addition to* the deployment-wide store. Same family as `ENC-564` and `ENC-543`: documentation describing machinery that does not exist, which is read as description rather than as intent |
| ENC-574 | Nothing can resolve a file's effective classification, so nothing can be embedded | P1 | DONE | Found by `ENC-557`, and it is the reason its stage refuses every document in any deployment that wires it. `ClassifiedText` requires the **effective** rank — the label after the classification stage has run — and there is no `classifications` table in `migrations/`, while `crates/classification` is a *policy stage that allows*, not a rank resolver. So the rank is a port (`FileClassification`) whose shipped implementation refuses, and that is deliberate because **both plausible defaults are wrong in opposite, undetectable directions**: a low default sends every document to a remote embedding provider while the ceiling comparison works perfectly — which is the S8 breach itself, arriving through a constant that looks reasonable, and the session demonstrated exactly that by trying it and watching its remote double panic. A high default writes a `classification_rank` no caller's ceiling admits, filing a document that is visible in the tree and absent from every search. Neither failure is visible from the outside, which is why the port refuses instead. Belongs in `crates/classification` when that crate becomes a resolver **Landed, and the framing in this row was incomplete.** Both defaults *are* wrong in opposite, undetectable directions — and that is an argument for not choosing, not for refusing forever. M4 had already solved the same problem under another name: `facts_unavailable`. So `ENC-574` follows that precedent exactly. **Unresolved is representable**: `enclave_core::ClassificationResolution::unlabelled` is a value, `ClassificationOutcome` is `#[must_use]` with three arms — `Labelled`, `Denied`, `Assumed` — and no arm a caller can mistake for a rank. **What an absence means is tenant policy** (D27): `Unlabelled` is `FAIL_CLOSED` (the default, and the behaviour every deployment already has) or `Assume(rank)` with the rank named by the **tenant**, and it implements `Serialize` and neither `Deserialize` nor `FromStr`, so no request can carry one — which matters more here than for `FactsUnavailable`, because the payload *is* a rank applied to content the caller is about to act on. **`FAIL_CLOSED` is mandatory only for the sensitive case**: `is_forced_closed` covers `Action::is_external_share` and deliberately not `exposes_content`, because a control that refuses every read of every unlabelled document is the one people switch off wholesale — the second half of this row's own argument. `alters_existing_share` is absent because it is half a question whose other half is the resource's `Exposure`, which this type is not given (`ENC-658`). **The table** is `migrations/0022_classifications.sql`: `tenant_id` first, RLS enabled and forced, the four `classification_id` columns `docs/04 §§7–8` have carried since 0004/0005 made into **composite** keys, and no `DELETE` for `enclave_app` on grounds of its own — one statement declassifies in bulk, and `security_facts.classification_rank` and the vector collection's copy are protected by no key. Withdrawal is the granted door and **does not stop a label resolving** for content carrying it, or it would be that same `DELETE`. **Effective means a walk**, and the walk is deliberately **not** `enclave_authorization`'s (`ENC-141` says read it and reuse *if the shapes match* — they do not). The ACL walk stops at `inherit_permissions = FALSE`, correctly, because breaking inheritance materialises the entries onto the node. **Nothing materialises a label.** A label walk that honoured the flag would drop an inherited `RESTRICTED` through a documented operation — `ENC-141`'s escalation, one control over — so reusing it would have imported the bug pre-merged. Resolution is the **maximum** rank over the file, its ancestors, its library default and its workspace default: nearest-wins would let a `PUBLIC` subfolder declassify a restricted tree, and `docs/07 §14` already composes labels this way. A chain past the 64 cap is an **error**, never `Ok(None)`. **Nineteen tests**, thirteen against a live database. Three deliberate breaks failed nothing and are recorded rather than smoothed over: `is_forced_closed` neutered to `false` left the whole workspace green (there was no test — there is now); dropping the `tenant_id` predicate from the label joins, *even with this table's policy weakened to `USING (true)`*, leaves the cross-tenant test green, because the walk anchors on `files` and the composite key already makes a foreign label unattachable — held by the module's own unit test instead; and `the_walk_actually_recurses` asserting `UNION ALL` + `FROM ancestry a` stayed green with the entire recursive term deleted, which is `ENC-594`'s finding one layer up, now asserted on the parent join. Two unit assertions failed the first time they were run. Leakage rows `D10`–`D14`; `docs/04 §12.4`. **What is not done**: `crates/dlp` and `crates/worker` belong to other sessions, so the two call sites are `ENC-655` and `ENC-656` with the exact patches recorded, and `crates/config` has no `classification.unlabelled` key yet (`ENC-659`) |
| ENC-575 | The embedding port has no sparse output, so the collection's sparse field stays empty | P2 | TODO | `ACTIVE.sparse` records that `bge-m3` emits learned-sparse vectors **from the same forward pass** — which was one of the two reasons it was chosen (`ENC-534`) — but `EmbeddingProvider::embed` returns dense only, so hybrid retrieval runs on the dense side alone and the `SPARSE_INVERTED_INDEX` field the collection has carried since `ENC-524` is still populated by nothing. Recall, not correctness: the post-filter is unaffected and every hit is still resolved against PostgreSQL |
| ENC-576 | `IndexPass.embedded` has no gauge, so the deployment that embeds nothing looks healthy | P2 | TODO | Named by `ENC-557`. `embedded` flat at zero while `indexed` climbs is *precisely* the shape of a deployment where the vector stage is unwired or refusing — and it is currently visible only in a `debug!` line. Same failure family as `ENC-521`: a number that exists in the process and reaches no operator. The gauges beside it (`enclave_search_index_*`) already exist and are scraped, so this is one instrument short of a complete picture |
| ENC-577 | The worker assembles a router, so a deployment can index text and PDF | P1 | DONE | The assembly `ENC-552`, `ENC-545` and `ENC-546` each stopped one step short of, and the last *code* step on M3's exit criterion. `crates/worker/src/main.rs` now builds a `MediaTypeRouter` — plain text always, `application/pdf` **only when PDFium is mounted**. Registering PDF unconditionally would make a deployment that never wanted it fail to start for want of a volume; registering it and letting extraction fail would be D24's shape, every PDF a `FAILED` manifest for a reason that is the deployment's rather than the document's. The routing marker is a literal at the composition root and the router verifies it per `+`-component, so bumping an extractor without bumping the marker refuses to start rather than leaving `docs/07 §3`'s reindex trigger comparing an unchanged string. `MountedOcr::library()` hands out the **same** `PdfiumLibrary` the OCR stage rasterises with rather than mounting a second: `ENC-551`'s `DOCUMENTS` lock is per-library, so two libraries would be two locks, which is no lock at all. **A note on how this was verified**: the first attempt was to run the binary with a broken marker and read the log, which printed nothing and proved nothing — the process exits at configuration load long before it reaches the router. The guard is now held by two tests over the shipped constants, and dropping `text/1` from the marker fails by name with `UnnamedBuild { component: "text/1" }` |
| ENC-578 | The exit criterion's last unjoined boundary: a pass writes, a search reads | P1 | DONE | M3 asks that a document be "searchable by its content", and three tests already covered that path in overlapping segments with real components at every step — `tests/pdf.rs` takes a scanned PDF through PDFium and `ocrs` to chunks citing their page, `ocr_mounts.rs` takes a textless outcome through the mounted stage to committed `chunk_text`, and `lexical_content.rs` takes `chunk_text` to a search hit. **What none of them did was cross the last join in one process**: a *pass* writing rows and a *search* reading them back. That is exactly where an assumption hides — the pass writing a shape the search does not read, or under a tenant it does not scope to — and either would leave every existing test green, which is this session's whole theme. `text_an_indexing_pass_committed_is_text_lexical_search_finds` runs a real pass, then searches for a word **read out of the rows the pass wrote** rather than one written into the test: asserting a literal would make it a claim about *extraction*, which on the OCR path is the engine's accuracy against a platform's font rasterisation — the defect `ENC-569` removed for failing on Linux while passing on macOS. Verified load-bearing: removing the grant so the post-filter drops the hit fails it by name — "a document the pass indexed was not findable by a word the pass itself stored (searched \"perihelion\")" |
| ENC-579 | Six tracker statuses silently reverted during a rebase | P2 | DONE | Noticed while assessing M3: `ENC-545`, `ENC-546` and `ENC-566` read `TODO` and `ENC-557`, `ENC-577`, `ENC-578` read `REVIEW`, all six long after they had merged with green CI. The row *text* survived every rebase; the status cell did not, because my conflict resolution kept both sides' rows by ID and the older side's status won. So the tracker was under-reporting completed work — the same "the signal says one thing and the state says another" pattern this session kept finding in gates and doc comments, arriving in my own bookkeeping. The rollup gate could not catch it: it verifies the table against the rows, and the rows were self-consistently wrong. Worth knowing for the next parallel batch — a status is a one-word cell, which is exactly the kind of edit a merge loses quietly, and re-reading the rows against what actually merged is the only check that finds it |
| ENC-580 | M4 plan: the governance baseline, with its decisions locked | P1 | DONE | `ROADMAP.md` recorded M4 as *"nothing logged yet — M4 has not been broken into rows"*, while two Phase 2 items already depend on it by name. `plans/M4-GOVERNANCE.md` breaks it down in the shape `M3-DISCOVERY.md` uses, with seven decisions locked before the design sets. The milestone's one sentence is about **rollout** rather than correctness: *a control that cannot be turned on gradually will be turned on carelessly, or not at all* — and the failure it is arranged against is not a control that blocks wrongly but one nobody dares enable, which is indistinguishable from an absent control except that it appears in the compliance answer. Four of the six policy stages are still `Unconfigured` stubs that permit everything, and `crates/dlp` and `crates/conditional_access` export one public item each |
| ENC-581 | M4/D26 — `SecurityFacts` are gathered once per request and passed down | P1 | DONE | The obvious argument is cost: facts are a read per resource and the chain has ten stages. The real one is that a stage re-fetching can observe **different** facts from the stage before it — a scan completing mid-chain flips `facts_unavailable` between DLP and retention, and the request is decided against two views of the same document. Not a race that occasionally gives a wrong answer, but one that gives a decision **nobody can reconstruct from the audit row**, because the row records one of the two. Consequence accepted and written down: facts are as of the start of the request  **Landed in `crates/core/src/policy.rs` and `crates/dlp`.** The value threaded down is a `FactsSnapshot`, not an `Option<SecurityFacts>`, and the difference is where the freshness question is answered: `gathered()` takes the *active* `DetectorSetVersion` and settles it once, at the single point facts enter the request, so no accessor can hand out counts the active rules never produced. Freshness is **equality, never an ordering** — the column is opaque `TEXT`, and any ordering we invented over somebody else's build string fails one-directionally: a version that sorts unexpectedly high reads as fresh and stale facts decide a request that believes it saw the current rules. **D27 is structural rather than documented**: `FactsUnavailable` implements `Serialize` and neither `Deserialize` nor `FromStr`, so there is no route from bytes on the wire to a value of it — a request cannot carry an override even as a field somebody meant to ignore — and `require()` takes only the action and the rank, which are facts about the attempt rather than choices about it. The `RESTRICTED`/external-share escalation is asserted for **both** configured modes, with the fail-open case proven to genuinely fail open, because a table of denials passes for free against a policy that denies everything. Detectors are Q16's shape and nothing more: a closed `CandidateClass` enum and a `validate(&Candidate) -> Verdict` — **there is nowhere to put a pattern**, which is what forces the custom-pattern request onto the asynchronous path instead of quietly onto this one. Luhn and ISO 7064 MOD 97-10 written out (twenty lines, no allocation, no new dependency) with published vectors. `Candidate` redacts itself in `Debug` as `<content withheld>`'s sibling `<candidate withheld>`, following `crates/search/src/excerpt.rs`; `SecurityFacts` needs no redaction because **it has no field a match value could occupy**, which is a property worth stating rather than an accident. The one real bug the tests caught was mine: the first tokeniser absorbed spaces and offered only the joined run, which found `4111 1111 1111 1111` and **missed an IBAN in a sentence** — the clever case working while the common one failed. A run is now offered joined *and* in tokens. Not threaded through `PolicyEngine::enforce` yet: that changes six trait signatures two parallel sessions are inside, and it belongs with the DLP modes (`ENC-582`) |
| ENC-582 | M4/D28 — `SIMULATION` must be indistinguishable from `ENFORCE` except in its effect | P1 | DONE | Landed as the whole of M4 step 3: the `FactsSnapshot` threading, the five modes, obligations that cost something, and the `crates/config` mapping. **D28 is a shape rather than a rule.** The plan's risk table says nothing structurally prevents a second code path appearing, so two things prevent it. Evaluation is *never told the mode* — `RuleSet` holds no mode field and `RuleSet::evaluate` takes no mode argument, so a branch on it cannot be written without first changing a signature a reviewer sees. And every observation's "what would `ENFORCE` have done" is produced by calling **the same** `DlpMode::effect` with `Enforce`, so a simulation cannot report a would-be decision enforcement would not have taken; the two are one call. `D26` is structural in the same way: the engine gathers the snapshot **once, before the first stage**, and `DlpService::evaluate` receives a `&FactsSnapshot` — there is no provider on the trait to call twice. Gathering before stage 1 rather than just before DLP makes the fetch point independent of control flow, at the cost of a read on a request a later stage denies. The modes are a monotone ladder (`docs/06 §9.1`): `DISABLED` inspects nothing, `MONITOR` and `SIMULATION` record, `WARN` additionally applies obligations, `ENFORCE` additionally refuses. **`facts_unavailable` and the mode are different questions** and this is the one judgement call worth flagging: the tenant policy decides what an evaluation *concludes* without facts, the mode decides whether a conclusion is *acted on*, so a missing-facts denial is recorded under the three non-enforcing modes and refused under `ENFORCE`. A tenant that has not enabled blocking has not asked DLP to block, and a mode that still blocked during a rollout is a mode nobody can roll out (`§2`). Q17 is honoured by construction rather than by a branch — there is no code that could exempt a simulated evaluation from load it caused, and none that could charge bytes never stored, because `execute` is never reached. `crates/config`'s `DlpConfig` was wrong in three ways and is now right in all three: `facts_unavailable` spelled `FAIL_OPEN` where `docs/06 §12` says `FAIL_OPEN_AUDIT` (a separate `FactsUnavailablePolicy` type, because `FactsUnavailable` deliberately has no `Deserialize` — D27 — so configuration converts rather than shares); no `restricted_at`, so D27's mandatory escalation had no configured boundary; and `enabled` beside a two-value `default_mode`, two keys for one fact with no defined answer when they disagreed — collapsed onto `DISABLED` plus `is_enabled()`, which is the shape `AntivirusProvider::None` already set. Twelve deliberate breaks watched to fail by name. **One failed nothing** and is recorded rather than smoothed over: stamping every observation `MONITOR` left all eleven mode tests green, because `MONITOR` and `SIMULATION` differ in nothing but the label — and that label is exactly what `docs/06 §9`'s "refuses to enable enforcement on a policy never simulated" reads. An assertion was added and then watched to fail. Leakage rows `D1`–`D4`; `ENC-588` and `ENC-591` fixed here because both are the same defect class — an escalation asked about an input `require` was never given |
| ENC-583 | M4/D30 — a forwarding header is believed only from a configured network, hop by hop | P1 | DONE | `crates/conditional_access/src/origin.rs` walks the chain **right to left**, believing a hop only while the address it is stepping past is itself in a configured trusted network, with the peer's `hops` as the budget. Never leftmost, never first-public — both let a client claim any source IP by sending enough entries, and each now has a test named for it against a fixture where leftmost, first-public and the correct answer are three *different* addresses. `via_trusted_proxy` was made honest in the process: it now says whether the address was **relayed to us**, not whether our edge happens to sit behind a load balancer, so a trusted proxy that forwards nothing yields `false` and the address in hand is the one we observed. Wired at `crates/api/src/edge.rs` from axum's `ConnectInfo` — the first time `NetworkContext` has been built from a real connection rather than hard-coded to loopback — and zones are resolved against the **client** address, because the proxy is nearly always inside a trusted zone and computing them from it would make every request through the load balancer arrive trusted. Q19's separate rule set landed with it: `HumanCondition` and `MachineCondition` are different types, so a posture rule against a service account is not skipped, it cannot be written, and needs no escape clause. `system` is in the machine set and is *not* exempt, because a token can assert `typ: "system"`. Break-glass traverses this stage rather than skipping it (`docs/11 §5.6`): network-shaped rules are waived, MFA and posture are not, and the waiver itself requires MFA. Leakage matrix rows `C1`–`C7`; fourteen deliberate breaks watched to fail by name |
| ENC-584 | M4/D31 — a quota is enforced in the same statement as the write it bounds | P1 | DONE | `migrations/0018_storage_quotas.sql` + `crates/db/src/quota.rs` + `crates/scheduler`. Q18's answer built: **storage bytes, per tenant**, one row, the limit in the charging `UPDATE`'s `WHERE` clause and a zero-row result as the refusal — `migrations/0008`'s shape. **One table, not `docs/04 §16`'s `quotas` + `quota_usage` pair**, and PostgreSQL decides that rather than taste: a `CHECK` cannot reference another table, so with the limit and the counter apart there is no `CHECK (used <= limit)` to write and D31's backstop becomes a per-charge trigger or nothing. **The reconciliation window is closed by never writing an absolute figure.** Every arrangement of an absolute write has the window somewhere: measure-then-assign erases the charges that committed in between, lock-then-measure blocks every charge for the length of a full-table sum on the largest tenant, and compare-and-set livelocks on exactly the busy tenants worth correcting. So the counter and the truth are read in **one statement — therefore one snapshot**, consistent because the charge commits in the same transaction as the `file_versions` row it pays for — and the difference is applied *relatively*, `used_bytes = used_bytes + drift`, in a second instantaneous statement. Nothing is locked while the sum runs and concurrent charges keep their full effect. Two columns exist for reasons worth keeping: `enforcement` (`MONITOR`/`WARN`/`BLOCK`) because §2's sentence is about rollout, and `overshoot_bytes` because a tenant can be *legitimately* over — a downgraded plan, or drift — and a `CHECK` with no escape would make the nightly job fail on that tenant every night forever, leaving the figure nobody can see as the true one. It is an acknowledgement, never headroom: the charge bounds itself by `limit_bytes` alone. **Deletes cannot be refused by construction** — `Released` has no refusal variant — and the release statement carries no bound at all; an over-release saturates rather than aborting, because failing a *delete* on a quota-accounting error is the hostage situation reached by a different road. 13 database-backed tests + 12 unit + 5 scheduler, `docs/12 §4.11` rows Q1–Q6. Seven deliberate breaks watched to fail. Two findings: my own source-scanning tests were wrong on first run (`split_once(" WHERE ")` keeps the `RETURNING` list, which mentions `limit_bytes`), and a release guarded by `used_bytes <= limit_bytes` passes the exit-criterion test — under `BLOCK` no charge can take a tenant *past* its limit, only exactly *to* it, so the strictly-over case needed its own leg |
| ENC-585 | M4/D32 — the audit coverage sweep is a gate, not a review item | P1 | DONE | Two halves, because they fail differently and neither is sufficient. **`cargo run -p xtask -- audit-coverage`** enumerates every site in `crates/*/src` that *constructs* a refusal and fails on any that returns it to something other than `PolicyEngine::enforce`. An enforcement point is defined mechanically as a call site of `StageDecision::deny`, `Error::denied` or `Error::denied_with` — nothing else can refuse on policy grounds, because `Error::PolicyDenied`'s fields are private and `StageOutcome::Deny` is only reachable through `StageDecision::deny` — and *audited* is decidable from the enclosing function's **return type**: a refusal returned as a `StageDecision` reaches a caller only through the engine, which records it before returning `Err`. That argument holds only while the engine is the sole consumer of one, so `ensure_allowed()` — the public conversion into the caller's error — is enumerated as a third kind and must be acknowledged. **`crates/audit/tests/policy_audit_coverage.rs`** is the other half: the real chain into the real record format, once per `Stage::ORDER` entry, asserting the row says *that* it was refused, *why*, and *by which stage*. The split is not redundancy — deleting `record_deny` from the chain leaves the lint green, and dropping `policy_refs.push(stage)` from the sink leaves the lint **and** `enclave-core`'s 81 unit tests green, because those assert against a mock that receives a `Stage` rather than a record that carries one. Both verified by doing it. **The liveness check earned itself on the first run**: it failed, because `enforce` takes every denial inside a `macro_rules!` body that an AST walk never enters — the gate now scans macro bodies as tokens, which also closes the obvious evasion. 36 sites, 12 acknowledged, printed with their reasons on every run, and a **stale** acknowledgement fails the gate too; the staleness check is what found that `Self::denied` was unmatched and an entire crate's refusals were outside the inventory. The sweep's own finding is `ENC-606`. `docs/12 §4.10` U1 rewritten, U5/U6 added; `plans/M4-PROVENANCE-WALKTHROUGH.md` |

The chain allows with an obligation, `crates/api/src/download.rs::satisfy` cannot discharge it, and returns `Error::denied(PreviewOnly)` — `CLAUDE.md` rule 8 honoured exactly and rule 10 not honoured at all. The executed transcript is in `plans/M4-PROVENANCE-WALKTHROUGH.md §2`: `POST /files/{id}/download` returned **403 PREVIEW_ONLY** and `audit_events` held one row with `outcome=ALLOW`, `reason_code=NULL`, `policy_refs=NULL`, structurally identical to the row for a preview that *succeeded* with its watermark discharged. Fourteen sites — five in `download.rs::satisfy`, seven across `preview.rs`, one in `me.rs`, and `admin/conditional_access.rs::author`, which the gate caught the moment `ENC-603` merged and which is the one that matters most: *who tried to change the access rules* is the investigation this table exists for. **Closed by `ENC-625`**, and the note here was wrong in a useful way: it said the fix needed an audit sink on `ApiState` and that `crates/api/src/main.rs` was owned by another session. `ApiState::new` already holds the pool, so the sink is built there and `main.rs` needed no change at all — the blocker was never the wiring. All fourteen fixed; `docs/12 §4.10` U7; `plans/M4-PROVENANCE-WALKTHROUGH.md §2.5` |
| ENC-607 | `detail.obligations` is JSON inside a JSON string, so an auditor cannot query it with containment | P3 | TODO | Observed while reading real rows for `ENC-585`'s walkthrough. The stored value is `{"obligations":["{\"type\":\"NO_DOWNLOAD\"}"]}` — each obligation serialized to a string and the strings collected into an array — so `detail -> 'obligations' @> '[{"type": "NO_DOWNLOAD"}]'` does not match and the query has to compare strings inside the array. Correct, stable under hashing, and awkward. P3 rather than P2 because the encoding is inside the canonical bytes every stored `event_hash` was computed over, so changing it is a `canonical` versioning exercise rather than an edit, and the trigger for doing it is the admin audit-search endpoint rather than this observation |
| ENC-586 | M4's blocking questions answered: detectors, quota units, non-human access | P1 | DONE | Three of `plans/M4-GOVERNANCE.md`'s four open questions decided by the repo owner before implementation, which is the order that makes them cheap. **Q16 — structured detectors only, no regex on the synchronous path**: Luhn, IBAN, national ID and API-key shapes validated by structure and checksum, because a regex engine reading attacker-supplied content synchronously is a DoS surface with a long CVE history, and its failure mode is the bad one — a crafted document stalls every write and arrives as *load* rather than as a refusal, which is far harder to attribute mid-incident. Custom patterns are the first thing enterprise buyers ask for, so the pressure will come; the answer is a linear-time engine on an **asynchronous** path. **Q18 — storage bytes, per tenant**, matching what `docs/04` models and what a customer is billed for, with the limitation named rather than rediscovered: bytes do not bound the metadata and index load a million 1 KB files create. **Q19 — conditional access applies to every principal, with a separate rule set for service accounts and MCP tokens** (network allowlists and token binding, not device posture). The rejected option is the one that looks simpler: a single rule set makes every posture rule need an escape clause for non-human principals, which is the exemption again — written once per rule instead of once — and an exemption is exactly the gap an attacker looks for, since compromising a service token would make the zone rules simply not apply. Q17 (does simulation consume the same quotas as enforce) is still open and does not block the first two steps |
| ENC-587 | `docs/06 §12` asks a fail-closed denial to say *scanning is in progress*, and no reason code says that | P2 | TODO | Found building `ENC-581`. The closest `ReasonCode` is `DLP_BLOCKED`, whose default remediation is `REQUEST_EXCEPTION` — which tells a caller to ask a security administrator to have their file excused from being scanned, for a condition that clears by itself in seconds. `FactsSnapshot::require` therefore passes `RETRY_LATER` explicitly, using the door `default_remediation`'s own doc comment leaves open, and a test pins it so the pairing cannot quietly revert. A `SCAN_IN_PROGRESS` code is the right answer and was **not** taken inside this task: `ReasonCode` is deliberately not `#[non_exhaustive]` so that a new code forces every mapping to be revisited, which means the diff spans `crates/core`, `crates/api`, `docs/05-API.md §5` and the web i18n catalog — a larger blast radius than a shape-defining step should carry while two sessions are running in parallel. Not urgent: the current answer denies correctly and advises correctly, it merely names itself imprecisely in the audit row |
| ENC-588 | `facts_unavailable` cannot escalate for a share that is *already* external, because `require` never sees the resource | P2 | DONE | Found reviewing `ENC-581`. `D27` makes `FAIL_CLOSED` the default for **external sharing at any classification**, and `FactsPolicy::is_forced_closed` implements that as `Action::is_external_share()` — which matches `File(ShareExternal)` and `Share(CreateExternal)`, the two actions that *create* external exposure. `ShareAction::Update` is not among them, and cannot be: it means *change expiry, permission or password on an existing share*, and whether that share is external is a property of the **resource**, which `FactsSnapshot::require(action, rank)` is not given. So under `FAIL_OPEN_AUDIT`, removing the password from an existing external link to unscanned content is permitted, while creating that same link would have been denied. The narrow reading — that the content was already exposed, so nothing new is — does not hold: broadening permission or dropping a password increases exposure of a document nobody has scanned. **Not a defect in `ENC-581`'s shape**, which is why this is P2 rather than a fix demanded of it: the predicate is correct for what it is given, and the missing input is the resource. `ENC-582` threads the snapshot through `PolicyEngine::enforce`, where the resource is in scope, and that is the point at which the escalation can ask *is this share external* rather than *does this action create an external share*. The test to write there is the one that fails today: update-an-external-share under `FAIL_OPEN_AUDIT`, denied | **Fixed in `ENC-582`.** The missing input was the resource, so the resource's state now travels *on the snapshot* rather than as a `require` parameter: `ResourceState` carries the exposure and the label, gathered in the same breath as the facts (D26), so two stages cannot answer the question differently and a stage that forgot it cannot exist — a parameter defaulting to `Internal` would have been silently permissive exactly when omitted. `Action::alters_existing_share` is the companion predicate to `is_external_share`: one recognises the actions that *create* exposure, the other the action that can *broaden* one that exists, and neither is a control alone. `Revoke` is deliberately excluded — revoking reduces exposure, and a tenant that cannot revoke an external link over unscanned content is left holding the link, which is `D31`'s hostage situation reached by a different road. Two tests, and two independent breaks fail them: removing the escalation from `FactsPolicy::is_forced_closed`, and removing it from `ActionScope::ExternalSharing`'s scope predicate — so the pair proves both mechanisms rather than one standing in for the other |
| ENC-589 | Wire `charge_storage` into the upload and version-commit paths | P1 | DONE | `ENC-584` built the statement, the constraint, the reconciliation job and the tests, and nothing called it: `unwired_report.py` listed `charge_storage`, `release_storage` and `configure_storage_quota` among the public functions whose only callers are tests. `charge_storage` is now off that list. **The charge is at the version commit and nowhere else**, and that follows from what the counter *means* rather than from convenience: `ENC-584`'s reconciliation defines truth as `SUM(file_versions.size_bytes) WHERE status <> 'FAILED'`, so a charge raised against a staged object — at `POST /uploads`, or at upload completion — has no row in that sum and the first nightly pass would subtract it as drift. Enforcement would then depend on whether the job had run, which is not enforcement. `VersionService::commit`/`restore` therefore take `&mut TenantScoped` rather than `&mut PgConnection`, because `charge_storage` does: a signature that took a bare connection could not call it at all, which is where "the charge is in the same transaction" stops being a comment (`plans/M4-GOVERNANCE.md` D31). Order inside `write`: file bump (existence — a caller naming a missing file gets `404`, not the tenant's billing state), then charge, then the version insert. **Multipart and resumable are charged at commit too**, with the reserve-time check kept as what `docs/05-API.md §8` and `docs/10 §5` actually ask for — a bandwidth courtesy. `enclave_uploads::quota::Preflight` has **no admitting variant**, so "the quota admitted this upload" is not a constructible value and the reserve check cannot become half of the check-then-write D31 forbids; `tests/sessions.rs` asserts the consequence out loud, two sessions that each fit the same headroom are both issued. Accepted and stated rather than hidden: a tenant can open many sessions at once and is bounded at commit, not at reservation — a reservation is not representable in a table with one counter and no reservation column, and a fake one is a charge the nightly job erases. `Refused` renders through `enclave_db`'s own `impl From<Refused>`, so exhaustion is `403 QUOTA_EXCEEDED` with the limit and never a `500`.
