| ENC-590 | M4 — conditional-access rules get an operator surface: a table, a repository, and the wiring `ENC-583` stopped short of | P1 | DONE | `ENC-583` built the evaluator and `main.rs` still handed the engine `UnconfiguredConditionalAccess`, so every rule in the crate decided nothing in a running deployment — the state `plans/M4-GOVERNANCE.md §2` is entirely about. Rules are **tenant data** (`docs/06 §7` has an administrator writing them against their own tenant, while one `enclave.yaml` serves every tenant on a host), so they are rows: `migrations/0019_conditional_access_rules.sql`, a repository in `crates/db/src/conditional_access.rs`, `TenantConditionalAccess` in the domain crate, and `docs/04 §12.1`. Zone *definitions* stay in configuration, because a zone names the deployment's networks and is what the edge resolves an address against before any tenant is known. **The one design question was whether storage could express a rule the types cannot**, and the answer is that `audience` is a *column* rather than a field in the document: it selects which Rust type the conditions are decoded into, and it cannot be inferred, because `client_is` and `action_is` are legitimately in both vocabularies. Decoding is strict and closed, so a `MACHINE` row naming `posture_below` is refused **by name** rather than having the clause dropped — a rule missing a condition matches more requests than the administrator wrote, and every rule in this stage denies. For the same reason one undecodable row fails the **whole** set: a policy silently missing one refusal is a policy nobody wrote. `ALLOW` is refused twice, by the decoder and by a `CHECK`, and the second is the one that holds for a repair script. **Caching is a bounded staleness, not an invalidation protocol**: rules are read on every request in the chain, and a stale rule set is *permissive* — there is no `ALLOW` effect, so every rule here denies — so a 15-second per-tenant TTL bounds the window on **every** replica whether or not any message arrives, with `invalidate` as the shortcut for the one that made the change. A load failure or a decode failure is never an empty rule set; the request fails. `enclave_app` holds no `DELETE` (`0018`'s argument, stronger here: one statement lifts every network restriction a tenant has and leaves nothing to say it existed), so removal is `deleted_at` and the text stays. Two residues named rather than absorbed: `ENC-601` (the start-up banner's list is hardcoded, and `main.rs` filters it) and `ENC-603` (no HTTP surface writes a rule yet) |

The only caller is the DLP stage, which has no label of its own — so where the scan had not completed it passed `None`, and the escalation was asked about an unknown rank **in exactly the case it exists for**: an unscanned `RESTRICTED` document under `FAIL_OPEN_AUDIT` was permitted, because the evidence that it was `RESTRICTED` was expected to come from the scan that had not happened. A label is a property of the resource and does not wait for a scanner. It now travels on the snapshot inside `ResourceState`, gathered once with everything else, and `require` takes the action alone — which also removes the caller's ability to pass a rank of its own. The break — passing `None` in place of `self.resource.classification()` — fails three tests by name |
| ENC-592 | `QUARANTINE`, `REMOVE_SHARE` and `NOTIFY_SECURITY` refuse without performing their side effect | P2 | TODO | Found building `ENC-582`. `docs/06 §10`'s thirteen DLP actions are all modelled, because a closed enum forces an exhaustive `match` and a wildcard arm is how an unconsidered action becomes an unguarded one. Three of them cannot yet be *carried out*: quarantine should mark the version, remove-share should delete the link, notify-security should raise an incident — and the version state machine, `crates/sharing`'s revocation path and `crates/incidents` are each somebody else's milestone. Two gaps, neither inside that task's ownership. **The wiring**: `crates/api/src/main.rs` still installs `DisabledDlp`, and it was held by another session; `PolicyEngine::new` keeps its seven arguments and `with_facts` is a builder step precisely so that file compiles unchanged. **The reader**: `security_facts` has no migration, so there is no table for a provider to read and `stub::NoSecurityFacts` reports every resource unscanned under the fail-closed default — which denies every rule it cannot evaluate rather than waving it through, but denies it for the wrong reason. Note the direction the stub leans, because the alternative is the dangerous one: a provider answering "facts, all counts zero" would make every rule evaluate cleanly and permit, with nothing reporting an error. Needs `migrations/` and `crates/api/src/main.rs`, and the DLP configuration is already mapped end to end (`DlpConfig::facts_policy`, `From<enclave_config::DlpMode>`) so the wiring is a composition change rather than a design one **Both gaps closed.** `migrations/0020_security_facts.sql` creates the table and `docs/04 §12.2` records the three ways it differs from §12's model, each a decision rather than an omission. The one worth reading is the **absent** `detector_results JSONB`: nothing reads a per-detector breakdown — a synchronous decision compares counts, a severity, a score and a rank, which is the whole of `Condition` by Q16 — and what an opaque results document *is*, in practice, is the first place a future scanner writes the string it matched. Rule 10 is structural everywhere else on this path (`SecurityFacts` has no field a match value could occupy, `Candidate` redacts itself), and a column that would hold anything is where that property would be lost. An absent column cannot be filled in by accident. Also: `classification_rank` rather than `classification_id`, because `classifications` is created by no migration and a UUID pointing at nothing is a column no code can interpret; and `CHECK (… >= 0)` on the counts, which is what makes the `INT`→`u32` conversion total instead of reading a negative row as two billion card numbers. `enclave_app` holds no `DELETE` — a rescan replaces via `ON CONFLICT`, and `ON DELETE CASCADE` from `file_versions` is what removes facts about content that no longer exists. **The reader** is `enclave_db::security_facts` (statements) plus `enclave_dlp::PgSecurityFacts` (the provider), the same split `ENC-590` used: one transaction, so the facts and the resource's exposure are as of one instant (D26); freshness settled by `FactsSnapshot::gathered` as **equality** with the active detector set, which is passed in rather than inferred from the rows — a set inferred from the rows answers "were you produced by the detectors that produced you"; a read failure propagates rather than becoming "no facts", since under `FAIL_OPEN_AUDIT` that answer is *allow*. **Exposure is a walk, not a lookup**: a link on a containing folder or library exposes the file as much as one on the file, so `ENC-588`'s escalation would quietly stop firing if it asked only about the file — and everything but `INTERNAL` counts as external, because over-reporting exposure makes an unscanned share harder to change and under-reporting makes it easier. **The wiring**: `main.rs` builds `ModedDlp` for every mode that evaluates and `DisabledDlp` for `DISABLED`, which stays reachable as one of the five modes; the banner's DLP entry is now computed, because a stage that cannot refuse anything has two causes — a non-enforcing mode, or an empty rule set, which is every deployment today (`ENC-615`). **Facts are written by nothing**, deliberately: the writer belongs to the asynchronous scan pipeline beside antivirus (`ENC-613`), and the absence is safe in both directions because no row means *unscanned*, refused loudly under `FAIL_CLOSED` and permitted-with-evidence under `FAIL_OPEN_AUDIT` — both asserted rather than assumed. Six deliberate breaks watched; **two failed nothing** and are recorded: dropping the load statement's `tenant_id` predicate, alone and even with this table's RLS weakened to `USING (true)`, leaves the cross-tenant test green, because a fact row is reached through two scoped lookups over globally unique keys — it fails when both statements *and* `files`' policy are broken, and the single predicate is held by the repository's own unit test; and the exposure test's `RECURSIVE` assertion passed with the whole recursive term deleted, because the keyword survives in `WITH RECURSIVE` — now asserted on `UNION ALL` and `JOIN ancestry`, and watched to fail. Leakage rows `D1` and `D3` extended. One gap logged rather than absorbed: `ENC-614`, D27's `RESTRICTED` escalation cannot fire because no table resolves a label into a rank |

Not taken inside `ENC-590` because `state.rs` was held by another change in flight, and because it touches the `enterprise` profile's refusal to boot, which deserves its own diff. The failure it prevents is the quiet one: a banner that keeps saying a control is off is eventually not read at all |
| ENC-602 | The conditional-access rule cache has no configuration key, so its staleness bound cannot be tuned | P3 | TODO | `ENC-590` caches a tenant's rules for a fixed 15 seconds, chosen from the failure it bounds — an administrator who tightens a rule and checks whether it applied must find that it did — and logged at start-up so an operator can at least see it. It is a constant because `enclave_config::ConditionalAccessConfig` carries only `zones` and `crates/config/src/model.rs` was held by another change. A deployment with thousands of tenants may want it longer and one under active incident response may want it near zero, and both are legitimate. Note for whoever takes it: the key bounds a *security* window, so it wants a documented maximum rather than a free-form duration — a TTL somebody sets to an hour to save queries is the stale permissive rule set, reintroduced as a configuration value |
| ENC-603 | Conditional-access rules can be written by a repository call and by nothing else | P1 | DONE | Four routes under `/api/v1/admin/conditional-access/rules` (`docs/05-API.md §14.1`), and the thing that makes them the row rather than the handlers is that **an API is the second place `ENC-590`'s strictness is silently lost**: a body decoded into a lenient shape accepts the rule the decoder refuses and stores what was left of it. So the module holds **no condition vocabulary of its own** — `when` is carried as raw JSON, assembled into the stored `RuleRow`, and handed to `decode_rule`, the same function the loader runs on every request — and the three things the row asked for are each structural: the rule's **name** is in every response and in no error (`RuleError`'s `Display` embeds it, so refusals are built from the error's *structure*), a refused document reports **which clause** (serde's own `unknown variant \`posture_below\``, bounded and echoed in `details[].detail`, which `docs/05 §5` gains a sentence for), and every write calls `invalidate` — asserted against an **hour-long** TTL so it cannot pass by expiry. `ALLOW` is refused a third time and told why, since the two existing refusals say only that it is not an effect. **The lockout risk is a refusal, narrowly**: a rule may always be written and rehearsed and may not begin *deciding* if it would deny its author's own `admin/manage_policy` — the action that would undo it — so a rule about downloads is unaffected, the way out is to enforce it from a session the rule allows, and break-glass is deliberately not honoured, because the session that can always get in must not authorize a lockout for every session that cannot. Authorization is `Admin(ManagePolicy)` through the chain on the tenant, never the rule: a decision that varied with the rule would be an oracle for a rule the caller has not been shown. Leakage rows `C16`–`C20`. Thirteen deliberate breaks watched; **twelve failed by name and one did not** — storing the request's own document instead of the re-encoded rule leaves every test green, because `jsonb` normalises key order and `decode_rule` is what refuses, so the module header's claim was wrong and now says so. Five residues named rather than absorbed: `ENC-619` (no deployment authorizes an `Admin` action, so this surface is closed in the binary and fails closed), `ENC-620`, `ENC-621`, `ENC-622`, `ENC-623` (an undecodable row puts the tenant's own repair surface inside its blast radius — asserted, not papered over) and `ENC-624` (the one line `main.rs` needs) |
| ENC-613 | Nothing writes `security_facts`, so every version is unscanned forever | P1 | DONE | Found building `ENC-594`, and scoped out of it rather than absorbed (`§2.1` rule 5): the reader, the table and the wiring are that task's, and the **writer** belongs to the asynchronous scan pipeline — which is the same job that already runs antivirus over a new version (`file_versions.av_status`, `CLAUDE.md` rule 9), because both are content inspection of bytes that have just arrived and neither may hold up the upload. `docs/06 §12`: *full scanning is asynchronous; synchronous decisions consume precomputed facts*. The statement exists — `enclave_db::record_facts`, an upsert, so a rescan replaces rather than deleting and leaving a window in which live traffic is refused — and no binary calls it. **The absence is safe and is asserted to be**: with no rows every version is *unscanned*, which `FAIL_CLOSED` refuses loudly and `FAIL_OPEN_AUDIT` permits with a high-visibility event, and both legs are in `crates/dlp/tests/stored_facts.rs`. It is safe, not harmless: a `FAIL_CLOSED` tenant that writes a rule finds every governed action refused, which is exactly the control nobody dares enable, so this blocks any rollout past `MONITOR`. Two things for whoever takes it — the counts must come from `DetectorSet::scan`, whose output is already the right shape, and the row must be stamped with the set that produced it rather than with the set running when it is written, or a rescan backlog stamps old findings as current | **Landed as `crates/worker/src/scan.rs`, scheduled beside indexing.** "Beside antivirus" was checked rather than taken: there is no antivirus job — `enclave-antivirus` has no caller in any binary and `file_versions.av_status` is written once at upload and moved by nothing — so the asynchronous pipeline that exists is the **indexing pass**, which is already downstream of rule 9 through `readable_version`. The scan sits beside it, holds up no write path, and shares the *instance*: `main.rs` builds one `Arc<dyn Extractor>` and one `Arc<MountedOcr>` and lends both to both passes (`impl Extractor for Arc<E>` in `enclave-indexing`), because a media type registered for indexing and not for scanning is a document that is searchable and permanently unscanned. It scans `Prepared::chunks` in memory, never `chunk_text`: a `NO_INDEX` classification keeps content out of that table (`docs/07 §2.3`), so reading the stored copy would leave a tenant's most sensitive documents the only ones never scanned. **The decision is what an unscannable document gets: nothing.** No text — encrypted, corrupt, an unsupported type, a scanned page with no OCR — writes no row, because every `Condition` is a threshold and a row of zeroes makes each of them evaluate cleanly and *permit*, with a `scanned_at` and a current stamp standing behind it; an absent row is `D3`'s unscanned state, which both `facts_unavailable` policies already answer. A clean document with real text still gets its row of zeroes, which is what stops the rule collapsing into "write nothing". **The budget is `RenderBudget` and no second set of numbers**, argued term by term: extraction is bounded by `BoundedExtractor` from outside, and detection is bounded by its *input*, because `enclave_dlp::detector` states a cost bound rather than an estimate and the text is already capped by `max_output_bytes`. Deliberately no wall clock over the detector pass — it is synchronous CPU work on `spawn_blocking`, and `tokio::time::timeout` stops polling a future without stopping a thread inside it. **The rescan is bounded by a rate and nothing else**, stated rather than solved: a detector-set change invalidates every row at once (equality, `ENC-581`), so the backlog is the whole corpus and `SCAN_BATCH` × `Cadence::scan_idle` per tenant is all that paces it. With `migrations/` out of scope there is no claim column, so the queue is a `LEFT JOIN` and an unscannable version never leaves it — a per-tenant in-memory cursor stops that starving everything behind it, and re-attempting once per sweep is *wanted*: it is how a document becomes scanned the day PDFium or OCR is mounted. Nine deliberate breaks; **two failed nothing** and are recorded in the log. Leakage rows `D9` and `G11`. Logged rather than absorbed: `ENC-637`–`ENC-641` |
| ENC-614 | D27's `RESTRICTED` escalation cannot fire in a deployment, because no table resolves a label into a rank | P2 | DONE | Found wiring `ENC-594`. `FactsPolicy::is_forced_closed` compares the resource's *current* label against `dlp.restricted_at`, and `ENC-591` put that rank on the snapshot precisely so the escalation fires on a document **nobody has scanned**. `PgSecurityFacts` passes `None` for it, always: `files.classification_id` exists as a column, `docs/04 §12`'s `classifications` table is created by no migration, and nothing writes either — so there is no rank to read and no row to read it from. The external-sharing half of the same rule does fire, because exposure has a table (`share_links`), which is what makes this a gap rather than a dead control. **P2 rather than P1 on the reasoning that no label exists to be missed**: with nothing writing a classification, an escalation that read one would find the same `None`. It becomes P1 the moment `classifications` lands, and it is the row whoever lands it has to close in the same change — a labelling feature that ships without this leaves a `RESTRICTED` document permitted under `FAIL_OPEN_AUDIT` while its label sits in the database. Note for that change: the rank must be read in the same transaction as the facts, or two stages can answer it differently, which is the whole of D26 **Closed by `ENC-574`, in the same change, as this row required.** `classifications` exists (`migrations/0022`), `enclave_db::effective_classification` resolves a rank from it, and the rank is read **in the same transaction as the facts and the exposure**, which is this row's own closing condition and D26's. `crates/classification/tests/effective_classification.rs` proves the escalation fires — leakage row `D13`, with the labelling itself as the positive control, because "an unscanned `RESTRICTED` document is refused" asserts nothing until something can label one. Restoring `ResourceState::new(exposure, None)` fails all three of its legs. One correction to the note in this row: the change to `PgSecurityFacts::gather` is **not** one line. `resolve_content` returns nothing for a file whose first upload has not committed, and `gather` returns early on that — so a label resolved after the early return is `None` for a `RESTRICTED` document whose label is sitting in the database. The label belongs to the *resource*, not to its content, so it is read before the version lookup may bail out. `crates/dlp` is another session's, so the patch is `ENC-655` with the exact lines given |
| ENC-615 | DLP rules have no storage, so the wired stage evaluates an empty rule set | P1 | DONE | Found wiring `ENC-594`, and the exact shape of `ENC-603` one stage over. `main.rs` now builds `ModedDlp` in the configured mode with `RuleSet::empty()`, because a rule is **tenant data** — `docs/06 §8` has a security administrator writing detectors and thresholds against their own tenant, while one `enclave.yaml` serves every tenant on a host — and nothing stores one. The consequence is precise: `RuleSet::evaluate` returns `NotGoverned` for every action, so `ENFORCE` refuses exactly as much as `DISABLED` does. Everything *around* the rules works — the modes, the facts, the escalations, the observations — which is why this is the last thing standing between M4 and a DLP control an operator can turn on. The start-up banner says so rather than implying otherwise (`unenforcing_stages` announces an empty rule set whatever the mode), and that is a stopgap for a message, not for the missing table. Wants `dlp_policies` (`docs/04 §12`) with the same treatment `0019` gave conditional-access rules: the effect as a column so a `CHECK` can see it, strict decoding so a rule missing a condition is refused by name rather than matching more requests than the administrator wrote, and a bounded-staleness cache rather than an invalidation protocol. It also needs the `SIMULATION`-before-`ENFORCE` gate of `docs/06 §9`, which is a query over simulation history and therefore wants `ENC-593` first  **Rules are rows.** `migrations/0021_dlp_rules.sql`, `enclave_db::dlp` (statements, no opinion about what a rule means), `enclave_dlp::store` (the meaning, no statement) and `enclave_dlp::TenantDlp` (the stage `main.rs` now wires), on `ENC-590`'s split exactly. Almost all of the design work was **which of `docs/04 §12`'s columns not to create**, and one of them is the milestone's structural guarantee: there is **no `mode` column**. D28 makes `SIMULATION` and `ENFORCE` unable to diverge by having `RuleSet::evaluate` take no mode argument, and a per-rule mode has to be carried on the rule type to be read — it is precisely the field a second code path becomes writable through. So the mode stays in configuration and the cost is a row rather than a silence (`ENC-632`: a tenant cannot walk the ladder at its own pace). There is also **no `ALLOW`**, and that one was a finding rather than a copy of `0019`: `DlpAction::Allow` exists, `docs/06 §10` documents it as the way to write an exception above a broader rule, and the evaluator does not give it that meaning — its demand is `Nothing` and `Verdict::blocking_code` scans **past** a `Nothing` to the next refusal, so an `ALLOW` above a `BLOCK` fires and changes nothing. An administrator would write the exception, see it stored, watch it fire and still be refused, which is the exact failure `docs/06 §7.4` refuses one stage over — so the `CHECK` does not accept it and `ENC-631` owns giving exceptions a meaning. `priority` is the one §12 column kept, because it is the one that is *read*: rule order decides which reason code a refused caller sees, so the loader sorts `priority, name` and `name` is unique among live rules, making the order total. `enabled` and `scope_type`/`scope_id` are absent (a second off-switch beside withdrawal; no rule type has a resource scope). **Caching was re-derived rather than copied**, because DLP *has* an `ALLOW` action and conditional access has no `ALLOW` effect — which looks like the opposite direction and is not: with `ALLOW` unstorable, every storable action refuses, constrains or records, so a stale set is **permissive** and the bound is a short time (15 s, the same number, because two windows on two stages are two numbers an operator has to hold). The rollout step `SIMULATION`→`ENFORCE` does not go through the cache at all, being configuration. **A load or decode failure is never an empty rule set**: `rules_for` propagates and the request fails, and a failure is not cached. Ten deliberate breaks; nine failed by name. The two worth reading are that a swallowed decode error makes the chain answer `Allowed` where it must answer `Failed`, and that dropping `deny_unknown_fields` lets `{"category_at_least":{…,"pattern":"x"}}` decode **as a count condition with the pattern silently dropped**, which is Q16 lost without an error anywhere. **The tenth failed nothing at the level it was aimed at**, and it is `ENC-594`'s finding again: dropping the load statement's `tenant_id` predicate leaves the cross-tenant test green, because row-level security holds that property on its own (`docs/12 §4.1` T5 says so by design). It fails the repository's own unit test by name, and the cross-tenant test *does* fail — *"tenant-beta was decided against tenant-alpha's rule"* — once the policy is weakened to `USING (true)` as well, so it is capable of catching the leak it is named for. The banner's DLP entry stopped counting rules: a rule count is per-tenant now, and unavailable to this binary **by construction** — `enclave_app` is not `BYPASSRLS`, so a cross-tenant count has no tenant context to run in. Logged rather than absorbed: `ENC-631`, `ENC-632`, `ENC-633` (no admin surface — a rule's only route is `psql`, `ENC-603` one stage over) and `ENC-634` (`docs/12 §4`'s D1 row does not name the deployment-level proof; that file was outside this task's ownership) |
| ENC-637 | The content scan has no metrics, so a stalled or starved DLP backlog is invisible | P2 | TODO | Found building `ENC-613`. `ScanPass` counts every disposition and `scanning_loop` logs them at `debug`, which is exactly the state `ENC-521` describes for coverage before it had gauges: the numbers exist and reach nobody. The one that matters is not throughput but `ScanPass::unscannable` — under `FAIL_CLOSED` every version in it refuses a governed action and under `FAIL_OPEN_AUDIT` every one of them permits, so a corpus nobody can read is either an outage or a hole depending on a configuration value, and neither shows up anywhere today. A rescan makes it worse: moving the detector set puts every version back in the queue, and there is no series that would show the backlog draining or failing to. Wants the treatment `crates/worker/src/coverage.rs` got — gauges published from the pass, a rule in `deploy/monitoring/`, and the `unscannable` split kept per reason rather than summed, because `no_text_extracted` climbing means mount OCR and `not_readable` climbing means antivirus is behind |
| ENC-638 | A version that cannot be scanned is re-extracted once per sweep, forever | P2 | TODO | Found building `ENC-613`, and the direct consequence of the queue being a query rather than a claimed work list — `security_facts` has no `attempts` column and `migrations/` was outside that task. So an encrypted archive is parsed again on every sweep of its tenant. It is bounded (`SCAN_BATCH` per `Cadence::scan_idle`, with the cursor stopping it from starving anything) and it is partly *wanted*: a document unsupported today becomes scannable the day PDFium is mounted, with no backfill to run. What it is not is free, and the pathological case is a tenant whose corpus is mostly unscannable, where the scanner spends its whole budget re-parsing documents that will never yield text. The cheap fix is a bounded in-memory quarantine keyed by version with a decaying retry; the honest one is a column, which is a migration and a claim protocol and belongs with whoever next opens this table. **P2 rather than P1** because the cost is CPU on a background loop, never a wrong answer |
| ENC-639 | `risk_score` is always zero, so `Condition::RiskAtLeast` can never fire | P2 | TODO | Found building `ENC-613`, and the same shape as `ENC-614` one column over: the condition exists, the column exists, and nothing produces a value for it. `docs/06 §12` describes a *composite* risk signal, which aggregates things the scan pipeline does not have — proximity between a card number and an expiry date, the resource's label, the tenant's incident history — and a number synthesised from the counts would be the counts again under a name the rule reads as independent evidence, so an administrator writing `RiskAtLeast(50)` beside `CategoryAtLeast(FINANCIAL, 5)` would have written one condition twice. `facts_from` therefore records `RiskScore::ZERO` and a unit test pins it, so closing this row means changing a line somebody has to look at. The consequence is precise and one-directional: a rule written against risk never fires, which under-enforces rather than over-enforces. Wants Q16's proximity work first — the scanner has to be able to say *these two findings were near each other* before a composite of them means anything |
| ENC-640 | An identifier inside a chunk overlap is counted twice | P3 | TODO | Found building `ENC-613`. The scan runs the detectors over `Prepared::chunks`, and `ChunkBudget::overlap_chars` repeats ~360 characters of an *oversized* segment into the next chunk so a passage straddling a boundary stays retrievable — so an identifier landing in an overlap is offered twice and counted twice. Only oversized segments are affected; merged prose chunks do not overlap. It errs **upward**, which `CandidateClass` already argues is the safe direction for a number a threshold reads, and both honest alternatives are worse: scanning the whole document as one string doubles peak residency for a worker already holding up to `max_output_bytes`, and scanning segments instead of chunks would lose whatever OCR recovered, since `OcrRetry` returns chunks. `P3` because the error is bounded, in the safe direction, and only reachable on a document with a single segment above `max_chars` |
| ENC-619 | **Nothing in a deployment authorizes an administrative action**, so the admin surface is closed in the binary | P1 | DONE | `crates/authorization/src/admin.rs`, wrapped around the service `main.rs` already had. The design question was the one the row warned about — *the question is not only "is this user an administrator"* — and it is answered by making the decision a **set** rather than a predicate: `AdminGrants` is the `AdminAction`s a principal holds, a grant source produces it, and the service asks membership. Nothing in the module can ask the boolean question, so `docs/06 §22`'s separation of *changing conditional access* from *changing branding* survives contact with whatever grants a deployment gains later. What a deployment can grant **today** is one role, and the module says so out loud: `users.is_admin` is the tenant's *global administrator* (`docs/06 §22` makes its membership itself a privileged operation) and holds every action. That is not the collapse the row feared — the collapse would be answering `ManagePolicy` by asking about `WriteConfig`, or handing the policy right to a narrower role. There is no narrower role: `docs/04 §9` specifies `role_definitions` and its `§2` inventory names `role_assignments`, which has **no DDL** (`ENC-649` carries the migration this needs, written out rather than guessed at). Three refusals are structural rather than configured. An `Action::Admin` is decided here and **never delegated** — `acl_entries.action` is free text, so a tenant that could reach ACL resolution with an administrative action would be one `manage_permissions` grant away from a workspace owner writing themselves the tenant's policy right; asserted against an inner double that allows *everything*. Only a `User` can hold a grant, because `is_admin` is a column on `users` and a machine has no row there. And the reference must be this tenant's own tenant record, checked here because `authorize_many` is reachable without the engine. `status` and `deleted_at` are in the statement's `WHERE` clause, so a suspended administrator's outstanding token administers nothing. `AdminAuthorization` **wraps** rather than replaces, which keeps `GET /api/v1/me` working and leaves `ENC-126` one line to change; `authorize_many_actions` splits the batch so the inner service still answers its actions in one pass (`ENC-167`'s measurement is exactly the thing a wrapper loses silently). Leakage row `A13`. Six deliberate breaks watched; **five failed by name and one did not**: making `targets_own_tenant` return `true` fails only the unit tests, because every handler builds its resource from `ctx.tenant_id` and the two therefore cannot differ over HTTP — the check is defence for the `authorize_many` path the search post-filter uses, and the tests that cover it are the ones in the crate. Also recorded, and it is `ENC-594`'s finding for the third time: dropping the grant statement's `tenant_id` predicate fails nothing, because row-level security holds the property alone — the cross-tenant test *does* fail, naming the leak, once the policy is weakened to `USING (true)` as well, so the claim is not vacuous. Residues: `ENC-649`, `ENC-650`, `ENC-652` |
| ENC-641 | `enclave-antivirus` still has no caller in any binary, so `av_status` never moves | P1 | DONE | Found building `ENC-613`, which went looking for the antivirus job `ENC-594` describes and found there is none. `crates/antivirus` is complete — a clamd client, the EICAR round trip, archive limits, `ScanOutcome::av_status` — and nothing constructs a scanner: the crate is named only in its own tests and in `Cargo.toml`. `crates/versions` writes every new version `SCANNING`/`PENDING` and nothing moves it, so in a running deployment **no version ever becomes `AVAILABLE`/`CLEAN`**, `readable_version` answers `None` for everything, and both content passes — indexing and the new scan — correctly do nothing at all. Rule 9 is not violated; it is satisfied by the whole corpus being unreadable. This is why `ENC-613`'s tests write their fixtures' `av_status` directly, and it is the row that has to close before either content pass does anything in an environment nobody seeded by hand. **P1**: `ROADMAP.md`'s M1 exit criteria assume an upload becomes readable, and nothing in `crates/worker` can make that happen **Closed by `crates/worker/src/antivirus.rs`, scheduled first among the passes.** The pass is a translator, never a second policy: `enclave_antivirus::decide` still owns `docs/06 §6.2` and `Target::of` maps its answer onto two columns plus `files.status`, so the table a reader checks against the document is one function. **`Publish` turned out not to mean readable** — `ALLOW_AND_RESCAN` publishes `AVAILABLE`/`PENDING` and `ALLOW_WITH_FLAG` `AVAILABLE`/`SKIPPED`, and `READABLE_PREDICATE` accepts neither, so both policies buy no availability at all. It errs closed and was left closed: admitting either to the predicate weakens rule 9, which is a design conversation (`ENC-646`). **The queue is `av_status` and never `status`**: `PENDING` covers a fresh upload, a `HOLD` retry *and* an `ALLOW_AND_RESCAN` version that is already `AVAILABLE`, which a status-based predicate would never find again. `SKIPPED` is offered only to an engine that reports `scans_content`, which is `§6.2`'s "rescan everything currently flagged `Unsupported`" specialised to the one signature change this workspace can observe — and it is what makes `provider: none` loud rather than terminal. **`AntivirusProvider::None` publishes nothing**, deliberately: `NoScanningPerformed` answers `Unsupported`, `ScanPolicy::from_config` pins `unsupported` to `BLOCK`, and there is no configuration key that could say otherwise — the alternative, not scheduling the pass, would have made "no antivirus" and "antivirus broken" the same observation and put the decision in `main.rs` instead of in `decide`. **Scanner unavailable**: `HOLD` writes *nothing* for a fresh version and nothing at all for one being rescanned, `ERROR` is recorded for the non-retryable leg so it stops consuming a queue slot, and a missing object is `ERROR` too — otherwise one absent blob starves every version ordered behind it forever. The stuck case is **reported**: `AvPass::held` per tick and `AvPass::oldest_due` as a backlog age, both `warn!`ed by `schedule::report`. **Ordering**: the queue never offers a `CLEAN` version, so nothing this pass writes can move a version out of `CLEAN` and the indexing/DLP race is closed by construction rather than by timing. Nine deliberate breaks. **Two failed nothing**: dropping the queue's `tenant_id` predicate (RLS holds it — `ENC-594` and `ENC-613`'s finding for the third time, and the predicate is held by this module's own unit test), and deleting the verdict write's compare-and-swap, which no behavioural test exercises because nothing here runs two writers at once (`ENC-150`'s shape; recorded rather than papered over). A third was worse than nothing and was fixed: the superseded-version test passed with the `files` guard deleted, because the queue is ordered oldest-first and the current version's `AVAILABLE` simply overwrote the other's `QUARANTINED` — re-fixtured and watched to fail. Leakage rows `G1` and `G6` extended and **`G12`** added, which is the section's missing positive control. Logged rather than absorbed: `ENC-643`–`ENC-648` |
| ENC-643 | `enclave_indexing::enqueue` has no caller either, so nothing ever enters the index queue | P1 | DONE | Found building `ENC-641`, and the same shape one crate over: `enqueue` writes the `index_manifests` row `claim` later picks up, and it is named only by `crates/indexing`'s own tests and `crates/worker/tests/indexing.rs`. No binary calls it. So with `ENC-641` closed a version now becomes `AVAILABLE`/`CLEAN` and readable — and the indexing pass still finds an empty queue, because a manifest is what it claims and nobody creates one. The DLP scan is unaffected: its queue is a *query* over `file_versions` (`ENC-613`), so it starts working the moment antivirus does; indexing does not. **The row belongs at commit, not here**, and that is the design rather than a preference: `enclave_indexing::defer` exists precisely so a manifest can be enqueued before antivirus has finished and returned to the queue until the version is readable, which is a mechanism with no purpose if enqueueing waits for the scan. So it wants `crates/api`'s commit handler, inside `VersionService::commit`'s transaction — a file whose manifest was enqueued in a separate transaction is a file that is either indexed twice or not at all. Deliberately not absorbed into `ENC-641`: `crates/api` is another session's, and the antivirus pass enqueueing would have quietly moved the trigger to the wrong place | **Wired inside `VersionService::commit` rather than in a handler**, and the row's own suggestion of `crates/api`'s commit handler turned out not to exist — `VersionService::commit` has no HTTP caller either, only tests. Inside the function is the better place regardless: a second handler cannot forget it, and the manifest shares the transaction that writes the version, so a queue entry for a version that does not exist is unrepresentable. Enqueued **before** antivirus runs, per this row's own reasoning about `defer`. A failure is a new `VersionsError::Indexing` that **fails the commit** — distinct from `Database` so an indexing-queue outage does not read as a storage outage on the file, and failing rather than warning because a version that exists and is not queued is exactly the unsearchable state this row describes, and this transaction is the last place it can be refused cheaply.
| ENC-644 | Nothing sets `files.status` back to `AVAILABLE` for a file whose only version fails | P3 | TODO | Found building `ENC-641`. `VersionService::commit` leaves the *file* `PROCESSING` on every commit and the antivirus pass moves it — to `AVAILABLE` on a clean verdict, `QUARANTINED` on a refusal — but only ever for the version the file is currently pointing at, and only for a terminal verdict. A version that sits in `SCANNING` because the engine is down leaves its file `PROCESSING` for as long as the outage lasts, which is correct (it points at content nobody may read) and is a state no listing test covers: `crates/testing`'s `Spine` writes files `AVAILABLE`, so every ACL fixture in the workspace bypasses this column. **P3 because it is the safe direction** — a `PROCESSING` file is hidden, not exposed — but a tenant whose scanner flaps sees documents disappear from listings with no explanation, and there is no test that would notice |
| ENC-645 | A malware detection raises no incident record, only a log line | P2 | TODO | Found building `ENC-641`. `docs/06 §6.2` requires `Infected` to raise a `CRITICAL` incident and notify security, and `enclave_antivirus::Incident` models one — severity, kind, the engine's signature name, `notify_security` — with `#[must_use]` so it cannot be dropped silently. The pass receives it and has nowhere to put it: there is **no incidents table**, no `Action` variant for antivirus in `enclave_core::action` and no `EventType` for a completed scan in `enclave_events`, so an `AuditEvent` would have to name an action that does not describe it. What exists is `tracing::error!` with the tenant, file, version, severity, kind and signature — greppable, unchained, unexportable, and gone when the log rotates. **The signature name is the specific loss**: `file_versions` records the engine and the signature *generation* but has no column for what matched, so an investigation cannot ask "which versions did this signature catch". Needs a migration and probably a table; see `ENC-641`'s report for the two options |
| ENC-646 | `ALLOW_AND_RESCAN` and `ALLOW_WITH_FLAG` publish versions no read path will serve | P2 | TODO | Found building `ENC-641`, by writing the transition table out. `VersionDisposition::Publish` is documented as "the version may proceed towards `AVAILABLE`" and `ScanOutcome::readable()` answers `true` for it — but the pair actually written is `AVAILABLE`/`PENDING` for `ALLOW_AND_RESCAN` and `AVAILABLE`/`SKIPPED` for `ALLOW_WITH_FLAG`, and `READABLE_PREDICATE` is `status = 'AVAILABLE' AND av_status = 'CLEAN'`. So both policies produce a version that is published and unreadable: `docs/06 §6.2` describes `ALLOW_AND_RESCAN` as trading a malware window for availability, and it buys no availability at all. **It errs closed, which is why this is P2 and why it was not fixed in place**: admitting `PENDING` or `SKIPPED` to the predicate is a weakening of `CLAUDE.md` rule 9 and belongs in a design conversation, not in a task whose job was to make the transition happen. Pinned by `a_published_but_unscanned_version_is_still_not_served` so the disagreement cannot be closed by accident in the permissive direction. `ALLOW_WITH_FLAG` is unreachable today in any case (`ScanPolicy::from_config`), so the live half is `ALLOW_AND_RESCAN` |

| ENC-648 | The antivirus pass has no metrics, so a stalled scanner is a log line or nothing | P2 | TODO | Found building `ENC-641`, and `ENC-637`'s row one pass over — the two should close together. `AvPass` counts every disposition and `schedule::report` warns on `held` and on the backlog age of the oldest version still waiting, which is enough to grep for and not enough to alert on. The series that matters is not throughput: it is **`held` not falling** and **the backlog age climbing**, because every version in either is content the tenant uploaded and cannot read, and neither shows up anywhere a dashboard can see. This is the shape `ENC-641` itself had — a control that was correct, silent, and wrong for four milestones. Wants the treatment `crates/worker/src/coverage.rs` got: gauges published from the pass, a rule in `deploy/monitoring/`, and the dispositions kept separate rather than summed — `quarantined` climbing is malware or a misconfigured provider, `errored` climbing is missing objects, and a single "not cleared" number cannot tell an operator which |
| ENC-655 | `PgSecurityFacts` still passes `None` for the resource's rank, so D27's escalation is dead in `crates/api`'s binary | P1 | DONE | Found landing `ENC-574`, which built everything the escalation needs and does not own the call site — `crates/dlp` was held by another session. Add `use enclave_core::ResourceKind;` and `use enclave_db::effective_classification;`, then **before** the `let Some((file, version)) = resolved else` block: `let labelled = match resource.kind { ResourceKind::File \| ResourceKind::Folder => Some(FileId::from_uuid(resource.id)), _ => resolved.map(\|(file, _)\| file) };` `let classification = match labelled { Some(file) => effective_classification(&mut tx, file).await.map_err(Error::from)?, None => None };` `let rank = classification.map(\|c\| c.rank());` — then pass `rank` in place of both `None`s in the two `ResourceState::new` calls. The read is inside the existing transaction, which is D26 and `ENC-614`'s stated requirement. `crates/classification/tests/effective_classification.rs` carries the same provider verbatim, so the patch is already under test; move those three legs to `crates/dlp/tests` when it lands |

What is *not* a straight swap is the unlabelled case: `effective_rank` returns `Result<ClassificationRank>` with no third outcome, and this row is the reason `ENC-574` built `ClassificationResolution::require_for_indexing` — the return type wants to become `ClassificationOutcome`, so `Assumed` can be honoured *and* audited rather than collapsing into the rank. `UnclassifiedFiles` stays, for the deployment whose classification service is unreachable, and becomes the `FAIL_CLOSED` case rather than the only case. `crates/worker` was another session's during `ENC-574` | **Closed by `PgClassification`**, which resolves through `enclave_db::effective_classification_on` — the same walk the policy chain and `crates/dlp`'s provider read, so there is one definition of a file's label rather than a second that drifts. `effective_rank` now returns `ClassificationOutcome` rather than `ClassificationRank`: a rank cannot express *nothing is labelled and the tenant said what that means*, so returning one collapses an assumption into a reading, and the call site matches three arms rather than unwrapping. `UnclassifiedFiles` stays — it is the deployment whose classification service is unreachable, an outage rather than a policy, and a different fact from `FAIL_CLOSED`. **Scope, because the row's own title over-claims:** this closes the classification source and *not* "no deployment embeds anything", which remains true for two further reasons — `ENC-661`.

The shape is settled by `DlpConfig`'s `facts_unavailable` (`ENC-582`): a separate `UnlabelledPolicy` type in `crates/config` that *converts*, never shares, because `Unlabelled` deliberately has no `Deserialize` (D27) — and the conversion is where the configured rank is validated against the tenant's own label set, since a rank naming no label is a policy nobody can read |
| ENC-661 | Nothing embeds, and the classification source was only the first of three reasons | P1 | DONE | Found closing `ENC-656`, whose title said *"so no deployment embeds anything"* — true, but not because of `UnclassifiedFiles`. Three things stand between the workspace and a document reaching Milvus, and fixing the first changed nothing observable: **(1)** the classification source, now `PgClassification` (`ENC-656`); **(2)** `PipelineRunner`, the indexing pass the worker binary actually constructs, holds **no `VectorStage`** — `main.rs` passes `""` as the model name with a comment saying that is the honest value because *nothing embeds yet*; **(3)** there is **no `EmbeddingProvider` implementation in the workspace at all**. `EmbeddingRouter` is the only `impl Embedder`, and the only providers are `NoLocalModel` and `NoRemoteProvider`, both of which refuse by construction. `ENC-508` marked the *port* done and `ENC-534` chose `bge-m3` at 1024 dims mounted, but nothing loads the weights, and `crates/config` has no embeddings section to point at them. So the honest order is: an `EmbeddingProvider<Local>` that loads the mounted model, then a `VectorStage` in the worker binary, then this row closes. Worth stating plainly because M3 was declared complete with dense retrieval explicitly not claimed — this is the row that makes that caveat concrete rather than a sentence in a plan | **Closed: a committed document now reaches Milvus, and there is a test that watches it do so.** The runtime is `rten` + `tokenizers`, which is Q12's criteria applied a second time rather than re-argued — pure Rust, permissive, and **no `links` key**, the property that disqualified `tesseract`/`leptess` for OCR and disqualifies `ort` here. **The conversion worked cleanly and was the first thing done, because it was the risk the row named:** BAAI's published ONNX export (opset 11, 28 operator types) converts to `.rten` with `rten-convert 0.22.0` — the version `rten`'s own `v0.24.0` tag ships — with no unsupported operator and no warning, and the forward pass produces 1024-dimensional vectors. Nothing was stubbed and no runtime was substituted. `docs/08 §18.1` is the reproducible procedure. **Three pieces:** `MountedModel` (`EmbeddingProvider<Local>` over the mounted weights, one forward pass per chunk on `spawn_blocking`, CLS-pooled and L2-normalised because the dense index is `COSINE`); a top-level `embedding_model` config key with `OcrMounts`' tri-state; and `main.rs` building a real `VectorStage` with `PgClassification` as the rank source. `NoLocalModel` stays and keeps refusing — it is what a deployment has before it mounts anything and what one has when the volume fails to attach. **`ENC-533` is now a comparison of two independently-sourced facts:** `MilvusIndex::dense_width` reads the collection's width back from the *server*, because `MilvusConfig::dimension` is what this process intended three lines earlier, and `ensure_collection` gains its first non-test caller. **Four findings worth more than the feature.** (1) A startup validator refusing `embedding_model` with no `search.milvus` was the right rule in the wrong process: `ConfigLoader` reads the whole environment, so a shell exporting `ENCLAVE_EMBEDDING_MODEL` made *every* binary refuse to start, `enclave-api` included — `ENC-544`'s failure through a validator instead of a variable name, caught by `tests/ambient_environment.rs`. The refusal moved to the worker, which is the only process that builds a stage. (2) The order test embedded `[A,B]` then `[B,A]` and asserted the vectors swapped; it **passed with `vectors.reverse()` inserted**, because a reversal applied to both batches is symmetric. It is anchored now — each chunk embedded alone — and three chunks, because a two-element reversal is also a swap. (3) The `dense_width` test asked through the same handle that created the collection and **passed with the implementation it forbids**, since `ensure_collection` creates *at* `config.dimension` and the two numbers are equal by construction; it interrogates through a second, differently-configured handle now. (4) The mount-time width check has **no test and cannot have one** with the only model we mount — deleting it fails nothing — so that is recorded in the source beside the two later layers that are covered. Nine deliberate breaks were watched to fail by name; two failed nothing and are the second and third findings above. Follow-ups logged rather than absorbed: `ENC-662`–`ENC-664`. |
| ENC-665 | `tokenizers` brings in `paste`, which is unmaintained (`RUSTSEC-2024-0436`) | P2 | DONE | Found when `ENC-661`'s CI run failed both `cargo audit` and `cargo deny`. Not a vulnerability — the advisory records only that the author archived the crate and names no defect. Accepted in **both** files, per the established pattern, and the argument is stronger than `ttf-parser`'s: **`paste` is a proc-macro, so it is not in the binary at run time at all.** It expands identifiers during compilation and contributes no code; by the time this product runs a document through a tokenizer, `paste` has been gone since the build. There is no attacker input to reach it, and a soundness bug in an identifier-pasting macro surfaces as a compile error rather than a run-time defect. No upgrade path exists: `tokenizers 0.23.1` is the latest release. Writing our own XLM-RoBERTa SentencePiece tokenizer would be a far larger correctness risk than an archived build-time macro, and `docs/12 §1.1` is explicit that we integrate rather than rebuild. Revisit 2026-11-25 |
| ENC-666 | `deny.toml`'s `RUSTSEC-2026-0002` exception is stale — the crate it names is gone | P3 | TODO | Found by `cargo deny` itself while fixing `ENC-665`: *advisory was not encountered … no crate matched advisory criteria*. `lru` is no longer in the dependency graph at all (`cargo tree -i lru` returns nothing), so the `milvus-sdk-rust → lru 0.12.5` exception written for `ENC-526` now guards nothing. A dependency bump removed it and the exception outlived its subject. Deliberately **not** removed in `ENC-661`'s pull request: a security-configuration change riding along in a feature branch is the shape nobody reviews, and `CLAUDE.md` asks for one logical change per PR. Worth noting the general case rather than just this instance — an ignore list is a place where entries accumulate and never leave, and `cargo deny` warning about its own unused entries is the only thing that reports it. Both `.cargo/audit.toml` and `deny.toml` need the same removal |
| ENC-671 | M5 plan: the MVP GA milestone, with its decisions locked | P1 | DONE | `plans/M5-MVP-GA.md`. D33-D38 locked before a component exists, and the reason this plan is longer than its predecessors is that reading the client design against `docs/09` surfaced **seven genuine contradictions** rather than stylistic differences — each settled in writing here. Two were decided by the repo owner: **D33**, surfaces with no backend render disabled rather than being omitted, and **D34**, the details panel is the design's five-tab peek and `docs/09 §7`'s eleven-tab docked panel is superseded. The milestone's sentence is *a screen is a promise, and a promise the backend cannot keep is a defect that ships looking finished* — M0-M4's recurring failure moved to where a customer can see it. Q20-Q23 are open and each blocks a specific route |
| ENC-672 | M5/D38 — grouped virtualization at 100k rows, sequenced first | P1 | DONE | **It holds, with room.** Measured in Chromium at 1440×900 over the grouped default view, not a flat one: first paint **129 ms** against `docs/09 §2`'s 400 ms; scroll **p95 3.2 ms / p99 4.1 ms / max 6.7 ms** against a 16.7 ms frame budget with **0 of 899 frames over**; 46 rows in the DOM across a 3.6 M-pixel scroll height; a collapse committing in 8.8 ms. Grouping stays on by default and nothing in the design has to change. The answer to all three of D38's named difficulties is one decision: **the flat index space is never materialized.** 100 000 rows are described by a few hundred group records, so building a layout is O(groups) and locating a window is O(log G) — which is what puts first paint out of reach of the row count. A collapse captures an anchor (*which item is at the top, and by how much*), rebuilds, and restores it in the same commit via `useLayoutEffect`, so the index space moving underneath the scroll position is a re-derivation rather than a jump. The pinned group header is one element **outside** the window rather than a `position:sticky` row inside it, and the window never renders the header it is showing — so sticky and windowing stop fighting, and no duplicate row reaches the accessibility tree. The window's bounds snap to a multiple of the overscan, **both ends at the same phase**, so the rendered set changes once per block instead of once per frame. That last one came from the benchmark catching the code comment lying: the first version asserted the window re-rendered only when it had to, measured **898 commits across 899 frames**, and was wrong. Also delivered here because the list could not exist without it: the whole web toolchain (Vite, TypeScript strict, Vitest, Playwright), the i18n catalog and `Intl` wrappers, the four states `docs/09 §11` requires and the reference shows none of, and 15 axe runs green across seven surfaces in both themes |
| ENC-673 | M5/D33 — a not-yet-built control is never rendered in the denial treatment | P1 | TODO | The implementation cost of the owner's D33 choice, and a **security** row rather than a product one. `docs/09 §12` and the design's denied-explained-inline pattern both use *dimmed control plus a reason* to mean **policy refused this** — that is how a user learns DLP, a barrier or a conditional-access rule stopped them. Rendering *not yet built* the same way teaches users that dimmed is background noise, on surfaces where it is harmless, and they carry the habit to the one place it is not. So the two are different treatments: unbuilt controls are **not focusable**, carry a neutral `Later` chip with no remedy, never use the denial colour, and speak in future tense about the product rather than present tense about the user. A `docs/12 §4` row asserts the two never share a class — the only thing standing between D33 and the erosion it invites |
| ENC-674 | M5/D36 — `capabilities` carries a reason per denied action | P1 | TODO | `crates/api/src/content.rs` builds `capabilities` from nine named booleans plus `obligations`, with **no reason attached to a `false`**. The design hovers a disabled action and explains why, which today could only be invented client-side — and `CLAUDE.md`'s React conventions forbid re-deriving permissions in the client for exactly that reason. A denied capability gains a stable `ReasonCode` and the user-safe sentence `docs/06 §24` specifies, and **never the rule that matched** (rule 10). `aria-disabled` + `title` is additionally not a reliable screen-reader path (`docs/09 §15`), so the reason is associated with `aria-describedby` and rendered as text rather than as a tooltip alone |
| ENC-675 | M5/D37 — search is lexical, and the degraded-search header is rendered | P1 | TODO | `ENC-661` is open, so nothing is embedded and dense retrieval returns nothing. `docs/07 §6` specifies hybrid retrieval and `docs/09 §10` specifies a degraded-search header that has never been designed because it has never been reachable. M5 ships lexical search and **renders that header**. The alternative is a search box that silently returns fewer results than the product promises, which is the reads-as-working failure with a customer on the other end |
| ENC-676 | M5/D35 — resolve the seven doc/design contradictions in `docs/09` | P1 | TODO | Each settled in `plans/M5-MVP-GA.md §3` and each needing an edit to the authoritative document, in the same PR as the code that diverges: row density 48/36 → 36/30 (§13, design wins — density is appearance); the peek panel (§7, D34); four keyboard bindings plus three additions (§6, **`docs/09` wins** — the keyboard model is behaviour, an accessibility commitment, and the one surface a screen-reader user cannot work around); the **stale shell diagram still drawing a top bar the design retired** (§3 — a reader implements what is drawn); shown-vs-hidden denied actions (§5, design wins, since a hidden action is indistinguishable from one that does not exist — `docs/06 §24`'s own argument); rule 12 violations **in the reference itself** (manual `₹ 4.8 Cr`, hand-built relative times, physical CSS throughout — defects to fix, not licences to copy, and `en-XB` in CI fails on them); and **no empty state or fetch-error state anywhere in the reference**, on any of five layouts, against `docs/09 §11`'s four-states rule | **Two copies of this row disagreed** — one `DONE`, one `TODO`, identical otherwise — so it is recorded open until someone confirms against `docs/09`. Closing it on the strength of a duplicate would be the tracker asserting a completion nobody verified (`ENC-687a`).
| ENC-677 | The web structural gate reports `pass` while enforcing nothing, and names the wrong milestone | P1 | DONE | Closed with `ENC-672`. **The pending branch is deleted, not updated** — a branch that cannot fire is one nobody notices rotting, and the fix for *the gate had an escape hatch* is not a better escape hatch. `web/package.json` now provides `lint:i18n`, `check:bundle-size` and `test:a11y`, and the job runs them unconditionally; if `web/` were ever absent, `npm ci` fails and the job goes red, which is the correct answer because the frontend is not optional after M5. Two steps added beyond the three it always claimed: `typecheck`, because `CLAUDE.md`'s TypeScript conventions are a build-breaking contract and only `tsc` can hold them, and a real Chromium install, because half of `docs/09 §15` — 4.5:1 text contrast, 3:1 for UI components, visible focus at 3:1 — depends on computed style and layout and jsdom has neither; a jsdom axe run would pass while enforcing only the half that needs no renderer, which is this job's own failure mode wearing a different hat. **All three scripts refuse to pass vacuously**, which is the part that stops this recurring: `lint:i18n` exits 2 if it scans zero files or finds zero catalog keys, `check:bundle-size` exits 2 if the manifest names no entry chunk, and `test:a11y` asserts its route list is non-empty before walking it. The gate was then broken on purpose seven ways and watched to fail each time |
| ENC-678 | `tokens.css` is extracted by hand, so a token missing from the extract reads as a token that does not exist | P2 | TODO | Found closing M5's Q22-adjacent dark-mode question. `ENC-661`'s brand session reported the favicon at 1.92:1 on a dark tab strip and raised a dark-mode `--accent` as an **open decision**, correctly noting that `web/src/styles/tokens.css` defines none. The reference it was extracted from — `web/design-system/design-system-v2.html` — carries a full dark ladder for all three brands (`harbor` `#4F46E5`→`#8B85FF`, `northwind` `#0F766E`→`#2DD4BF`, `meridian` `#111827`→`#E5E7EB`). The session was reasoning from the extract and reached a wrong conclusion honestly; anyone reading only the extract will reach the same one. `web/design-system/README.md` already says *change the token in the HTML first, then re-extract* — this is the first evidence of what a lagging extract costs, and the answer is that extraction should be a **checked** step (a script, and a gate that fails when the two disagree) rather than a remembered one. The immediate defect is fixed: the favicon switches on `prefers-color-scheme` and reaches 3.96:1 |
| ENC-679 | M5 frontend LLD: module boundaries, state ownership, component contracts | P1 | DONE | `docs/17-FRONTEND-LLD.md`, written before the shell's architecture set. Authoritative for how the frontend is *built*; `docs/09` stays authoritative for how it looks and behaves. Built on one sentence — **the server decides, the client renders the decision** — from which the rest follows: no global store and **no normalized cache**, because `capabilities` is not a property of a file but of *this user, this action, this moment* (`docs/05 §7`), and normalizing it is precisely how a stale permission renders as an enabled button. Four state kinds with one home each (server → TanStack Query, URL → the route, local UI → Zustand, ephemeral → `useState`); Zod at the fetch boundary with types **inferred** rather than declared beside it; features that may not import each other, enforced by an ESLint boundary rule rather than by convention. Two contracts are security ones rather than styling: **the three ways a control can be non-actionable** — denied, unbuilt, busy — must never look alike (`ENC-673`), and **a denial is not a failure** and never renders a retry affordance, because retrying a `403` teaches a user the product is broken rather than that they lack permission. Q24 (does the Activity tab read a feed that does not exist?) and Q25 (which mutations may be optimistic) left open |

The discipline point matters more than the inventory: none of this is M5. A signing pipeline existing does not make signing an MVP feature — it makes `ENC-314` (M9) an **integration** rather than a build, and `ENC-312` external-editor brokering (M8) the same for opencalc. | ENC-681 | `@schnsrw/design-system` is rejected — do not use it, do not vendor from it | P2 | DONE | **Rejected by the repo owner, 2026-08-25, on quality.** Not a candidate for any component, not a dependency, and not a source to copy from. The technical blocker found while evaluating it stands as a separate record, because it is the kind of thing that recurs: its token entry point `@import`s two Google Fonts stylesheets, which is disqualifying three times over — `docs/08 §18` makes air-gapped installs first-class and the request fails there, `ENC-135` already self-hosts Inter, Inter Tight and JetBrains Mono as local `.woff2` precisely to avoid a CDN, and an enterprise security product reaching Google on every page load is a compliance conversation. **Any** future package proposed for `web/` gets that check before anything else. | ENC-682 | **`crates/api` registers ten routes; you cannot sign in, upload, search or share** | P1 | DONE | Found by the M5 spec workflow reading the prototype against the tree, and it is `ENC-543`'s pattern at the HTTP layer — built correctly, reachable by nothing — but larger than any previous instance. The router registers exactly ten paths: `me`, four `files/{id}` reads, `libraries/{id}/items`, and the two admin surfaces. **There is no `/api/v1/auth/*` at all** — no login, no MFA verify, no refresh, no logout, no sessions — although `crates/auth` is complete (password, refresh, revocation, JWKS) and `crates/api` already *depends* on it. `crates/uploads` (217 lines), `crates/sharing` (token minting, redemption, the concurrency-safe budget from `ENC-150`) and `crates/search` (post-filter, denylist, lexical, degraded, excerpt, Milvus) are all substantial, all tested, and `crates/api` **does not depend on any of them**, so none of their documented routes exist. This is a hard blocker for M5: a shell cannot be built against endpoints that are absent, and the sign-in screen — the first surface a user meets — has no endpoint to post to. Note what it says about our own gates: `xtask policy-routing` passes because every *registered* handler reaches `PolicyEngine::enforce`; it has no opinion about routes that were never registered. A gate that checks the handlers you wrote cannot see the ones you did not | **Closed by PR #71.** `crates/api` registers **43** routes across auth, uploads, shares, search, delivery, admin, sync, workflows, workspaces and libraries; 51 handlers all reach `PolicyEngine::enforce`. A client logs in, uploads, browses and previews — every step verified by an HTTP request against the running binary rather than by a test.
| ENC-683 | Toast auto-dismisses at 3 s, but `docs/09 §14` promises undo for every reversible destructive action | P2 | TODO | The eighth design/doc contradiction, and the only one the audit found beyond `ENC-676`'s seven — worth noting the audit was explicitly told that finding none was a valid result, so this is a real one rather than a filled quota. The prototype puts the undo affordance **only** inside a toast and force-dismisses every toast on a fixed 3-second timer. `docs/09 §14` promises undo for every reversible destructive action. A three-second window is not undo — it is a reflex test, and it fails exactly the users least able to pass it, which makes it an accessibility problem rather than a timing preference. Resolve by keeping the toast as the *fast* path and giving undo a durable home (the trash, or an activity entry), so the promise does not depend on reaction time |

| ENC-685 | **`/api/v1/auth/*` exists.** Login, MFA verify, refresh, logout, logout-all and session management, per `docs/05 §3` | P1 | DONE | The first half of `ENC-682`, and the M5 blocker inside it: `crates/auth` was 5,402 lines of complete, tested authentication — Argon2id, rotation, reuse detection, revocation, the `HttpOnly; Secure; SameSite=Strict` cookie — reachable by nothing, so nobody could sign in. This is the transport and only the transport: no password is verified, no token rotated and no family revoked anywhere but `crates/auth`. Four things needed real decisions rather than defaults. **The tenant on an unauthenticated route** is a `RoutedTenant` extractor over a new `enclave_db::resolve_routed_tenant` — written inside `crates/db` beside `active_tenants`, because `DbPool::platform_connection` had already predicted this caller in its own doc comment and named where it must live. The handler receives a `TenantId` it did not choose and the body has no field that could steer tenancy, so rule 3 is structural rather than remembered. **`revoke_family` takes no tenant** — deliberately, so a caller cannot supply one — which means calling it with a `sid` straight from the path would let any authenticated caller end any session in any tenant; the family is confirmed to be the caller's own in a tenant-scoped transaction first, and one that is not theirs is `404` (rule 7; `K16` asserts another tenant's, another user's, and nobody's, against the caller's own succeeding in the same run). **`logout` is an authenticated mutation and must go through the chain**, but there is no `ResourceKind::Session` and adding one changes `enclave_core::Action`, which by design breaks every exhaustive match in every policy service — so it asks `Container(Delete)` on the caller's own `User` and `SelfServiceAuthorization` gained one narrow rule, with the "reads as delete-my-account" hazard named on the type and owned by `ENC-689`. **Double-submit CSRF** is real: a readable `enclave_csrf` cookie, a constant-time comparison, and `Path=/` on that one because `document.cookie` cannot see the API-scoped path the refresh token keeps. Fifteen integration tests against live PostgreSQL, every absence paired with its positive control; thirteen deliberate breaks watched to fail by name. **One failed nothing**: deleting the application `tenant_id` predicate from the login lookup leaves the cross-tenant test green, because row-level security makes beta's row invisible rather than filtered — the fourth session to find that shape — so the mechanism is recorded on the test and layer 2 is held by a unit test that does fail. | ENC-686 | Custom-domain routing cannot read `tenant_domains`, so the routing key is the slug | P2 | DONE | Found writing `ENC-685`. `enclave_identity::TenantRepository::find_by_verified_domain` exists and is correct, and nothing can call it: `tenant_domains` carries `tenant_id`, so migration `0002` gave it a policy, and `0002` grants `enclave_platform` nothing on it — a query fails with `permission denied` rather than returning no rows. `enclave_app` cannot help either; it has no grant on `tenants` at all, because `0003` derives its grants from the `tenant_id` predicate and `tenants` has no such column. So `resolve_routed_tenant` reads the **slug** from the leftmost label, which `docs/04 §7` already calls a routing key. Second grant in this shape today after `ENC-705`: correct code above, superuser underneath, months unnoticed |
| ENC-878 | The platform role's grants are now swept, not sampled | P2 | DONE | Two grants were found missing in one session — `refresh_tokens` (`ENC-705`) and `tenant_domains` (`ENC-686`) — each with correct code above it and a superuser underneath it, each unnoticed for months. The list is derived from the four call sites of `DbPool::platform_connection`, which `no_raw_pool.py` keeps exhaustive — that gate exists so `grep -rn platform_connection crates/` stays a complete list of the places isolation is stepped around, which makes it a usable index here. | ENC-687 | **The binary can sign a user in.** PostgreSQL-backed refresh, denylist and session-facts stores, key material by reference, and the composition in `main.rs` | P1 | DONE | The other half of `ENC-682`'s auth gap, and the row four milestones of tested backend were waiting behind: `ENC-685` shipped seven routes and every one of them answered `503 DEPENDENCY_UNAVAILABLE` in the deployed process. **Three independent layers each looked correct in isolation.** `crates/auth` declared `RefreshTokenStore`, `DenylistStore`, `EpochStore` and `SessionFactsProvider` and shipped only the in-memory implementations it exports for its own tests; `main.rs` built `KeySet::new(std::iter::empty())` and left `AuthSurface::unconfigured` on `ApiState`; and `db_config_from` passed neither `database.platform_url` — without which `resolve_routed_tenant` fails and every login answers `404` — nor `database.application_role`, without which the pool connects as whatever the DSN names and row-level security does not apply to it. Each of the three was individually defensible and the product was unusable. **`crates/db/src/auth_tokens.rs`** is the missing storage: it sits in `enclave-db` for the ordinary reason `dlp` and `conditional_access` do *and* one only it has — three of its five statements are cross-tenant, because `RefreshTokenStore`'s signatures deliberately take no tenant, and `platform_connection` may not be called from outside that crate. **Key material is a reference** (rule 11): `auth.signing_keys.key_ref` resolves through the secret registry to base64 PKCS#8 and `ConfiguredKeyProvider` loads it; nothing about the key can reach a log except its `kid`, which is public information, and a test asserts a malformed-material refusal echoes none of what it was given. **The development default is a mechanism, not a rule**: `SigningKeys::choose` hands out a generated key only for a `community` profile bound to a **loopback** address, so a process any other host can reach has no branch that produces one and every other configuration refuses to start — the refusal being in this binary rather than in `ConfigLoader`, which is `ENC-661`'s trap, since a signing key is meaningless to `enclave-worker`. **`enclave-cli set-password`** is how a deployment gets its first sign-in at all: `seed` writes users and nothing had ever written a `user_credentials` row. Proven against the running binary and the dev database — `POST /api/v1/auth/login` returns a real EdDSA token, `GET /api/v1/me` accepts it, rotation issues a successor, a replayed token returns `SESSION_REPLAY` and destroys the family, logout revokes it, and a wrong password and an absent user both take 1.03 s. Ten deliberate breaks watched to fail by name; **three tests did not hold what they claimed** on the first attempt and were rewritten — the concurrency test was racing the classification rather than the `UPDATE` predicate, and `auth_time` needed both an aged family and two rotations before the two candidate values differed at all |

Two smaller pieces belong with it: `user_credentials.failed_attempts` is now incremented and cleared but nothing sets `locked_until`, because a lockout threshold is a policy value that belongs beside the rest of `PasswordPolicy` and not at the transport layer, where it would be an account-lockout denial-of-service in a file nobody would think to look in; and `STEP_UP_REQUIRED`'s `acr` and `maxAge` are `docs/05 §3.3`'s literals because no stage can express a per-rule value yet |

A second attempted break did not compile, which is the answer: `DegradedReason` has no constructor but `Retrieval::decide`, so a route that reached the lexical generator without taking the decision is not expressible. | ENC-699 | A search result carries no classification badge, because the effective label has no batch form | P2 | TODO | Found building `ENC-695`. `docs/05-API.md §11` puts `classification` on every result and `docs/09 §10` renders it as a badge. The two implementations available without a new query were both refused: the file's own `classification_id` reports an inherited `CONFIDENTIAL` as unlabelled, and a badge that under-reports sensitivity is worse than no badge; and a per-hit chain walk is twenty recursive queries for a page of twenty. | ENC-890 | The API cannot see the coverage trigger, so a collection recreated empty still reports `degraded: false` | P2 | TODO | Found closing `ENC-698`. `docs/07-SEARCH-INDEXING.md §6.4.1` makes coverage the **third** degradation trigger, alongside unreachability and denylist overflow, precisely because a store that is up and *empty* answers every probe and returns two hits out of forty thousand documents. The probe that establishes it is `crates/worker/src/coverage.rs` — per tenant, in a background loop, against `index_manifests` — and its verdict is exported as a metric and reaches nothing else. `crates/api`'s retrieval decision reads `VectorIndex::reachability` and `denylist::in_force`, so `VectorStore::Depleted` is **unreachable from the query path**: a tenant whose collection was dropped and recreated gets confident, complete-looking, nearly empty answers while the operator gauges say otherwise. That is `§6.4.1`'s own failure mode one process along. Not a correctness defect — the post-filter is unaffected and coverage is a recall signal — which is why it is a P2. Closing it means the probe's per-tenant verdict reaching an API replica, and the shape matters: it must stay a background state and never become a per-request measurement, or the same query answers completely at 10:00:01 and degraded at 10:00:02, which `crates/search/src/degraded.rs` refuses. Recorded in `docs/07 §6.4.2` |
| ENC-891 | Dense retrieval is dense-only, so `docs/07 §5`'s hybrid fusion has never run | P2 | TODO | Found closing `ENC-698`. `docs/07-SEARCH-INDEXING.md §5` specifies a **hybrid** query — dense and learned-sparse, fused and reranked — and `§4`'s collection has a `sparse_vector` field that `crates/search/src/writer.rs` populates on every chunk. Nothing issues a hybrid request: `MilvusIndex::candidates` builds a `SearchRequest` over `dense_vector` alone, so the sparse side is written, indexed, paid for in storage and never read. `ENC-698` therefore reports `diagnostics.mode: "semantic"` rather than `"hybrid"`, which is the honest value and the reason this is a row rather than a silent shortfall — reporting `hybrid` would be the same lie `degraded` exists to prevent, one field along. The recall cost is real and asymmetric: dense retrieval is weakest on exact tokens (identifiers, clause numbers, part codes), which is what the sparse side is for, so the queries this misses are the ones a user is most certain should have matched. Also unapplied: `§5`'s server-built `library_id in {…}` and `classification_rank <=` pre-filter, which `Prefilter::resolved_from_postgres` exists for and which nothing calls — a cost narrowing, never a control (`crates/search/src/vector.rs`) |
| ENC-892 | Every search pays a Milvus round trip, and an outage pays a connect attempt, with no breaker | P2 | TODO | Found closing `ENC-698`. The retrieval decision needs the store's health, and the only source of it is `VectorIndex::reachability` — a `has_collection` probe taken **per request**, inside the search's transaction. That is deliberate and the alternative is worse: a health verdict cached per process would make the same query answer completely on one replica and degraded on another with no state change between them, which is the per-request-signal failure `crates/search/src/degraded.rs` is built to refuse. What is missing is the circuit breaker `crates/search/src/milvus.rs` already says is absent: while the store is down every request pays one `connect_timeout` (2s by default, short *because* of this) holding a pooled PostgreSQL connection, so a Milvus outage becomes a connection-pool problem on a busy replica. The fix is a breaker in front of the probe whose open state is itself the cross-request signal — the shape the doctrine wants anyway, rather than an exception to it |

The seven tests in `crates/search/tests/milvus.rs` and the vector half of `crates/search/tests/vector_write.rs` connect to `MILVUS_URI`, defaulting to `http://127.0.0.1:19530`, and there is nothing there. They pass locally against the compose stack — verified while closing `ENC-698`, including `s5_an_over_permissive_index_decides_nothing`, which is the live-index half of leakage row `S5` — so the store-side guarantee **is** proven on a developer machine and is not proven by CI. Two resolutions and they are not equivalent: add the service, or gate the suite on the endpoint being reachable and report it skipped. The second is worse for the reason `docs/12 §1.2` keeps finding — a security test that quietly does not run is indistinguishable from one that passes — so the first is the intended fix |
