It is the class `crates/api/tests/reachability.rs` exists for: a seam between two individually correct components, in the one file no in-process test executes. It cannot go there today for two reasons that both have to be solved — CI runs no Milvus (`ENC-893`), and the composition also wants a mounted model, which CI stages but which would make the API process load 2.2 GB of weights in a smoke test. The tractable first step is the half that needs neither: asserting the three warning paths, and that `vector_retrieval` returns `None` rather than refusing to start, which is the property `docs/08 §18.1` now states and nothing checks |

Dark is fine: `--fg3` there is `#8A8A84` on `#161615`, 5.2:1. So this is a **light-theme-only defect in the neutral scale**, and it is load-bearing — the prototype uses `--fg3` for secondary text on every screen. `ENC-672` worked around it by using `--fg2` for every quiet text colour in the file list, which is darker than the design intends and does not scale to five more screens. The real fix is one step in the scale, decided by whoever owns the token values, and it belongs in the prototype first and then in the extract (`ENC-678`). Until then every new surface pays the same workaround |

| ENC-705 | **`enclave_platform` has no grant on `refresh_tokens`, so refresh and logout fail in a role-separated deployment** | P1 | DONE | Found by `ENC-687`, and the same shape as `ENC-686`. `RefreshTokenStore::find_by_hash`, `revoke_family` and `revoke_all_for_subject` take no tenant *by design* — accepting one would mean accepting it from a caller, one layer from a request body — so the implementation resolves the tenant from the stored row, which is a cross-tenant read on the `BYPASSRLS` connection. `migrations/0002` grants `enclave_platform` only `SELECT, UPDATE, DELETE ON events_outbox` and `SELECT ON tenants`; `0003` grants only `enclave_app`. So those three statements fail with `permission denied` rather than returning no rows. They work today because the dev stack and the test harness both connect as the cluster superuser, which bypasses grants and RLS alike — a property of those environments and not of the code. **The migration:** `GRANT SELECT, UPDATE ON refresh_tokens TO enclave_platform;` and nothing more — no `INSERT`, because every new row is written with a tenant in hand through `DbPool::begin`, and no `DELETE`, because a refresh row is revoked through `revoked_at` and never removed. Login is unaffected and works in a role-separated deployment already | **Closed.** `migrations/0025_refresh_token_platform_grants.sql` grants `SELECT, UPDATE ON refresh_tokens` — the two privileges `find_by_hash` and `revoke_returning` need, and no more: `INSERT` and `DELETE` are deliberately withheld because `insert` and `rotate` carry their own `tenant_id` through `DbPool::begin`, and this role holds `BYPASSRLS` so every privilege it gains sees every tenant. The test **runs the statements under `SET ROLE enclave_platform`** rather than reading `information_schema`, because a grant existing and the statement the code issues being permitted are different claims — and because the harness connects as the cluster superuser, which is precisely why this survived: any assertion made without `SET ROLE` passes whether or not the migration ran. Paired with a negative control (`DELETE` is refused), since "two statements succeeded" passes for free on a connection that bypasses grants. Watched to fail by removing the grant: *permission denied for table refresh_tokens*.
| ENC-877 | **Twelve tracker IDs were duplicated in one evening, and no gate could see it** | P2 | DONE | Seven of them asserted a thing and its negation — the same ID `DONE` on one line and `TODO` on another. Two causes, and they need different fixes. **Exact duplicates**: resolving a `TRACKER.md` conflict by keeping both sides is right for distinct rows and wrong for one row appearing on both, and I applied it six times tonight without checking for that case — `ENC-579` is the same lesson from the other direction, where keeping both sides let the *older* side's status cell win and six completed rows silently reverted. **ID collisions**: parallel sessions each take the next free number from the copy of the file they started with, so two sessions on one evening reach for the same one; seven pairs of unrelated rows collided that way and are now renumbered `ENC-870`–`ENC-876`, each saying where it came from so a reader who meets the old ID in a commit message can still find it. `tracker_rollup.py` cannot catch either: it deduplicates by ID with `§3` winning, so the duplicate is dropped from the count while both rows stay on the board. `.github/scripts/tracker_unique_ids.py` is the gate, wired into the aggregator's `needs` — checked, because `ENC-531` records that a gate missing from that list runs and cannot fail the build. Watched to fail by duplicating a row: names it by ID and line. It carries its own liveness check for `ENC-543`'s reason. **`ENC-676` is left `TODO`**: its two copies disagreed and nothing in the tree settles it, and a falsely closed row costs more than a redundant check |
| ENC-706 | `token_revocations` cannot distinguish a denied token from a denied session | P2 | TODO | `crates/auth/src/lib.rs` has recorded the gap since M0 — *"persisting that needs a session-scoped row; `token_revocations` is keyed `(tenant_id, jti)` only. Redis can express it today; PostgreSQL cannot."* `ENC-687` made it real rather than theoretical: `PgDenylist::deny_session` writes the `session_id` into the `jti` column and `is_denied` asks for either value. That is safe — both are 122-bit random UUIDs, a collision denies a token that should not have been denied rather than the reverse, and nothing else reads the table — but it is not **legible**: an operator reading the table cannot tell the two apart, and incident response reads this table. **The migration:** `ALTER TABLE token_revocations ADD COLUMN IF NOT EXISTS kind TEXT NOT NULL DEFAULT 'JTI' CHECK (kind IN ('JTI','SESSION'));` with the primary key widened to `(tenant_id, kind, jti)`, expand-then-contract so the existing rows keep their meaning |
| ENC-707 | A rotation cannot know which factors the session actually used, so MFA sessions weaken to `acr: 1fa` | P2 | TODO | Found by `ENC-687` writing `PgSessionFacts`. `refresh_tokens` records no `amr`, so a session that completed a second factor re-issues with `amr: ["pwd"]` ten minutes later. It errs **downwards** — a conditional-access rule requiring `acr: 2fa` starts refusing a genuinely multi-factor session rather than admitting a single-factor one — so it is a correctness and availability defect and not a privilege one. **What must not be done in the meantime** is the obvious shortcut of reading `user_mfa_methods`: enrolment is not use, and a token whose `amr` claims a factor the user did not present is a forged assertion the storage layer would be manufacturing. The fix is a column on the refresh family, written at issue and inherited by every successor |
| ENC-708 | Signing keys are one key from configuration, so rotation is a restart rather than an overlap | P2 | TODO | `ENC-687`'s `ConfiguredKeyProvider` holds a single key, so `auth.signing_keys.rotation_interval` and `overlap` are configuration nothing reads and `KeyStatus::Retiring` is a state no deployment enters. `migrations/0001` already defines `signing_keys` — `kid`, `public_key`, `private_key_ref`, `status`, `activates_at`, `retires_at` — and nothing reads or writes it. The provider that does is this row: it is what makes `KeySet::verification_key`'s retirement check (test K2) mean something in a deployment rather than only in a unit test, and what turns a key rotation from a coordinated restart into the propagation window `KeyStatus::Pending` was designed for |

It is **announced in the start-up banner** beside the unenforcing policy stages, with a test that it is announced in all five DLP postures, because an operator reading `conditional_access` as wired and nothing here would reasonably conclude that a network rule bounds a session. Blocked on `ENC-689`: a real guard needs a `RequestContext` built from a refresh row, which needs an action and a resource for "refresh a session", and `enclave_core` has neither |

That is no longer true: the store resolves the family's tenant from its stored row, so the tenant **is** known at the point of refusal. Writing the row is the remaining half, and it belongs with `ENC-709`, which is what makes the refusal reachable at all |
| ENC-711 | **A generated private key reached a commit, because `.gitignore` protected a path and the path became a setting** | P0 | DONE | `deploy/README.md` and `deploy/config/enclave.example.yaml` had both said `deploy/config/dev-keys/` was git-ignored since M0. Found while wiring the key provider into the binary, which is when the directory acquired its first caller — and then demonstrated for real, when a checkpoint commit taken during a session stall captured a generated key from a scratch directory. Fixed in both directions: the directory rule now exists, **and** `*.pkcs8.der` is matched wherever it is written, because `auth.signing_keys.directory` is configuration now and a path-based rule cannot be complete once the path is a setting. | ENC-690 | **`ENC-682` part B — the upload and sharing routes** | P1 | REVIEW | `crates/api` gains `POST /uploads`, `POST /uploads/{id}/complete`, `GET`/`DELETE /uploads/{id}` (`docs/05-API.md §8`) and `POST`/`GET /files/{id}/shares` plus `PATCH`/`DELETE /shares/{id}` (`§10`). `crates/uploads` and `crates/sharing` were complete, tested and depended on by nothing; this is the transport layer they were missing, not new domain logic — no ACL is read here, no quota is charged here, and no state is written that either crate could not already write. Rule 9 is held by construction rather than by a comparison: `UploadService::complete` returns a `Session<Scanning>` and there is no phase after it, so the wire `state` is rendered from `Session::state()` and `AVAILABLE` is not a value this module can produce. Four things came out of the work as rows rather than as scope: `ENC-691`, `ENC-692`, `ENC-693`, `ENC-694`. Two constants in the new module are honest placeholders and are named as such at their definitions — the 24 h session TTL that `docs/03-LLD.md §15` gives as `upload.session_ttl` (`crates/config` models no such key), and the tenant-default file ceiling that `docs/04 §17`'s `MAX_FILE_BYTES` quota kind would supply if anything read it |
| ENC-691 | **Nothing consumes `ScanHandoff`, so a completed upload never becomes a version** | P1 | DONE | Found by `ENC-690` writing `POST /uploads/{id}/complete`. `UploadService::complete` verifies the bytes, writes `SCANNING` and returns a `#[must_use]` `ScanHandoff` described by its own crate as *"the entire interface between an accepted upload and everything that has to happen before anyone can read it"* — and **no crate in the workspace consumes one**. So a completed upload leaves a session row in `SCANNING`, no `files` row, no `file_versions` row and nothing for `crates/worker`'s antivirus pass to find: that pass queues on `file_versions.av_status`, and the reaper's `holds_staged_bytes` excludes `SCANNING`, so the session is stranded and its staged bytes are never released either. This is `ENC-641` one stage earlier — correct code on both sides of a join that nobody wrote — and it is why the `complete` handler stops where it does rather than committing a version: doing it in the handler would need a `files` row for the new-file intent and a `storage_profile_id`, and `ENC-573` established that no `storage_profiles` table exists, so there is no honest source for that column at the HTTP edge. The commit belongs beside the antivirus pass in `crates/worker`, driven from the handoff, and `crates/versions::commit` already carries the quota charge (`ENC-589`) and the index enqueue (`ENC-643`) it must run inside | **Closed by `promote` in `crates/api/src/routes/uploads.rs`, called from `complete` inside the transaction `UploadService::complete` already had open.** The handoff is taken **by value**, which is the part that matters: `#[must_use]` was already satisfied by the old handler, because it read `handoff.staged` for the two identifiers it put on the wire — a `202` naming a `fileId` and a `versionId` for rows that did not exist is what "consumed" looked like from inside. A move has no such reading. **Both blockers this row recorded turned out to be answerable, and neither wanted the worker.** The row proposed committing beside the antivirus pass; that cannot work, because that pass queues on `file_versions.av_status` and would have had to invent the row it was waiting for. (a) *No `files` row for a new-file upload*: `FileRepository::create_file` existed with **no caller in any binary**, so nothing had ever created one. It now takes the node's id, because `StagedObject` spent that id at session creation to stage the bytes under `tenant/{t}/files/{f}/versions/{v}` — a node minted with a fresh id leaves an object key naming a file that does not exist, and the key cannot be rewritten without the 5 GB copy the staging layout exists to avoid. (b) *No source for `storage_profile_id`*: correct, and the honest value is a named one. `UNPROVISIONED_STORAGE_PROFILE` is the nil UUID, which `new_v4`/`new_v7` cannot produce, so the backfill that ends it is one `WHERE` clause; copying `libraries.storage_profile_id` was rejected because it is a nullable pointer into the same absent table and would make the column mean two unresolvable things at once. **The same argument applied one level down and was taken**: `VersionService::commit` minted its own `VersionId`, so the object key named a version no `SELECT` could find. `NewVersion` now carries the id; `restore` still mints one, because a restore's key is invented by its caller. **One transaction, session state included.** A refused commit rolls the `SCANNING` write back too, so "session says SCANNING, nothing exists to scan" is unrepresentable rather than unlikely — the version is `SCANNING`/`PENDING` numbered `1.0`, the quota charge (`ENC-589`), the index manifest (`ENC-643`), the outbox row and the audit row all commit with it or not at all. Rule 9 is untouched: the status and verdict come from two constants inside `commit` that take no argument, and a new source-scanning test refuses `VersionStatus::`, `AvStatus::` and any statement of its own anywhere in the route module. The journey test is now the **positive control** for the absence the old one asserted — "no version is `AVAILABLE` after complete" passed for free against a path that created none — and a second test runs `enclave_worker::antivirus::av_pass` over what the upload committed, because "the row exists" and "the queue that has to move it can see it" are two claims. Eight deliberate breaks. **One failed nothing and is the day's finding**: replacing the handler's `rollback` with a `commit` left every test green, because a duplicate-name refusal is a constraint violation, PostgreSQL aborts the transaction and `COMMIT` on an aborted transaction *is* a rollback — so that test held its property by accident of the database. The case where the choice is real is a **quota** refusal, which comes back from a statement that *succeeded*, and it now has its own test that does fail. Verified by hand against the running binaries and MinIO, not only by test. Logged rather than absorbed: `ENC-784`–`ENC-788`, and `ENC-789` for the required reachability gate, which fails identically at the base commit `b5830d3` on a stale `ENC-770` quarantine entry |
| ENC-784 | `libraries.versioning_mode`, `version_limit`, `require_checkout` and `require_approval` decide nothing | P2 | TODO | Found closing `ENC-691`, which had to choose a `VersionBump` and found no column that could tell it. All four are modelled in `LibrarySettings`, written by `library_repo`, and read by **no code path that acts on them**: `versioning_mode` appears outside `crates/libraries` only in a test fixture, `version_limit` only in the repository's own column lists. So every upload commits `VersionBump::Major`, a library in `NONE` mode accumulates a full history anyway, a `MAJOR_MINOR` library can never produce a draft, `version_limit` prunes nothing, and `require_checkout` guards nothing — `file_locks` exists and no write path consults it. `file_versions.approval_state` is the fourth: the column and the vocabulary are there and nothing ever sets it, so `require_approval` is a checkbox with no effect. `Major` was chosen deliberately rather than by default — it is what `docs/04 §7`'s default mode gives, and minor bumps would file every upload as a draft of a version nobody published — and it is named as a decision at its call site. Closing this is a policy read at commit time plus a pruning pass, in a crate `ENC-691` did not own |
| ENC-785 | An upload that **creates** a file audits `file.edit`, because commit has one audit action | P2 | TODO | Found closing `ENC-691`. `VersionService::commit` writes `file.edit` and `restore` writes `file.version_restore` — a distinction `ENC-589` made deliberately so an auditor filtering for "who put this content back" need not infer it. There is now a third case it does not have: a completion whose intent was `UploadIntent::NewFile`, which the chain decided as `container.create` on the library and which the commit then audits as an edit of a file that did not exist a statement earlier. The chain's own allow row *is* written and carries `container.create`, so nothing is unaudited and no investigation is blind — the two rows are adjacent in one chain — but "who created this document" is answered by joining two rows on a request id rather than by reading one. The fix is a third action on the commit, chosen by whether the file was created in the same transaction, and it belongs with `ENC-622`'s question about handlers attaching detail to the row the engine writes |
| ENC-786 | A rolled-back completion has already completed the object at the provider | P2 | TODO | Found closing `ENC-691` and stated rather than papered over. `UploadService::complete` calls `BlobStore::complete_upload` — a side effect outside any transaction, because blob storage cannot join one (`docs/03-LLD.md §15`) — and `ENC-691` then rolls the whole transaction back when the version commit is refused, which returns the session to `UPLOADING`. That is the right state to be retryable from, and for a single-shot upload the retry works: `complete_upload` is a `HeadObject` against bytes that are already there. For a **multipart** one it may not, because `CompleteMultipartUpload` on an upload the provider has already completed is an error, and the session still carries the `multipart_id` it would present. So a large upload refused by the quota is retryable in principle and possibly not in practice, and the client is told to retry either way. Two candidate shapes: make the retry idempotent by treating "already completed" as success when the object's size and checksum match, or record the provider-side completion on the session so the retry skips it. Neither is a change `ENC-691` owned, and the failure is at least loud rather than silent |
| ENC-787 | Sessions stranded `SCANNING` before `ENC-691` are still stranded, and nothing reclaims them | P2 | DONE | `ENC-691` stops new ones being created; it does not clear the ones already there. The development database has one, from the hand-run that raised the row. Such a session has no `files` row and no `file_versions` row, so no antivirus pass will ever touch it, and `UploadState::Scanning.holds_staged_bytes()` is `false`, so `reap_expired` will not release its staged object either — the bytes are in the bucket, unmetered, referenced by nothing, indefinitely. The reaper's exclusion is correct for a session that *did* commit a version, which is why this is not a one-line predicate change: the two cases are distinguishable only by whether a `file_versions` row names the session's `staged_key`. Wants a repair pass (or a migration) that finds sessions in `SCANNING` with no version behind them and expires them, and it should report what it found rather than delete quietly | **Closed by `enclave_uploads::reclaim_stranded` and `enclave-cli reclaim-uploads`.** The row's own reading was right and understated the danger. It is not that the reaper's `SCANNING` exclusion is *merely* correct for a committed session — since `ENC-691` the staged key **is** the version's `object_key` (nothing is copied on commit), so a reclaim that claimed a committed session would delete a live file's only copy and leave `file_versions` pointing at nothing. That is data loss, not a missed clean-up, which is why the distinguishing fact is carried in a **type** rather than in a predicate somebody has to remember: `StrandedSession` has exactly one constructor, `UploadRepository::claim_stranded`, whose statement asserts `NOT EXISTS` a `file_versions` row naming the staged key *inside the same `FOR UPDATE` claim*, and it is the only value from which the `SCANNING` → `EXPIRED` transition is reachable. `Session<Scanning>` still has no transition out and rule 9 is untouched. **Two independent guards, failing in opposite directions.** The version join is load-bearing and sufficient on its own today — the version commits in the same transaction as the `SCANNING` write, so under MVCC a committed `SCANNING` row with no version cannot be a completion in flight. The second is `updated_at <= now - idle_for`, which is what still holds if a future path ever writes `SCANNING` and commits its version in a *second* transaction. `expires_at` is deliberately **not** used: it is the upload TTL, not a measure of how long a session has been claiming to scan. **Where it runs**: an operator command, because the defect is a historical backlog rather than an ongoing leak, and because `reap_expired` has no production caller either — `ENC-806`, logged rather than absorbed. `--dry-run` prints the whole candidate list and deletes nothing, which is this row's "report what it found rather than delete quietly" in its strong form. **Four deliberate breaks, each watched to fail by name**, and the third is the finding: deleting the `tenant_id` predicate **failed to fail** — the ninth time in this repository — because RLS holds it alone, so the cross-tenant test says in its own doc comment that it proves isolation and not the predicate, and the source-level gate `every_statement_carries_the_application_tenant_predicate` is what actually catches it. Verified by hand on the development database, which held the row's own example: ten `SCANNING` sessions, nine with versions behind them (one of them 24 seconds old), and `01a04094-cb29-…` `hello.txt` with none. The pass found exactly that one, deleted its object, moved the row to `EXPIRED`, left the other nine untouched, and a second run found nothing |
| ENC-788 | `FileRepository::create_folder` still has no caller, so no folder can be created | P2 | DONE | Noticed closing `ENC-691`, which took `create_file` off the unwired list and left its neighbour on it. `docs/05-API.md` gives folder creation a place and `crates/api` registers no route for it, so every uploaded file lands at a library root: `POST /uploads` accepts a `parentId`, the policy chain decides against that folder rather than the library, and there is no way for a client to have obtained one. The whole of the tree — `create_folder`, `rename`, `reparent`, `trash`, `restore`, `list_children` — is in the same position, which makes this the container half of `ENC-682` rather than a one-route gap, and it is why the `ENC-691` journey exercises the library-root path only | **Closed by `POST /api/v1/libraries/{id}/folders` in `crates/api/src/routes/folders.rs`.** `docs/05-API.md` gave folder creation a path and four words and nothing else — no body, no response, no status — so `§7.2` was written first and the code follows it (`ENC-794`'s precedent, `ENC-809` records that the rest of `§7`'s table is in the same state). **The half of the row that turned out to be already done**: `POST /uploads` needed no change at all. It has accepted `parentId` since `ENC-690` and `target_of` already resolves `container.create` against `ResourceRef::folder` — the gap really was only that no client could ever obtain a folder id to send. `folders::container_of` is written to mirror `uploads::container_of` line for line, and a unit test asserts the two agree, because a folder a caller may put a *file* in and may not put a *folder* in would be two answers to one question. **The decision worth arguing is which container the chain is asked about**: the named parent, never the library id in the path. The test that separates them is a folder with `inherit_permissions = FALSE` inside the granted library — the resolver's walk stops at it, so the library's grant does not reach it, and the wrong reading answers `201` and creates a folder inside a container the caller cannot write to. That is `ENC-141`'s shape, gained privilege from a truncated walk, and it is the **only** test in the file that catches it: the other four pass against the wrong implementation. **Two smaller decisions.** A name collision is `409` per `§5`'s status table, intercepted before `crates/files`' blanket `From<FilesError>` conversion, which maps `NameTaken` onto `Error::Validation` — a `400` (`ENC-808` records that the upload path still answers that); the refusal never echoes the name. And there is deliberately no `inheritPermissions` field: shipping a folder with inheritance already broken would make `ENC-141`'s edit reachable in one unreviewed request. Leakage row `A20`. Verified by hand against the running binary and MinIO: login, `201` for the folder, `409` for the same name again, `201` for a folder inside it, then `POST /uploads` with that `parentId`, a pre-signed PUT, `202 SCANNING` — and the `files` row for `q3.txt` carries `parent_id` = the folder rather than `NULL` |

It answers `201`, so the gate fails on the *positive* half of the quarantine check, which is the half that exists to stop the list rotting. Two things behind it, and the second is the reason this is a P1 rather than a one-line delete. **The entry must go and `ENC-770` must close** — the test says so in its own failure message. And **the reachability job in CI has no object storage at all**: its comment states the binary *"contacts neither Redis nor NATS nor object storage"*, which stopped being true when `ENC-770` was wired, because `S3BlobStore::connect_and_verify` runs `HeadBucket` and the public-access self-check at start-up and the binary refuses to start when they fail. | ENC-692 | **`GET /shares/{token}` cannot be registered: no connection the API may hold can resolve a token to a tenant** | P1 | TODO | Found by `ENC-690`. `sharing::repo::find_by_digest` documents itself as resolving a digest *"across every tenant"* and closes with *"the `no-raw-pool` gate and RLS still apply to everything else here"* — but RLS is a table policy, not a per-query opt-out. `migrations/0008` puts `USING (tenant_id = current_setting('app.tenant_id')::uuid)` on `share_links` with `FORCE`, so on a `TenantScoped` connection the lookup sees one tenant, and on an unscoped one `current_setting` raises and it sees nothing. The only connection that would work is `DbPool::platform_connection`, which `.github/scripts/no_raw_pool.py` refuses outside `crates/db` and which `crates/api/src/main.rs` does not configure a URL for in any case. So the crate's redemption path is written against a connection type nobody can supply, and its own tests never caught it because every one of them redeems inside the owning tenant's transaction. The route is therefore **not registered** rather than registered and broken: a `GET /shares/{token}` that 503s or that silently only ever resolves same-tenant links is `ENC-170`'s shape. Two candidate fixes, and the choice is a design conversation: (a) a narrow `share_link_tenant(digest) -> Option<TenantId>` beside `crate::active_tenants` in `crates/db` — which is the procedure `DbPool::platform_connection`'s own doc comment prescribes for a fourth caller — plus a platform URL for the API binary; or (b) custom-domain routing, which `docs/03-LLD.md §56` already names as the second lawful source of tenant identity and which nothing implements. `POST /shares/{token}/authenticate` is blocked behind the same question. What *is* proved meanwhile is the rule-7 half: `a_share_token_from_another_tenant_is_indistinguishable_from_one_that_does_not_exist` presents alpha's token under beta's scope and gets `LinkUnusable`, so the isolation is structural even though the resolution is missing | **Both blockers this row names are gone, and it should be read with that in mind.** It says custom-domain routing is what *nothing implements*: `ENC-686` implemented it — `resolve_routed_tenant` resolves a verified domain before a slug, and `migrations/0026` grants the `SELECT` on `tenant_domains` it needs. It says `crates/api`'s binary configures no platform URL: `main.rs` reads `database.platform_url` and warns when it is unset. And `RoutedTenant` already exists as an extractor (`routes/auth.rs`), used by the login handlers. So candidate (b) is buildable, and it is the right one: resolving a digest across tenants would make the URL path a **third** source of tenant identity, which is the shape rule 3 exists to forbid, while `RoutedTenant` establishes the tenant exactly as login does and leaves RLS to do what `migrations/0008` wrote it for. The cost is that a share link only works on its tenant's host — acceptable, and already implied by `CreatedShareView`, which says the client composes the redemption URL. In flight.

The API's copy deliberately never selects `token_hash` or `password_hash` (it projects `password_hash IS NOT NULL`), so the duplication is not merely a copy; folding it back means `sharing::repo` growing `list_for_resource`, `find` and `update` and the API projecting its wire view from a `ShareLink` |

Enforces `file.export` and **only** `file.export` — a caller with `download` and no `export` is refused, which is the assertion `docs/12 §4.2` A2 has been asking for since M1. Serves a rendition through `PreviewPipeline`, which has no method that can name an object key or mint a URL, so the handler cannot answer an export with the original. `NO_DOWNLOAD` refuses it: `docs/06 §5.2` says no-download means no *downloadable representation*, and a flattened export is one. A `WATERMARK` obligation is discharged in the pixels where the compositor can (`png`) and **refuses** where it cannot (`pdf`), per rule 8 |
| ENC-720 | `POST /files/{id}/print-token` — a print capability with a lifetime and one use | P1 | DONE | 2026-08-26. Enforces `file.print`. The grant is 256 bits from the OS CSPRNG, returned once, and **only its SHA-256 is retained** — `plans/M2-ACCESS-DELIVERY.md` D19's treatment of share tokens, applied here for the same reason: a token that is stored is a token a leaked backup yields. It is bound to one tenant, file, version, actor and session, expires in 120 s, and is removed from the registry by the act of redeeming it, so a second redemption finds nothing. A `WATERMARK` obligation is *carried on the grant* rather than discharged, and the mint refuses outright when the actor is not a person the mark could name — a grant is not an exposure, so nothing can be served unmarked, but nothing may be redeemable unmarked either |

| ENC-620 | The admin surface's step-up requirement is enforced beside the chain, so its refusal is not audited | P2 | TODO | Found in `ENC-603`. `docs/06 §22` requires recent MFA for changing conditional access and `docs/05 §14` states it as a property of the admin surface; it is currently a check in the handler, run **after** `enforce` so that the chain decides first. The cost is stated in the code and is real: the engine has already written an *allow* row when the step-up check refuses, so audit records a decision the request did not act on, and the refusal itself leaves only a log line. It belongs in the conditional-access stage — it is a `RequireMfa` effect every deployment holds for one action — where it would be audited as the denial it is, and where a tenant could not weaken it because the stage's rules only ever add refusals. The same shape as `ENC-602`, and the same warning applies with more force: the key bounds how stale a second factor may be on the most privileged mutations in the product, so it wants a documented **maximum** rather than a free-form duration. A deployment that sets it to a day has turned the requirement off while leaving it switched on |
| ENC-622 | An audit row cannot say *which* rule an administrator changed | P2 | TODO | Found in `ENC-603`. Audit happens inside the policy engine (`CLAUDE.md` rule 10) and records the actor, the action and the resource — and the resource for an administrative action is the tenant, deliberately, because a decision that varied with the object would be an oracle for it. So the row says "this administrator changed conditional access at 14:03" and cannot say which rule, while `conditional_access_rules.name` exists precisely so an operator can name one. The handler logs it, which is not audit: a log line is not chained, not exportable and not what an investigation reads. Wants a way for a handler to attach a detail to the row the engine writes — the same port `crates/api/src/download.rs` says is missing for a justification, so the two are one change |
| ENC-623 | **An undecodable rule puts a tenant's own repair surface inside its blast radius** | P2 | TODO | Found by `ENC-603`'s own test, which asserts it rather than working around it. `ENC-590` makes one rule that cannot be decoded fail the **whole** set, for the right reason — a policy silently missing one refusal is a policy nobody wrote — so while such a row is live every request in the tenant errors, *including* `GET /api/v1/admin/conditional-access/rules`, which is where an administrator would find out which rule to withdraw. The list handler already survives it (each row is decoded individually and reported with its clause), and it does not help, because the chain runs first. P2 rather than P1 because the API refuses such a document, so the row can only arrive from `psql` or a repair script — and P2 rather than P3 because the recovery is another `psql` session, which is the surface `ENC-603` exists to remove. Both easy fixes are worse than the bug: an admin route that skipped the stage is rule 1, and a stage that read a decode failure as "no rules" is the permissive failure `C9` names. The shape worth considering is a stage that *denies* rather than errors while a tenant's rules are undecodable, with the administrative read exempted by action rather than by route — which is a design conversation, in a crate `ENC-603` did not own |
| ENC-624 | `main.rs` does not hand the API the rule cache, so a rule change waits out the TTL on the replica that made it | P2 | TODO | The one line `ENC-603` could not write: `crates/api/src/main.rs` is owned by another session. `ApiState::with_rule_cache` takes an `Arc<dyn RuleCache>` and `TenantConditionalAccess` implements it; the binary needs `Arc::new(conditional_access.clone())` handed to it before the value is moved into `PolicyEngine::new` (clones share one cache, by design). Bounded rather than broken, which is why it is P2: without it a tightening applies within `DEFAULT_CACHE_TTL` everywhere, which is exactly what an administrator is told anyway, and `ENC-603`'s tests wire the handle themselves so the behaviour is proved. The routes need no wiring at all — they are registered in `router()` |
| ENC-625 | An audit path a handler can reach, so `ENC-606`'s refusals leave a row | P1 | DONE | The fix `ENC-606` names, logged as its own row because `ENC-606` is the *defect* and this is the mechanism. `crates/api/src/refusal.rs`: `Refused` — private fields, `#[must_use]`, and **no conversion into `Error` or `ApiError`** — plus `HandlerAudit::refuse`, the only thing that can consume one, which writes the row and then returns the caller's error. So "a handler refusal is audited" is a property of the type system, the same argument that makes a `StageDecision` audited by construction one layer up, rather than a review convention. **A second row, not an amended one**: `audit_events` is append-only and hash-linked, so rewriting the chain's `ALLOW` would invalidate `event_hash` for every later row — the integrity property is what the fix had to work around rather than through. Both facts are true and the log carries both, paired by `request_id`; neither row asserts anything about the other, because a claim about a different row can be wrong. The row carries `outcome=DENY`, the same `reason_code` the caller was given, `policy_refs=handler:obligation`/`handler:actor` — prefixed so it can never be read as a chain stage, asserted against `Stage::ORDER` — and `detail.refused_by=handler` with the obligation's *kind* and never its payload (`RECLASSIFY`, never the rank). The sink is built in `ApiState::new` from the pool it already holds, so `main.rs` needed no change. `xtask audit-coverage` gains `Refused::` as a third constructor, `audited (handler)` by return type, a remedy line per kind, and a **second liveness check** on the handler port — because a tree full of `Refused` sites with no `refuse()` in it would classify every one as audited off its return type alone. Its six Group-3 acknowledgements went stale and were deleted; the staleness check is what required it, which is that mechanism doing the job it was built for. Six deliberate breaks watched to fail by name, and **one failed nothing**: deleting the sink write inside `refuse` leaves the gate green — the same shape as deleting `record_deny` from the chain, and recorded in `docs/12 §4.10` U7 rather than reworded |
| ENC-631 | **A DLP exception cannot be written**, because `ALLOW` fires and changes nothing | P2 | TODO | Found storing rules for `ENC-615`, and it is a defect in the *evaluator* that storage merely declined to enshrine. `docs/06 §10` documents `ALLOW` as "a rule that fires and does nothing, which exists so an exception can be written above a broader rule", and `DlpAction::Allow` exists — but its demand is `Demand::Nothing`, and `Verdict::blocking_code` is a `find_map` that **scans past** a `Nothing` to the next fired rule. So an `ALLOW` written above a `BLOCK` matches, fires, is recorded, and the caller is refused anyway. That is `docs/06 §7.4`'s "exception that appears to exist" exactly, one stage over, and `migrations/0021` therefore does not accept the string: refusing to store an exception can only deny more than was written, and it denies loudly at the moment the rule is written, where a stored one denies quietly during the incident. Closing it is not a column — it is a suppression rule in `Verdict`, and the question that has to be answered first is *what an `ALLOW` suppresses*: the first refusal below it, every refusal below it, or every refusal for the rules whose scope it shares. Each is defensible and they disagree, so it wants a decision recorded beside D28 rather than a guess in a `match`. P2 because the fail-closed direction holds meanwhile: a tenant that needs an exception writes a narrower blocking rule |
| ENC-632 | The DLP mode is deployment-wide, so one tenant's rollout sets the pace for every tenant on the host | P2 | TODO | Found wiring `ENC-615`. `dlp.default_mode` is one key in one `enclave.yaml`, and `TenantDlp` holds the mode it produced beside the pool — so a host serving fifty tenants runs all fifty at whichever rung of `docs/06 §9`'s ladder the operator last set. `plans/M4-GOVERNANCE.md §2` is precisely the argument for why that matters: a control that cannot be turned on gradually is turned on carelessly or not at all, and here "gradually" has to mean *per tenant*, because the tenant whose rules are still being tuned is not the tenant that has finished. The fix is a tenant-level setting, and the constraint on it is the one thing this row exists to carry forward: **the mode must not become a column on `dlp_rules`**. D28's guarantee is that `RuleSet::evaluate` takes no mode argument, and a per-rule mode has to reach the rule type to be read. A per-*tenant* mode read beside the rules — the same shape `TenantDlp` already has, one lookup earlier — keeps the property; a per-rule one destroys it. `docs/04 §12.3` and `migrations/0021`'s header both record the reasoning so whoever lands this does not have to reconstruct it |
| ENC-633 | DLP rules can be written by a repository call and by nothing else | P1 | DONE | `crates/api/src/admin/dlp.rs` and `docs/05-API.md §14.2`, on `ENC-603`'s shape: the module holds **no condition vocabulary of its own** — `scope` and `conditions` are carried as raw JSON, assembled into a `DlpRuleRow` and handed to `decode_rule`, the function the loader runs on every request — because an API is the second place `ENC-615`'s strictness is silently lost. The deliberate break confirms it at the new boundary: with `deny_unknown_fields` removed from `Condition`, `{"category_at_least":{…,"pattern":"x"}}` is answered **`201`**, and the stored document has no pattern in it. `ALLOW` is refused a third time with the reason it does nothing, `deny_unknown_fields` on the request body makes a `mode` field a `400` rather than a silent rehearsal (D28 at the edge), an empty `scope` is refused because it would govern nothing and fire never, and every write calls `invalidate` — asserted against an **hour-long** TTL so it cannot pass by expiry. There is no `PATCH`: that endpoint exists next door to carry a mode, and a DLP rule has none by construction. There is no `DELETE` underneath the `DELETE`, and here the argument is stronger than "history is evidence" — `docs/06 §9`'s mandatory-simulation gate is a query over observation history that *names a rule*, so a deleted rule is one whose rehearsal cannot be found. **The lockout analogue is real, and worse than conditional access's** — which was the substantive finding. `ActionScope::Any` governs administrative actions too, and `exactly` can name one outright; once a rule governs `admin.manage_policy`, three separate routes refuse the request and none of them needs the rule's conditions to hold: a refusing demand, an obliging demand (this surface can discharge none, so D29 refuses), and the one that needs no rule to fire at all — `RuleSet::evaluate` consults the facts only *after* deciding the action is governed, and an administrative call is made against the **tenant**, which has no content and therefore no facts, so `facts_unavailable: FAIL_CLOSED`, the default, refuses every administrative request in the tenant. There is no rehearsal to write it into (D28) and no session it decides differently for, because DLP conditions are about the resource rather than the principal: the rule that locks out its author locks out every administrator equally. So the check is structural — a rule may not *govern* the action that would withdraw it — and the test proves the harm rather than assuming it, by writing such a rule through the repository and watching `GET /admin/dlp/rules` answer `403`. `ENC-654` owns the better fix. Leakage rows `D10`–`D12`. Residues: `ENC-651`, `ENC-653`, `ENC-654` |

Trivial to close and worth doing before the milestone is called complete, since the matrix is what a reader consults to find out whether a criterion is demonstrated |
| ENC-649 | **`role_assignments` has no DDL, so a deployment has exactly one administrative role** | P2 | TODO | Found closing `ENC-619`. `docs/04 §9` specifies `role_definitions` with `permissions` as an array of action strings, and its `§2` inventory names `role_assignments`; `migrations/0004` says in as many words that the second has no DDL. So the only administrative grant a deployment can express is `users.is_admin`, which `ENC-619` reads as the *global administrator* holding every `AdminAction`. `AdminGrants` is a set precisely so that closing this is a change to one implementation of `AdminRoles` and to nothing else. **What the migration needs** — written here rather than in `migrations/`, which that task did not own and where `0022` is allocated elsewhere: `admin_role_assignments (tenant_id UUID NOT NULL, principal_type TEXT NOT NULL CHECK (principal_type IN ('USER','GROUP')), principal_id UUID NOT NULL, role TEXT NOT NULL, granted_by UUID NOT NULL, granted_at TIMESTAMPTZ NOT NULL DEFAULT now(), expires_at TIMESTAMPTZ, deleted_at TIMESTAMPTZ, PRIMARY KEY (tenant_id, principal_type, principal_id, role))`, with `role` in `docs/01 §4`'s vocabulary (`TENANT_ADMIN`, `IDENTITY_ADMIN`, `SECURITY_ADMIN`, `COMPLIANCE_ADMIN`, `AUDITOR`) as a `CHECK` so the database refuses an invented one; `tenant_id` first; RLS enabled **and** forced with the `app.tenant_id` policy; composite foreign keys `(tenant_id, principal_id)` onto `users`/`groups` and `(tenant_id, granted_by)` onto `users`, because referential integrity runs with row security deliberately not enforced (`docs/04 §3.3`); a partial unique index over live rows; and `SELECT, INSERT, UPDATE` to `enclave_app` with **no `DELETE`**, since revoking an administrator is evidence. The role-to-action mapping belongs in `crates/authorization` unless `role_definitions.permissions` is used, in which case its decoder must be closed for `ENC-615`'s reason. Group assignments want the transitive closure `crates/authorization/src/repo.rs` already computes |
| ENC-650 | An Auditor cannot read the audit log without also being able to rewrite the tenant's policy | P2 | TODO | Found closing `ENC-619`, and the sharpest consequence of `ENC-649`: `docs/01-PRD.md §4` has an Auditor whose whole persona is *read-only access to security, configuration and audit histories*, and `AdminAction::ReadAudit` exists for them — but the only grant a deployment can express is all-or-nothing, so the seeded `auditor` fixture holds nothing and the only way to give somebody the audit log is to give them `admin.manage_policy` as well. That is the least-privilege inversion the action vocabulary was split to avoid. Asserted rather than left to be discovered, in `crates/authorization/tests/admin_grants.rs` and `crates/api/tests/admin_authorization.rs`. Closes with `ENC-649` |
| ENC-651 | **An undecodable DLP rule puts a tenant's own repair surface inside its blast radius** | P2 | TODO | `ENC-623` one stage over, found closing `ENC-633` and asserted rather than papered over. `decode_rules` fails the whole set on one bad row so that a policy is never silently missing a refusal, and `TenantDlp` carries that to the caller — so while such a row is live every request in the tenant fails, `GET /api/v1/admin/dlp/rules` included. The list handler was written for exactly this case: it decodes each row individually and reports `decodes: false` with the clause named. It cannot be reached, because the chain runs first and its DLP stage fails on the same row. Both alternatives are worse than the bug — a route that skipped the stage is `CLAUDE.md` rule 1, and a stage that returned "no rules" on a decode failure is the permissive failure `ENC-615` exists to prevent — so the fix belongs in `crates/dlp`, and it is the same fix `ENC-623` needs: a way for a rule set to be *partially* usable by a read path that is explicitly repairing it |
| ENC-652 | An administrative action is not narrowed by the token's scope | P2 | TODO | Found closing `ENC-619`. `ScopeSet` narrows what a caller may do and never widens it, so a scope check on an administrative action would be a second *gate* rather than a second grant — the right shape, and one this deployment cannot express: no scope vocabulary for the `admin.*` actions exists, `SubjectFacts` resolves scopes from a provider that mints none of them, and requiring a scope nothing issues would close the surface again for a different reason. The consequence today is that a narrowly-scoped token belonging to an administrator still carries their full administrative rights — the shape `crates/auth/src/revocation.rs` already treats as privileged, since `admin:*` fails closed there. Needs the vocabulary decided first: `docs/03-LLD.md §5` names `admin:*` as *notation for a family* rather than as a list |
| ENC-653 | Reordering or retuning a DLP rule is a withdrawal and a rewrite, with a gap in between | P3 | TODO | Found closing `ENC-633`. A rule's scope, conditions, action and priority are deliberately not editable — changing what a rule refuses is a withdrawal and a new rule, so the text of what was in force during any period stays readable, which is the argument `docs/05 §14.1` already makes for conditional access. The cost is sharper for DLP because `priority` is *read*: changing which of two refusing rules a caller is shown means withdrawing and rewriting, the live-name unique index means the rewrite cannot reuse the name until the withdrawal commits, and in between the rule does not apply at all. A small window and a real gap; the honest fix is a transactional replace rather than an in-place edit |
| ENC-654 | **Content DLP is consulted for actions that have no content**, which is why `scope: ["any"]` is unwritable | P2 | TODO | Found closing `ENC-633`, and the design conversation behind that row's lockout check. `PolicyEngine::enforce` runs the DLP stage for every action including `admin.*`, and an administrative call is made against the tenant — which has no version, no facts and nothing to inspect. `RuleSet::evaluate` decides *governed* before it requires facts (`docs/06 §9.3`, correctly), so a rule that merely governs an administrative action is refused under `FAIL_CLOSED` without any rule firing. `ENC-633` closes the hole by refusing to store a rule whose scope reaches `admin.manage_policy`, which is narrow and effective and makes the documented `Any` scope unwritable through the API — a real cost, because *"notify security on any action over a document carrying a card number"* is a rule an administrator should be able to write. The better fix is one stage up: an action whose resource cannot carry content is not a content-inspection question at all, and a stage told so would refuse nothing rather than everything. That is a change to `crates/core` or `crates/dlp`, neither of which `ENC-633` owned, and the same question should be asked of the classification stage |
| ENC-507 | A crates.io outage fails whichever gate happens to be running, under that gate's name | P2 | DONE | Raised and worked 2026-08-20 with `ENC-503`, logged as its own row rather than absorbed (`§2.1` rule 5): same disease, different registry. `main` went red on `failed to get successful HTTP response from https://index.crates.io/... got 503` and it arrived as **`RULE: every tenant_id table is granted to enclave_app, which cannot bypass RLS`** — the sentence for a tenant-isolation defect, printed for a CDN having a bad minute. A check whose red state can mean either is one people re-run instead of read, and the run that gets waved through as flaky is eventually the real finding. Fixed by separating the fetch from the verdict: every cargo job in `structural-gates.yml`, `security.yml` and CI's two security-asserting jobs now runs `cargo fetch --locked` in a step of its own, retried four times with quadratic backoff (~70 s), which says *"Dependency registry unavailable — NOT a gate failure"* and *"nothing was asserted and no rule was broken"* when it gives up. It works because that fetch is the only crates.io traffic in these jobs — afterwards the assertion resolves from `Cargo.lock` and the local cache and touches no network, so an outage can only fail the step that names the registry. **Only the fetch is retried, and the comment at each site says so**: three attempts at "is EICAR quarantined" passes on the run where the answer was no, once. Two `Explain on failure` steps were re-conditioned on the gate step rather than on the job, because a fetch failure was otherwise printing a full page about which security rule had been broken; the pending gates skip the fetch entirely via `hashFiles`; and the scheduled-scan P0 issue template now says to read which step failed before triaging an advisory that may not exist |

**Accepted risk, `ENC-138`:** `RUSTSEC-2026-0253` — unsoundness in `lru`, reached transitively
through `aws-sdk-s3`. `LruCache::pop()` is not panic-safe. Accepted because there is nowhere to
move to: `lru 0.16.4` is the latest release, the advisory names no patched version, and the SDK pins
it. Suppressed in `.cargo/audit.toml` with its reason and its expiry condition — it ends when
`aws-sdk-s3` bumps past the fix, and `cargo audit` will say so on the first build after that. An
acceptance, not a resolution.

**Phase 1 (MVP) is feature-complete.** Content enters through a scanned, versioned, immutable path
and leaves through a policy-gated one. 922 tests, 8 routes, every handler proven to reach the chain.

**Next:** **M3 — Discovery**, plan at [`plans/M3-DISCOVERY.md`](plans/M3-DISCOVERY.md). **M2 is closed** — all five exit criteria demonstrated, definition of done met, assessment in [`plans/M2-CLOSEOUT.md`](plans/M2-CLOSEOUT.md). No gate falls here: `ROADMAP.md §6` puts G1 at the end of M5.

M3 carries the highest-severity design risk in the product, and the plan is arranged around one sentence — the vector index is a candidate generator, PostgreSQL is the authority. Two decisions in it exist because of what M2 measured: both disclosure levels resolve in **one** call (D20), and over-fetch is the cheap side of the trade (D21). `docs/07 §6.2` describes a two-pass design that predates the measurement and needs correcting before it is copied.

**Correction (`ENC-142`).** This line previously read *"Next: gate G1 — ship the MVP?"*, and
`plans/M1-CONTENT-CORE.md §5` said the same. Both were wrong: `ROADMAP.md §6` places **G1 at the end
of M5**, with M2, M3 and M4 in between. The error originated in the M1 plan and was copied here, which
is exactly the failure mode `plans/README.md` warns about — a plan restating a roadmap commitment
instead of referring to it. Had it stood, M1 would have been assessed against the MVP's ship criteria
four milestones early, and the four milestones of work those criteria assume would have been read as
missing rather than as not yet due. Both documents now point at the roadmap.

**Follow-up worth doing first (`ENC-137`):** `Cursor`, `PageSize`, `FilterFingerprint` and
`normalize_slug` live in `enclave-identity` and are now used by four crates. They are a security
primitive — a cursor is signed and bound to a tenant and filter set — and they belong below the
domain layer, not inside one domain crate that others reach sideways into. Flagged by the
implementing session rather than found in review.

**M0 is now fully closed.** Exit criterion 1 — one request traversing login → JWT → `enforce` →
tenant-scoped query → audit row — is demonstrated by `crates/api/tests/me.rs`, not asserted.

> **ENC-116 — decided 2026-08-18 by the repo owner: option (c), accept.** The racy `CREATE ROLE`
> stays in migration 0001. It is not amended, so the forward-only rule and the gate that enforces it
> remain intact with no exception carved into either.
>
> The risk is accepted on the basis that role provisioning is a deployment concern: roles are created
> before any migration runs — `deploy/compose/init/01-roles.sql` locally, the credential provisioning
> step in production (`docs/11-OPERATIONS.md §12`) — which makes 0001's guard a no-op. Verified at
> 0 failures in 10 stress runs where it previously failed 10 out of 10.
>
> **The residual risk, stated plainly so it is not rediscovered as a surprise:** anyone who migrates
> into a cluster where the roles were never provisioned can still hit the race, and the symptom is an
> opaque `unique_violation` on `pg_authid_rolname_index` during startup. `docs/11-OPERATIONS.md §12`
> documents the provisioning step as a requirement rather than a suggestion, which is what makes this
> acceptance defensible rather than merely convenient. Revisit if migrations ever create another
> cluster-wide object.

> **The original framing, kept for the decision record.** The clean fix is to catch `duplicate_object` **and**
> `unique_violation` in migration 0001 — but 0001 is merged, and migrations are forward-only
> (`CLAUDE.md`), which the structural gate enforces with no escape hatch. A later migration cannot
> repair it: 0001 runs first and fails before anything else executes. So the options are (a) amend
> 0001 and grant the gate a narrow, reviewable pre-release exception, (b) move role creation out of
> migrations entirely into deployment provisioning — which is where 0001's own comments say
> credentials come from — or (c) accept it, since production role provisioning is a deployment
> concern anyway. My recommendation is (b): migrations arguably should not be creating cluster-wide
> roles at all. Not decided unilaterally, because it touches a control.

> **Branch protection is unavailable on this plan — closed, not deferred.** Both
> `repos/CasualOffice/enclave/branches/main/protection` and `/rulesets` return
> `403 Upgrade to GitHub Pro or make this repository public`. The repository is private under an
> organization account, so required status checks cannot be enforced server-side without either
> paying for Pro or making the code public. Neither is mine to decide.
>
> This matters because it was the mitigation proposed twice after `main` went red: PR #10 and PR #12
> were both merged while their checks were still running, and both landed a known failure. Until the
> plan changes, that remains a process control rather than a technical one — **wait for green before
> merging**. Every gate is wired to block *in CI* (`structural-gates-status` requires all eleven);
> what is missing is GitHub refusing the merge button, not the signal.

> **Deviation from §2.1, recorded deliberately.** The repo owner directed parallel execution of the
> M0 foundation crates on 2026-08-18. Seven items are in flight at once rather than one. This is
> sound here only because the tasks touch disjoint directories and share no files, and because an
> integration step (`cargo check`/`clippy`/`test` across the workspace, by one person) follows before
> anything is marked `DONE`. It is not the new default: the rule resumes at `ENC-106`.

**Paused / blocked:** none.

**Open P0s:** none.

| ENC-662 | The unlabelled-classification policy is per tenant, and the worker has only the deployment-wide default | P2 | TODO | Found wiring `ENC-661`. `VectorStage` takes a `PgClassification`, which takes a `ClassificationPolicy`, which is a **tenant** setting — `Unlabelled::FailClosed` or `Unlabelled::Assume(rank)` — and `crates/worker/src/main.rs` is a deployment-wide composition root with no tenant in hand. So it passes `ClassificationPolicy::fail_closed()` for every tenant, and a tenant that configured `ASSUME_RANK` has nowhere to say so: its unlabelled documents are not embedded rather than embedded at the rank it nominated. That is the safe direction and it is not the configured one, which is the distinction `ENC-574` built `ClassificationOutcome::Assumed` to keep visible. The fix is not a config key — the value is per tenant, so it belongs in tenant settings and has to be read inside the pass, per tenant, the way `dlp.facts_unavailable` is. `UnclassifiedFiles` and the `FAIL_CLOSED` path are unaffected; what is missing is only the `Assume` arm reaching a real deployment |
| ENC-663 | Nothing fills the sparse vector, so `bge-m3` was half-chosen | P2 | TODO | Named in `crates/worker/src/indexing.rs` before `ENC-661` and now concrete. `ACTIVE.sparse` records that `bge-m3` emits learned-sparse vectors natively from the same forward pass, and that was **one of the two reasons it was chosen** (`crates/embeddings/src/model.rs`) — the collection has had a `SPARSE_INVERTED_INDEX` field since `ENC-523` and nothing has ever written to it. `EmbeddingProvider::embed` returns `Vec<Embedding>`, which is dense only, so `MountedModel` computes the CLS state and discards the sparse head the graph could produce. Hybrid retrieval therefore runs on the dense side alone: a recall difference and not a correctness one, which is why it is P2 and why `SparseTerms::new()` is honest rather than a stub. It is a gap in the **port**, not in the provider — widening the return type is what makes it expressible — and it wants doing before a corpus is embedded, because filling the field later is a reindex |
| ENC-664 | A panicking tokenizer is retried forever, because the error type has no permanent arm | P3 | TODO | Found writing `MountedModel::embed`. `crates/embeddings/src/error.rs` states, deliberately, that there is no such thing as text that will not embed — every variant is transient, because a permanent one is a variant somebody maps to *give up on this document*, which is the silently unfindable file the crate is arranged against. The cost is stated here rather than hidden: a panic inside `tokenizers` or `rten` on particular bytes is **deterministic**, so mapping it to `EmbeddingError::Provider` means indexing retries it, panics again, and the file consumes its `attempts` budget until a supervisor escalates it. `crates/indexing/src/ocr.rs` faces the identical question and answers it the other way — a parser that panics has made a statement about the document, so it records `Refused` — and the two crates disagree on purpose, because an OCR refusal is a manifest state somebody reads and an embedding refusal would be a document that is filed, visible and unfindable. P3 because no such document is known to exist; the shape to fix it is probably the `attempts` budget quarantining the version, not a new error variant |
| ENC-725 | `GET /api/v1/bootstrap` — the first request a client makes, split so that the authenticated half is unreachable without a verified context | P1 | DONE | The handler arrived on `feat/enc-725-bootstrap-and-dependencies` as 609 lines **registered on no router** — the eleventh instance of the shape M5a exists to end — and its module documentation cited `crates/api/tests/bootstrap.rs` three times in the present tense as the thing that proved its central claim. That file did not exist. Both halves are now true: the route is registered in `crates/api/src/lib.rs`, probed on **both** credentials by `crates/api/tests/reachability.rs` against the composed binary, and covered by five DB-backed tests that are `docs/12 §4.13` N1, N2, N5 and N6. Every one was watched to fail by name. The sharpest: deleting `WHERE id = $1` from the `tenants` read served **beta's caller alpha's row** — `tenants` carries no `tenant_id`, so `migrations/0002` gives it no policy and row-level security is not the second layer there. One doc correction went with it: the module claimed `enclave_db::resolve_routed_tenant` does not exist. It does (`ENC-685`), so host-independence stopped being an inevitability and became a decision — argued in the module and held by N2 |
| ENC-726 | `GET /health/dependencies` — unauthenticated summary, authenticated detail | P1 | DONE | Same defect as `ENC-725` and the same fix: the handler existed and no router registered it. Now on the router beside `/health/live` and `/health/ready` — outside `/api/v1`, because an operator's probe configuration names `/health/**` — probed on both credentials by `crates/api/tests/reachability.rs`, and covered by four DB-backed tests (`docs/12 §4.13` N3, N4, N5). The summary's key set is asserted **equal** to `["status"]`, not merely lacking `dependencies`: a count or an elided name list leaks the deployment's shape just as surely. The topology scan takes its needles from the DSN the harness handed the pool and asserts they are in that DSN first, because a search for strings this deployment never had proves nothing — broken by adding an `endpoint` field to `Dependency`, which failed naming the host. Two gaps left open and named rather than implied: `ENC-860` (the `Health::Degraded` arm and `health::reason` have no test anywhere) and `ENC-729`, unchanged |
| ENC-727 | Bootstrap serves no branding, because there is no branding store | P2 | TODO | `crates/branding` is five lines of module doc and `tenants.branding` is an unread `JSONB` default. `docs/09 §18`'s contract needs `ENC-310`; until then the field is **absent** rather than a placeholder, because a placeholder reads as configured |
| ENC-728 | Bootstrap emits no feature flags, because none can be answered honestly from the handler | P2 | TODO | Two reasons, and both have to go: the crates behind the plausible flags (`sync`, `workflows`, `signing`, `search`) are stubs, and `ApiState` carries no `Config`, so the handler cannot read what a deployment configured. `features: {}` would read as "nothing is on"; an absent key reads as "not answered yet" |
| ENC-729 | `/health/dependencies` probes PostgreSQL and nothing else | P2 | TODO | The API process holds a handle to the pool and to `BlobStore`, and to no other dependency. Milvus, Redis, NATS, antivirus and SMTP are reported `unknown` with `evidence: none` — never omitted, because an absent dependency reads as a healthy one. Closing this means a probe port, not a wider handler |
| ENC-730 | The unauthenticated half of bootstrap carries nothing tenant-derived — now a decision rather than a consequence | P1 | DONE | **This row's premise was false when it was written.** It said `enclave_db::resolve_routed_tenant` and the `RoutedTenant` extractor do not exist in this tree; both have existed since `ENC-685` and `POST /auth/login` uses them. The handler's module header repeated the same claim. Corrected in place rather than deleted, because what the claim was justifying survives and only its status changed: the public half stays host-independent **by choice**, since a response that varied by `Host` is a tenant-enumeration oracle for a caller holding nothing (`CLAUDE.md` rule 3). Closed because the decision it asked for has been taken and is now checkable — `docs/12 §4.13` N2 compares alpha's host, beta's host and an unrouted host byte for byte, with an `Accept-Language` control proving the comparison can tell two answers apart. What is genuinely left is a product question, not a wiring gap: a per-tenant sign-in page cannot be branded without publishing something per tenant. That belongs with `ENC-727`'s branding store |
| ENC-876 | **`main` does not compile with `--all-targets`**, so `clippy`, `test` and every gate downstream of them are red on it | P0 | DONE | Found merging `origin/main` into `ENC-725`'s branch, and it is not a merge artefact — it reproduces on `bc2ca71` alone. Two PRs that were each green on their own: `f046123` (`ENC-806`) added `crates/worker/tests/upload_reaper.rs`, which destructures `UploadTarget::Single { url }`, and `380c1ab` (`ENC-820`) gave that variant a second field, `required_headers`. Neither branch contained the other's file, so neither build could see it; `main` merged them in sequence and nothing re-ran. `E0027`, one pattern, and it takes down `cargo test --workspace` for every session on the machine. Fixed by destructuring the field and **asserting it empty** rather than ignoring it with `..`: the test's own comment says it sends a bare `PUT` and would get a `400` if any header were signed into the URL, and `ENC-820` is exactly the change that starts signing one. Ignoring the field would compile and would leave that comment one `with_checksum` away from being false with no test to say so. Preempted `ENC-725` under `§2.1`, which is what a P0 is for; the interrupted work was two minutes of gate-running and resumed immediately. **The process finding is the merge queue, not the code**: the tracker's own §3 note says two PRs were merged while their checks were still running. This pair passed their checks and still broke `main`, because a semver-compatible field addition is only a compile error in a file the other branch does not have. That is what a merge queue that rebuilds the *merge result* prevents, and `merge_group` is already in `structural-gates.yml`'s triggers | *(Renumbered from `ENC-863`: two parallel sessions allocated that ID to different work on the same evening. Renumbered rather than merged — they are unrelated — and recorded because a reader who meets `ENC-863` in a commit message needs to find it.)*
| ENC-873 | `health::reason` and the degraded verdict have no test anywhere | P2 | TODO | Numbered from `ENC-860` rather than the next free number, because these three were opened as `ENC-849`–`ENC-851` and `ENC-849` was allocated to a different row on `main` while the work was in flight; `§2.3` says a gap costs nothing and a reused number costs the reader. Found writing `ENC-726`'s tests, and named in `crates/api/tests/dependencies.rs` rather than left implied — documentation in the present tense about tests that do not exist is the exact defect `ENC-725` was sent to fix. `crates/api/src/health.rs` carries no `#[cfg(test)]` module, and neither integration test can produce a `Health::Degraded`: making PostgreSQL stop answering mid-test means reaching through the pool the assertions themselves share. So the `match` on the `DbError` variant — the single thing standing between `sqlx::Error`'s `Display`, which carries the connection target, and the caller — is unexercised. It needs no database: a unit test constructing each `DbError` and asserting the returned code covers the arm an outage actually reaches | *(Renumbered from `ENC-860`: two parallel sessions allocated that ID to different work on the same evening. Renumbered rather than merged — they are unrelated — and recorded because a reader who meets `ENC-860` in a commit message needs to find it.)*
| ENC-874 | The `Accept-Language` negotiator is documented in detail and tested by one header | P2 | TODO | `crates/api/src/routes/bootstrap.rs` argues four specific behaviours for `best_accepted` and `parse_weight` — a missing `q` is 1 and ties keep source order, `q=0` means *not acceptable* and must fall through to `fallback` rather than to English, `*` matches the platform fallback rather than the first supported entry, and a malformed weight makes the range unusable rather than maximally preferred. Every one of them is attacker-supplied input parsed by hand, and none has a test: `ENC-725`'s integration test sends `en-GB` and that is all. The functions are pure and take no database, so this is a `#[cfg(test)]` module in the same file, not an integration test | *(Renumbered from `ENC-861`: two parallel sessions allocated that ID to different work on the same evening. | ENC-875 | `Session`'s constructor is not as unforgeable as its own documentation claims | P3 | TODO | Found by breaking `ENC-725`'s split on purpose. `crates/api/src/routes/bootstrap.rs` argues that `Session::observed` takes a `&RequestContext`, *"a value produced by exactly one thing in this crate, `Authenticated`"* — but `enclave_core::RequestContext::system(tenant_id)` is public, so any handler in this crate can conjure one and build a `Session` on an anonymous path. The break did exactly that in three lines. This is a claim that is weaker than it reads, not a live defect: nothing does it, and `docs/12 §4.13` N1 catches it if anything starts to. P3 because the honest fixes are both larger than the problem — a sealed marker on the context, or narrowing `system()`'s visibility, which background work legitimately needs | *(Renumbered from `ENC-862`: two parallel sessions allocated that ID to different work on the same evening. | ENC-731 | `crates/sync`: the device registry, the delta cursor and the eligibility model | P1 | DONE | `crates/sync/src/lib.rs` was five lines of module documentation. It is now the model `docs/10-SYNC-AND-EDITING.md` describes: `Eligibility` is `§5`'s six conditions as a struct with one **required** field each, so a verdict cannot be assembled having forgotten one — `download_allowed` and `sync_allowed` are two of those fields and never one, which is `CLAUDE.md` rule 6 held by the type rather than by a comment. The decision worth reading is the one `docs/10 §4` does not make: an ineligible file becomes a `TOMBSTONE` **only if the caller may `file.metadata_read` it**, and is otherwise omitted entirely. A tombstone carries a path, so tombstoning everything would hand a caller with no grant the complete contents listing of a library they may not browse — the same reasoning `crates/api/src/content.rs` gives for its silent listing trim. `docs/10 §4`'s sentence is about the file that *disappeared from a device*, and is honoured for that |
| ENC-732 | `migrations/0023_sync_devices.sql`: the registry, the per-scope change feed and the cursors into it | P1 | DONE | `docs/04 §15` models `sync_devices` and `sync_cursors` and documents `cursor` as *"a monotonic change sequence"* — which nothing in the schema produced. Two further tables now do. **`seq` is a transactional counter row, not a `SEQUENCE` and not a timestamp**, and that is the whole design: the incrementing `UPDATE` holds a row lock to commit, so allocation order is *commit* order and the visible set is always a contiguous prefix. `nextval` neither locks nor rolls back, so two writers can take 5 and 6 and commit in the other order — after which a reader that saw 6 stores 6 and never sees 5, and the file is missing from that device for ever with every row in the database correct. A `modified_at` cursor fails identically one layer up. The cost, per-scope write serialisation, is recorded rather than discovered, and is why a scope is a library. The feed is appended by a **trigger on `files`**, so no future writer can forget to; `enclave_app` holds `DELETE` on `sync_change_log` alone, because deleting a counter row restarts a scope at 1 and every device above that cursor stops syncing silently and permanently. `docs/04` §15.1 records it; version 1.9 |
| ENC-733 | The four sync endpoints of `docs/05-API.md §13` | P1 | DONE | `GET /sync/delta`, `GET|POST /sync/devices`, `POST /sync/devices/{id}/wipe`, `POST /sync/reserve`. `delta` runs the container gate through the whole chain, then asks the authorization stage three **separate** batched questions per page — `metadata_read`, `download`, `sync` — which is where rule 6 is decided per file; `reserve` runs the full chain for `file.sync` *before* `file.edit`, so a caller who may edit in the browser cannot push from a device the tenant has not permitted to hold a copy. `410 CURSOR_TOO_OLD` and `409 CONFLICT { currentVersionId }` are rendered envelopes, because `enclave_core::Error`'s status comes from its variant and neither exists. `reserve` claims an ordinary `enclave_uploads` session rather than a sync-only one — `docs/10 §2`'s *"it gets no privileged endpoint"* held structurally |
| ENC-734 | The sync leakage-matrix rows and the tests behind them | P1 | DONE | `docs/12 §4.7` Y8–Y14. Twenty-four DB-backed tests. The one the work exists for is `a_caller_who_may_download_but_not_sync_is_refused_the_content`: one caller, one request, two files, a single `DENY` on `file.sync`, asserted on the verdict, on the payload (no `versionId`, no `checksumSha256`) and against the positive control. Every meaningful assertion was watched to fail by name (`docs/12 §1.2`) — and one break **failed nothing**: removing `cl.tenant_id = $1` from the feed's window left `one_tenants_feed_is_invisible_to_another` green, because row-level security holds that property alone. That is the seventh time in this repository. The test now says which layer it proves, and `a_caller_in_the_same_tenant_without_the_grant_is_not_found` is the one that proves the authorization stage, where RLS has nothing to say |
| ENC-735 | A delta cursor is not the workspace's pagination `Cursor`, and the divergence is deliberate | P3 | TODO | Recorded so it is not read as an oversight. `enclave_db::Cursor` binds a position to a tenant and a **filter fingerprint**, which is right for a listing and wrong here: a delta cursor is persisted by a client for weeks and must survive being written down and presented after a release, `docs/10 §4` puts it on the wire as the bare integer `"8841205"`, and there is no filter set to disagree about — a delta is the whole scope, always. What a signed opaque cursor would add is integrity against tampering, and a tampered delta cursor can only move a caller within a scope whose every entry is eligibility-evaluated for them anyway. Revisit if a scope ever gains a filter |
| ENC-736 | `devices` and `sync_devices` are two registries for one machine; no token is bound to either | P1 | TODO | `docs/10 §3` and `docs/03-LLD.md §5.2` require a `dev` claim on every sync token, so a token stolen from one device cannot be replayed from another. `migrations/0001`'s `devices` is the table that binds it and **nothing writes it**; `crates/api/src/auth.rs` builds every `DeviceContext` with `device_id: None` and `DevicePosture::Unknown`. `ENC-732` created `sync_devices` for the Delivery-domain registry `docs/04 §15` lists rather than writing into an Auth-domain table `crates/auth` owns. Three consequences, all live: `crates/api/src/sync.rs` takes the device from a `deviceId` parameter it checks belongs to the caller, so one of a user's own devices can impersonate another; a wipe cannot revoke a refresh family, because nothing ties one to a device; and registration accepts no `publicKey`, deliberately — a key stored and never verified reads as a control and is not one. Also blocks the device endpoints in the shipped binary: `SelfServiceAuthorization` permits a principal to read itself, so `GET /sync/devices` works and `POST` and the wipe are refused until an identity-authorization model exists |
| ENC-737 | An ACL revocation that does not touch `files` produces no delta entry, so no tombstone | P2 | TODO | The change feed is appended by a trigger on `files`, which covers creation, rename, move, a new current version and a permission **break** (which bumps `files.acl_revision`). It does not cover an `acl_entries` row withdrawn on a file that inherits — nothing writes `files`, so nothing appends, and `docs/10 §4`'s *"a file the user lost access to appears as a `TOMBSTONE` with a reason, not as an omission"* is not met for that case: the file simply stops appearing. It is caught one step later — `docs/10 §5` re-evaluates eligibility when the client asks for bytes and returns `SYNC_NOT_PERMITTED`, at which point the client tombstones it — so nothing leaks; what is lost is the *promptness* and the reason. The fix is a second change source keyed on `acl_entries`, which needs care: one `EVERYONE` withdrawal on a library is a fan-out of one entry per file in it |

The read path already refuses a cursor that has fallen off the window, so the *behaviour* is correct before the pruner exists — which is why this is P2 and not P1 — but a deployment that runs for a year has a feed table nobody trims |

| ENC-740 | Delegation is bounded in the schema, not only in the handler | P1 | REVIEW | Part of `ENC-739`, recorded separately because it is the privilege-escalation surface the task turns on. `workflow_instances.delegation` has a two-value vocabulary — `FORBIDDEN` and `ONCE` — and **no value that means an onward chain**, so an unbounded delegate chain is unstorable on every path including `psql`. The transfer is a single `UPDATE … WHERE delegated_to IS NULL`, so a concurrent second delegation loses in the database rather than in a read-then-write. The delegate must independently pass authorization for the same action on the same resource at the moment authority is offered *and* again when they act, because a check made only at transfer time turns the step into a stored capability that survives revocation |
