Both handlers call one `plan_start`, so the policy chain, the definition decode, the scope check and every refusal are literally the same code; they differ only in whether the returned `Plan` is applied or described |

| ENC-743 | A stale version cannot be approved (`docs/15 §12` W3) | P2 | TODO | Held at decision time rather than by a sweep: `/approve` and `/reject` compare the instance's `version_id` against the file's `current_version_id` and expire the instance when they differ, unless the definition pinned `on_new_version = CONTINUE`. That is the point where the property matters — an approval over content that is no longer current — and it needs no other crate. What is *not* built is the proactive half: `crates/versions` does not notify workflows on commit, so an in-flight instance stays `RUNNING` in the listing until somebody tries to act on it. The row is P2 because the gate holds the security property and the sweep only improves what the inbox shows |

Group assignment wants the transitive closure that lives in `crates/authorization`, fanned out into one step row per resolved member at instantiation, which is a change to the evaluator and to the schema together |

And `docs/15 §4`'s rework branch is not implemented — a rejection terminates the instance, which is the section's default and the direction that cannot over-permit |

Without it a tampered row turns the preview path into the download path with a `Content-Type` of `image/png`, which is rule 6's collapse arriving through the cache rather than through the handler. Object storage has no row-level security, so this is where the equivalent check happens |

So `NoRenditionSink` is what every deployment runs: the artefact is served and dropped, and **no `renditions` row is recorded**, because a row is a claim that bytes are at a key and the next request would fetch an object nobody wrote — a cache entry that fails for the life of a generator. The cost is a re-render per request, bounded by the same `RenderBudget`; the fix is a `put` on the trait (or a rendition-store port beside it), which is `crates/storage`'s decision and not one to take from inside `crates/preview`. `migrations/0007`, `repo::record` and `repo::find` are all in place and exercised — what is missing is the verb underneath them |

Four of the five already exist in `crates/worker`; none exists in `crates/scheduler`, whose one loop runs unconditionally and reports nothing about what it is or is not doing, which is precisely the mechanism whose absence let this survive five milestones. Verified by hand against the running binary twice: once over the development database (2 expired + 1 stranded released, objects gone, rows `EXPIRED`) and once after a genuine sign-in-and-upload through `enclave-api`, whose object the same sweep left untouched. Logged rather than absorbed: `ENC-837`–`ENC-840` |

| ENC-816 | **The upload flow, all three requests, with truthful progress** | P1 | DONE | `POST /uploads` → a `PUT` of the bytes **straight to object storage** → `POST /uploads/{id}/complete` with the size and a client-computed SHA-256. | ENC-817 | The peek panel's Preview tab reads real bytes, and tells four outcomes apart | P1 | DONE | `GET /files/{id}/preview` answers PNG bytes and the panel renders them — verified in a browser, `data-state=ready` with a real `<img>`. The work is in the *other* three answers. **404 is not "missing"**: rule 9 refuses to serve a version that is not readable, and rule 7 makes that a `404` rather than a `403`, so a file the user is looking at in the list answers 404 here whenever its scan has not cleared. Rendering that as not-found would be false and alarming, and rendering it as a retryable error would be worse, because the retry cannot succeed until a scanner runs. It earned its place immediately: the upload queue was written into `features/upload/` and read by `features/libraries/`, which is precisely the import it refuses, and the fix `§2` prescribes — move the shared thing down — put it in `entities/upload/`. | ENC-820 | **`complete` did not verify the client's checksum against the store at all** | P2 | DONE | Reproduced by hand before anything was changed: `POST /uploads` with `sha256` of all zeroes, a real 43-byte `PUT`, `complete` → **`202`**, and `0000…` on `file_versions.checksum_sha256` beside the object's true digest `05c6e08f…`. Two halves were missing and only one was known. `create_upload` never put the declared digest on the pre-signed `PUT`, so `x-amz-checksum-sha256` was not in `X-Amz-SignedHeaders` and the provider had nothing to compare the body against; and `head` never asked for `ChecksumMode::ENABLED`, without which S3 and MinIO omit `x-amz-checksum-*` from a `HeadObject` response **even for an object they did verify** — so fixing only the first would have changed nothing observable. Both are fixed, and the same request is now refused by MinIO itself (`400 XAmzContentChecksumMismatch`) with nothing stored; the honest digest still answers `202` and persists `05c6e08f…`. **`ChecksumEvidence` is deleted rather than acted on.** Its `ClientDeclared` variant was honest and read by exactly one thing in the workspace — a log field — so the fix is not to add a consumer but to remove the state: `VerifiedContent` now exists *only* when the provider confirmed the digest, which is a fact about the type rather than a field a caller can forget to check. `verify` returns the new `FailureReason::ChecksumUnconfirmed`, a **`503` and not a `400`** — nothing the client sent is wrong when a backend fails to produce a digest it was asked for — and the session is still persisted `FAILED`, because retrying against the same store cannot work. `sha256` is now **required** on `POST /uploads` and on `POST /sync/reserve`, since a session created without one cannot be verified at all. The one refusal that is a product cost is `ENC-829`: an upload above the 16 MiB multipart threshold is refused, because a multipart checksum is a checksum of the part checksums and MinIO rejects AWS's `FULL_OBJECT` type (`InvalidArgument`, probed against `RELEASE.2025-04-22`). Refused at `create_upload`, before a URL exists, so no bandwidth is spent. **Nothing weakens if a client ignores the new `requiredHeaders`** — the headers are signed, so a `PUT` that drops one fails the signature check. Proved against real MinIO in `a_lying_client_cannot_store_an_object_under_a_digest_it_declared`, four assertions with the honest client as the control; a mock cannot prove this, because the whole defect was about what a real provider does. `ENC-821` closed alongside it: `content-type` is signed too and is now reported |
| ENC-821 | The pre-signed PUT signs `content-type`, and a mismatch reads as a permission failure | P2 | DONE | Cost two attempts to find. The client threads one `mimeType` value through both calls so the two cannot drift. Recorded because the next person to write a client against this API will hit it, and nothing in `docs/05` mentions it |

The same class of invented fact as a guessed capability, on a surface where the classification badge is how a user knows whether a document may leave the building. Both cells are now empty for a folder, and it gets a folder glyph rather than a page one |
| ENC-823 | `crypto.subtle` has no streaming digest, so a large upload is held whole in memory | P3 | TODO | The Web Crypto API has no `update`/`final` pair, so the chunked read in `entities/upload/digest.ts` buys the *read* and not the digest: the whole buffer still reaches `crypto.subtle.digest` once. Closed with `avStatus` and `isReadable` on a shared `VersionState`. **`isReadable` is rendered from `FileVersion::is_readable`, not recomputed**: that function is the Rust twin of the `READABLE_PREDICATE` the delivery query splices, so the two endpoints cannot drift while both read the one definition — a unit test asserts both render sites call it and that rule 9 is spelled out nowhere in the module. **Both fields, not one**, and the argument is `docs/09 §8`: a boolean says *no* without saying *why*, and the ladder needs `Scanning` and `Quarantined` told apart from `Processing`. | ENC-826 | **`GET /uploads/{id}` reports `SCANNING` forever after the worker publishes the version** | P2 | DONE | The session's phase machine *ends* at `SCANNING` — `Session<Scanning>` has no transition and `Processing`/`Available`/`Quarantined` are deliberately not phases in `enclave_uploads` — so everything after the handoff happens to the version and `state` could never report the end of an upload. `ProgressView` now carries that version, as the **same `VersionState`** `GET /files/{id}` returns, plus a `fileId` that is populated for new-file uploads (it comes off the *committed row*, never the staged key, which is `ENC-691`'s lesson: read off the key it is a promise about a row nothing has written). **Derived at read time rather than written by the antivirus pass**, and the argument is not merely that the worker was out of scope: a written copy is a second mutable copy of a fact `file_versions` already owns, it is reaped with the session while the version is permanent, and it would require `PROCESSING`/`AVAILABLE` to become phases in the sealed machine whose whole purpose is that they are not — a large hole to open in rule 9 for a status field. `state` therefore still says `SCANNING`, which is true of the *session*, and the wire names its subject rather than overloading one field across two rows with two owners and two lifetimes. Looked up by the two ids the staged key reserved, so it reports *this* upload's version and not whatever the file points at now |
| ENC-827 | The client does not implement multipart upload | P3 | TODO | `POST /uploads` issues `MULTIPART` with a `urls` array for a large enough file, and the client implements only `SINGLE`. It says so — the row fails with `upload_multipart_unsupported` — rather than sending the whole body to the first part's URL, which would store a corrupt object and pass `complete`'s size check only by accident. Needs the per-part `PUT` loop and the ETag report |
| ENC-828 | With `antivirus.provider: none`, **nothing uploaded to a deployment is ever readable** | P2 | DONE | The row read this as rule 9 working correctly and wanted a sentence in the config template. Half of it was: under the default `BLOCK` every version is `QUARANTINED`/`SKIPPED` and refusing it is right. The other half was a defect. `ALLOW_WITH_FLAG` produces `AVAILABLE`/`SKIPPED` — a version `docs/06 §6.2`'s policy **deliberately published** — and `READABLE_PREDICATE` refused it anyway, so the setting was a no-op with a misleading name and the only workable configuration on a machine with no scanner made the product a **write-only store**. Reproduced against the running binaries before touching anything: upload → `202 SCANNING` → `AVAILABLE`/`SKIPPED` → `GET /files/{id}` `isReadable: false`, `GET /files/{id}/preview` `404`, `POST .../download` `404`. **The fix is one word of SQL and three days of argument about whether it is allowed.** The predicate becomes `status = 'AVAILABLE' AND av_status IN ('CLEAN','SKIPPED')`, and what makes that sound is that `status` carries the *policy decision* while `av_status` carries the *verdict*: `AVAILABLE` is written by exactly one statement in the workspace — `RECORD_SQL` in `crates/worker/src/antivirus.rs`, from `Target::of` — and only for `VersionDisposition::Publish`, so the pair cannot arise except from a tenant writing `ALLOW_WITH_FLAG` down. Every writer of `av_status` was enumerated to check that, not just `decide`. **Rule 9 survives literally**: the predicate still requires `AVAILABLE`, `SCANNING` is still refused for every verdict, and `AVAILABLE`/`PENDING` — a scan that has not *completed*, which is the clause's own words — stays refused, so `ENC-646`'s `ALLOW_AND_RESCAN` half is still open. `SKIPPED` is never rewritten to `CLEAN`; that one-word version fails `no_verdict_other_than_clean_ever_records_av_status_clean`, and it is wrong for the three reasons `disabled.rs` gives. The `CONFIDENTIAL` ceiling is untouched and now has a test at the *write site* rather than only over `decide`. **The scope was four spellings, not two.** The row named `READABLE_PREDICATE` and `is_readable`; the workspace also had `enclave_preview::repo`'s hand-written copy (which gates preview, thumbnail, export, indexing and the DLP scan) and a retyped literal in `crates/sync`. Changing only the first is not a smaller fix, it is `ENC-825` reintroduced — the first build answered `isReadable: true` beside a `404` from the preview route, in the same run. `crates/preview` **cannot** import the definition (`enclave-versions` → `enclave-indexing` → `enclave-preview` makes it a cycle), so the two texts are held equal by a test in `crates/worker`, the one crate that sees both; `crates/sync` now splices the exported macro instead of retyping it. **Nine deliberate breaks, each watched to fail by name.** The sharpest: reverting the preview copy alone gives *"AVAILABLE/SKIPPED: GET /files/{id} says isReadable=true and GET /files/{id}/preview answered 404 Not Found"* — the defect quoted back. The cross-product test is the one that matters, because two hand-picked cases are exactly what let this live four milestones: it sweeps all thirty `status` × `av_status` pairs against a real PostgreSQL with the predicate spliced from the export, and its *control* — the servable set asserted by name — is what catches both halves being widened wrongly together, which the agreement assertion cannot. The HTTP sweep requires a `200` carrying PNG bytes, never merely not-404. Verified end to end after, against the merged tree with `ENC-820`'s signed checksum header: preview `200`, 95 bytes of PNG through MinIO, download `200`, and `SCANNING`/`AVAILABLE`+`PENDING` still `404`. `BLOCK` re-verified as still refusing everything. Both binaries now say what the configuration means at start-up — `enclave-api` had said nothing at all, and it is the process serving the 404s. Leakage row **`G16`** added; `G1`, `G7` and `G11` reworded, and `G7`/`G11` are real changes rather than tidying — the indexer and the DLP scan now read `ALLOW_WITH_FLAG` content. Logged rather than absorbed: `ENC-850`, `ENC-851`, `ENC-852` |
| ENC-837 | `upload.session_ttl` is named in three documents and modelled in no configuration | P2 | TODO | Raised by `ENC-806`. `docs/03-LLD.md §15` gives it as the knob that decides when a staged object is reaped, and `crates/config` has no such key, so the number lives as a compiled-in constant in **two** places that cannot disagree loudly: `crates/api`'s 24 h session TTL (an honest placeholder since `ENC-690`) and now `crates/worker`'s `STRANDED_GRACE`. Neither is wrong today, and both are invisible to an operator who reads `enclave.example.yaml` looking for the one the document told them about. The fix is a modelled `upload:` section with `session_ttl` and a `stranded_grace`, read by the API for the session's `expires_at` and by the worker for the reclaim window — and the two are different knobs, so one key would be worse than none. Note that the *grace* must keep a floor: a zero would let a completion still in flight be read as stranded, which is currently a `const` assert in the worker binary and would have to become a validator |
| ENC-838 | The upload reaper's `deferred` count is a log line, not a metric | P2 | TODO | Raised by `ENC-806`. `ReapReport::deferred` is the number that says the object store is refusing deletes, and the pass's own documentation says "a number that stays high across passes is the signal, and it is the operator's to act on" — but the only place it appears is a `warn!`, so acting on it means grepping. Same shape and same reason as `ENC-637`/`ENC-648` for the content passes: metrics are their own row rather than smuggled into a wiring change. What this one needs beyond a counter is a *gauge of what is left*, because the interesting failure is bytes accumulating while every pass reports success on the handful it manages |
| ENC-839 | Nothing aborts an incomplete multipart upload, so a big abandoned upload's parts are never released | P2 | DONE | Raised by `ENC-806`, and it is the gap the reaper cannot close from inside the process. Deleting the staged key removes a *completed* object; the parts of a multipart upload that was never completed are invisible to `DeleteObject`, and `BlobStore` exposes no abort verb — `S3BlobStore::abort_upload` exists and takes an `UploadSession` the reaper does not have, because the provider's `upload_id` is on the row (`upload_sessions.multipart_id`) and the part list is not. So for every abandoned upload over `multipart_threshold_bytes` — 16 MiB by default, which is most of what this product is for — the reaper marks the row `EXPIRED` and releases nothing. The compensating control is the bucket lifecycle rule `AbortIncompleteMultipartUpload` (`docs/08-BYO-INFRA.md §5`), which is an integrator action nothing verifies; `S3BlobStore`'s start-up self-check already probes four public-access facts and could probe this one. Either that, or a `BlobStore::abort` the reaper can call from `multipart_id` alone | **Closed with the verb rather than the lifecycle rule.** `BlobStore::abort_multipart(key, upload_id)` takes the two values the reaper can read off a row — `upload_sessions.multipart_id` is there and the part list is not — which is also all `AbortMultipartUpload` needs; `S3BlobStore::abort_upload` keeps its `UploadSession` signature for callers that hold one. The trait method has a **default that returns `Unsupported`, never `Ok(())`**: a store that cannot abort must say so, because the caller's next act is to mark the row released, and silently succeeding would turn *nothing was freed* into *everything was freed* and put the parts beyond every later pass — worse than the leak. The default also means nine test doubles did not have to change. The reaper aborts **before** deleting and defers on failure, and the existing source-order assertion now covers it. The MinIO test is three acts because two are controls: the provider is holding the upload, `delete` does **not** release it — so the leak is demonstrated against this provider rather than assumed from AWS's documentation — and then abort does. Watched to fail by removing the `S3BlobStore` override: *Unsupported { capability: "aborting a multipart upload" }*. The lifecycle rule (`docs/08 §5`) stays a useful belt-and-braces for parts orphaned by a crash between mint and reap; probing it at start-up is still unclaimed |
| ENC-840 | `crates/scheduler` cannot say what it is running, which is the mechanism `ENC-806` was invisible for | P2 | TODO | Raised by `ENC-806` while deciding where the reaper belonged. `crates/worker` has `Scheduler::scheduled()` — a list of the passes this process will actually run, logged at start-up — and it is the reason a missing bucket reads as a line an operator sees rather than as a graph that never leaves zero. `crates/scheduler` has nothing equivalent: it runs `run_storage_reconciliation` unconditionally and announces only the interval, so "this process is reconciling quotas" and "this process is reconciling quotas over a tenant list it cannot read" look identical from outside. The binary's `has_platform_access` refusal covers exactly one of the ways that goes wrong. Not urgent while the crate has one pass; it stops being cheap the moment it has two, which is the point at which the `ENC-806` shape becomes available to it |

| ENC-846 | `docs/05-API.md` has **no change-log section** to add a row to | P3 | TODO | `CLAUDE.md`'s documentation discipline says to "bump the doc's version line and add a change-log row when the change is substantive". `05` has a version line and has never had a change log, so half of that instruction has silently not applied to the most-edited document in `docs/`. `ENC-825` bumped the version and had nowhere to record what changed. | ENC-847 | The reachability suite fails without `S3_ACCESS_KEY_ID` in the environment, and says only that the server did not start | P3 | TODO | Hit running `cargo test --workspace` with only `DATABASE_URL` set, which is what `CLAUDE.md`'s "run before pushing" list implies is enough. `Server::start` inherits those two variables deliberately (`ENC-796`, so no credential is written down), the child then refuses to start because `storage.s3.access_key_id` is an unresolvable reference, and the test spends 60 seconds polling `/health/live` before failing. The failure message does print the child's log, which is what makes it diagnosable at all — but the 60-second wait plus a message about liveness reads as a flaky test rather than a missing variable. | ENC-848 | The client's two workarounds for `ENC-825`/`ENC-826` are now dead weight, and three of its comments are false | P2 | TODO | Not fixed here: `web/**` was out of scope for the session that closed the server side, and a client change belongs with someone who can run the browser checks. Three things go stale the moment `ENC-825`/`ENC-826` merge. `entities/upload/store.ts` polls `GET /files/{id}/versions` and says in its own doc comment that it does so because `GET /uploads/{id}` "reports `SCANNING` and never changes" and `currentVersion` "carries no `isReadable`" — both now untrue, and the poll can move to `GET /uploads/{id}`, which is the endpoint that actually describes the upload and which now carries the `fileId` the store had to thread from the `complete` response. `features/libraries/peek/preview-tab.tsx` reads `versions` before asking for bytes for the same reason and can read `currentVersion.isReadable` from the detail it already has, removing a request per peek. `entities/upload/model.ts` carries the third comment. **The comments matter as much as the code**: each one explains a workaround by asserting a server limitation, and a false explanation of a correct workaround is how the next person concludes the workaround is still load-bearing. `CurrentVersion` in `entities/file/api-model.ts` is a `z.object`, not `z.strictObject`, so nothing breaks in the meantime — the new fields are stripped until the schema names them |
| ENC-829 | **Uploads above 16 MiB are refused: no S3 multipart checksum is the whole-object digest** | P1 | TODO | The cost of `ENC-820`, and it is a product regression stated rather than hidden. Above `multipart_threshold_bytes` (16 MiB, not operator-settable — `S3Config::from_operator_config` takes the compiled default) the store sends a multipart upload, and what S3 and MinIO compute for one is a *composite* checksum, base64 of the SHA-256 of the concatenated part digests with a `-N` suffix. That is not `file_versions.checksum_sha256`, so there is nothing to verify the client's whole-object digest against and the session is refused before a URL exists. AWS's `FULL_OBJECT` checksum type would close it and MinIO `RELEASE.2025-04-22` answers `InvalidArgument: Invalid checksum provided.` to it — probed directly, not assumed. **Blast radius today is zero and that is why the refusal was acceptable**: `ENC-827` records that the web client implements only `SINGLE` and fails a large file with `upload_multipart_unsupported`, so nothing that works today stops working. It blocks M1's fifth exit criterion (5 GB resumable upload), which is why it is P1 rather than P2. Three ways out, in order of preference: gate `FULL_OBJECT` on a probe at `connect` so a backend that supports it gets large uploads and MinIO does not; or have the client declare per-part digests, sign them into the part URLs and verify the composite the provider returns against the reported parts — which verifies the transfer but still leaves the whole-object digest unattested; or confirm the digest in the antivirus pass, which already streams every byte, and carry an evidence column on the version row until it does (needs a migration, so not doable inside this row's ownership) |
| ENC-830 | A missing required field on `POST /uploads` answers `field: "body"` rather than naming the field | P3 | TODO | Found verifying `ENC-820` by hand: `POST /uploads` with no `sha256` answers `400 VALIDATION_FAILED` with `details: [{code: "INVALID_FORMAT", field: "body"}]`. Correct and unhelpful — the handler decodes the body with `serde_json::from_slice` and renders `unreadable_body()` for any failure, so `libraryId`, `name` and `sizeBytes` behave identically and have since `ENC-690`. Not introduced here and not fixed here: pointing at the field means either mapping serde's path into a `FieldError` or decoding into an all-optional shadow struct and validating afterwards, and both are changes to how every route in `crates/api` reports a malformed body rather than to this one. `docs/05-API.md §8.1` states the actual answer rather than the one that would be nicer |

So the three refusals that use it — the library's per-file ceiling, the tenant's stored-byte quota and now `ENC-829`'s verification ceiling — are indistinguishable on the wire and none of them tells the user the number. Several rows in this file describe the limit as being on the response *specifically so a client can show headroom rather than a bare refusal* (`UploadError::StorageQuotaExceeded`'s own doc comment says it), which is not true of anything that ships. The fix is `details` entries on that arm, in `crates/api/src/error.rs`, which is outside `ENC-820`'s ownership |

The tests exist (`a_lying_client_cannot_store_an_object_under_a_digest_it_declared` against real MinIO, plus the two session tests), and the row does not, because `docs/12-TESTING.md` was outside that row's ownership. | ENC-850 | The indexing pass held one transaction across the object read, extraction, OCR **and** the embed | P0 | DONE | `main` went red on `25P03 — terminating connection due to idle-in-transaction timeout`, and the pass returned a *database* error for work that had nothing to do with the database. `idle_in_transaction_session_timeout` is 60 seconds by deliberate configuration — a transaction left open holds its `SET LOCAL app.tenant_id` — so the timeout was right and the pass was wrong. | ENC-853 | **`shared/ui` was five files while `features/` held 4,548 lines of CSS** | P1 | DONE | An 8:1 ratio between feature CSS and shared CSS is the inverse of a design system: every screen was restating the same surfaces. Measured rather than asserted — four byte-identical copies of one 44px state figure, six copies of one paragraph rule, seven copies of a request-ID row, three copies of the button, four of the classification badge, eight per-file answers to `prefers-reduced-motion` and three identically-shaped shimmer keyframes under three names. `features/**` CSS is **4,548 → 3,303 lines (−27%)**, and the shared layer that absorbed it is 571 → 1,197. The ratio is 8:1 → 2.8:1. **The line count is not the argument**; the defects the copies were carrying are: four of the seven request-ID rows omitted `unicode-bidi: isolate`, so one identifier rendered correctly on three screens and reversed on four; one of three "not built yet" row treatments added an `opacity: .5` the other two deliberately refused, which is `ENC-673` eroding from the inside; and the library list's avatars were light-theme hex and did not flip in dark mode. Each was fixed once and left broken two to four times over |
| ENC-854 | **Motion existed in the reference and nowhere in the tree** | P1 | DONE | `grep -c "transition\|--motion\|cubic-bezier" src/styles/tokens.css` returned **0**, while the prototype ran eight keyframes, two easings and three separate stagger steps. `styles/motion.css` names them from the reference rather than inventing them: `--ease-enter` is its `cubic-bezier(.2,.7,.3,1)`, `--dur-row` its `encIn .22s`, `--stagger-row` its `Math.min(i, 12) * 0.02s`. **The reduced-motion answer is the reason they are tokens.** `docs/09 §12` requires every animation to degrade, and expressed as literals that has to be remembered once per component — it was, eight times, in eight per-file media queries. One block now rewrites the tokens: `--travel-*` to `0px` and the durations to `1ms`, **not** `animation: none`, which would drop `animation-fill-mode: both` and snap every entering element back to its un-animated state. Indeterminate loops are slowed to 6s instead of cut, which is the answer `web/public/logo-loader.css` already gives and for the stated reason: a loop stopped dead freezes on an arbitrary frame and the mark reads as broken rather than still |
| ENC-855 | The tree had no spacing scale, no size scale and no type ramp — roughly 700 raw px | P1 | DONE | `styles/tokens.css` carried colour, three radii and the font families. Every other dimension in `web/src` was a literal, including the body font size `12.5px` in all ten feature stylesheets and the 44px figure's `13.75px` inset written out four times. `docs/09 §13` commits to an 8px spacing scale, a 4px radius scale and a type ramp; a commitment expressed as literals cannot be checked and cannot be changed — a density switch that has to find 700 numbers is a density switch nobody ships. `styles/scale.css` names them, counted from the reference rather than chosen: `height:26px` appears 63 times in the prototype and is `--ctl-h`; `font-size:12.5px` appears 61 times and is `--fs-body`. `[data-density='compact']` is now one declaration |
| ENC-856 | The four states of `docs/09 §11` were implemented five times, and none used the shared component | P1 | DONE | `features/{home,search,ask,admin,libraries/list}/states.tsx` each carried a private `Figure` helper that was character-for-character identical in four of them, and a local `{ retryable, requestId }` type in four — **none of which was `Failure`**, which is precisely why no feature could call the `surface-states.tsx` that already existed. The shared block now takes the three catalog keys that are genuinely per-surface (a title is a different fact per screen) and owns the other four parts. `DeniedPanel` still has no retry and none may be added: `docs/17 §7`, and a policy denial with a retry button teaches a user the product is flaky rather than that they lack permission |
| ENC-857 | **The locked classification palette had four uncoordinated readers** | P1 | DONE | `docs/09 §16a` locks `--c-pub … --c-restr` because *"a tenant recolouring Restricted to match its palette is a tenant whose users misread sensitivity at a glance"* — and four hand-maintained copies of the badge defeat that from the inside, whatever the token says. They had already drifted: block sizes 20/20/18/22px, dots 6/6/5/6px, type 11/11/10.5/11px, and three of the four carried an `unclassified` branch while the fourth did not. One implementation in `entities/classification/chip.tsx`, and `design-system.test.tsx` asserts it is the only stylesheet in the tree that reads a `--c-*`. **Locking a token and duplicating its only consumer locks nothing** |
| ENC-858 | Four of seven request-ID rows rendered the identifier reversed under RTL | P2 | DONE | Three of the seven copies carried `unicode-bidi: isolate; direction: ltr` on the `<code>` and four did not. A request ID is an opaque token, not text in the document's language; without the isolation an RTL reader sees its segments reordered while the clipboard keeps the original — so the user reads one string and pastes another, which is worse than either alone. Fixed by there being one `RequestId` |
| ENC-859 | The library list's avatars did not flip in dark mode | P2 | DONE | `.egl-avatar`'s four tone rules hardcoded light-theme hex (`#e0e7ff/#3730a3` and three more) while the two other copies of the same component read the `--av-*-bg`/`--av-*-fg` pairs, which flip. Not a styling preference: `tokens.css` says in its own header that the `--av-*` and `--selbar-*` sets are **pairs that flip together**, and taking a background from one theme and a foreground from the other is how a legible chip becomes an illegible one |
| ENC-860 | The unbuilt row treatment had drifted, in the one place `docs/17 §6` forbids it | P1 | DONE | Three implementations of "not built yet" on a row; one added `opacity: .5` and the other two deliberately did not. The rule is not cosmetic — the denial treatment is how a user learns DLP or a barrier stopped them, and if dimmed usually means *not written yet* they stop reading dimmed at all, on harmless surfaces first and then on the one that matters. The shared `Row` renders unbuilt as a non-focusable `<span>` rather than a `<button tabindex="-1">`, because a button is still announced as a button and is still reachable through a screen reader's control rotor, which does not consult `tabindex` |
| ENC-861 | `docs/17` had no component contract, so "what a component must expose" was folklore | P1 | DONE | `docs/09` owns behaviour and `web/design-system/` owns token values; the gap was the *component* rule. `docs/17 §12` now states it: a surface that appears twice is a component and a number that appears twice is a token; a component takes a `MessageKey` rather than a `string` so `CLAUDE.md` rule 12 is enforced by the type; a variant is a data attribute selected in CSS, not a class list assembled in TypeScript, so a state that has an accessible attribute is styled *from* that attribute and appearance cannot disagree with announcement; and **no component accepts `disabled: boolean`**, because that prop merges the three treatments `§6` requires to stay distinct and cannot be un-merged later. `web/src/shared/ui/README.md` is the inventory beside it, deliberately a map rather than a contract. Also corrected `§2`'s claim that the layer boundary is "enforced by an ESLint boundary rule" — there is no ESLint in this tree; the gate is `tools/lint-web.mjs` |
| ENC-862 | The accessibility gate's defence against checking one page N times was an argument, not an assertion | P1 | DONE | A previous session found `test:a11y` inspecting the sign-in screen 75 times and fixed it with a per-surface `ready` selector — sound, and it holds only for as long as every selector in the list happens to be absent from the sign-in screen, which nobody re-verifies when a selector changes. It was caught last time **only because the suite timed out**. `routes.spec.ts` now asserts directly that no surface except `/signin` renders `[data-signin-state]`. Watched to fail by renaming the stub's seeded `enclave_csrf` cookie, which reproduces the original bug exactly: the refresh short-circuits and every route concludes nobody is signed in. Both themes of the first surface failed by name. **94 axe tests pass**, 43 surfaces × 2 themes plus the emptiness assertion plus seven geometry checks |
| ENC-863 | **The reference's four-grey text ramp cannot be reproduced accessibly, and one step fails even as a graphic** | P2 | TODO | A decision for the repo owner, recorded rather than resolved either way. Measured on `--sheet`: `--fg` 18.44:1, `--fg2` 6.83:1, `--fg3` 4.84:1, `--fg4` **3.47:1** — so `--fg4` cannot carry text at any size this product uses. On `--sunken` even `--fg3` falls to **4.24:1**. Worse, dark-theme `--fg4` (`#5E5E59`) is **2.78:1**, which fails the 3:1 that WCAG 1.4.11 requires of a *graphical* object, so it is not safe for an icon or a rule either. The tree's current answer is that text uses `--fg2`/`--fg3` and `--fg4` is decorative, which quietly makes the ramp three steps for text and two in dark. Darkening `--fg4` to `#767670` reaches 4.57:1 but puts it four units from `--fg3` and collapses the two steps into one. **The choice is between four greys where the quietest is unreadable and three that are all legible**, and it belongs to whoever owns the palette |
| ENC-864 | Every button in the tree sat two pixels taller than the design it was extracted from | P2 | DONE | `.ui-btn` was `block-size: 28px`. The reference uses `height:26px` on 63 controls and 28 on eight, and `specs/library.md §2.2` names 26 for the Filter/Display/Upload cluster. `docs/09 §13`'s density brief is decided two pixels at a time, and a primitive that is 2px wrong is 2px wrong on every screen at once. Now `--ctl-h`, with `--ctl-h-sm` (24) for the controls the reference nests inside another control's row and `--ctl-h-lg` (28) for the segmented tab |
| ENC-865 | The z-index ladder was prose in a comment, and two floating surfaces independently picked 20 | P2 | DONE | `shared/ui/primitives.css` carried an accurate eight-step ladder in its header, unreachable from any stylesheet — so the search filter menu and the upload tray both arrived at `z-index: 20` by coincidence and neither author could have known. Same ladder, now `--z-accent … --z-toast` in `styles/scale.css`. A z-index written inline is a number whose only meaning is "larger than the one I saw last" |
| ENC-866 | Nothing in the repository let anyone *look* at the app beside the prototype | P2 | DONE | `web/design-system/enclave-client-prototype.html` renders in a browser and `tools/prototype-shot.mjs` captures it; there was no matching capture of the app that did not require a running Postgres, MinIO and API. `tests/shots/surfaces.spec.ts` is a non-gate Playwright project that writes one PNG per surface in both themes at the reference's 1440×900, reusing the accessibility stub. It asserts nothing on purpose — a pixel diff against the prototype would fail on fonts, on seed data and on every surface the server does not populate, and a gate that fails for reasons nobody can act on is a gate people learn to skip. Reading each other's markup instead of looking is how the divergence happened |
| ENC-867 | **`i18n/orphan-key` could never fire, and the double-quoted half of every reference was invisible** | P1 | DONE | Two bugs stacked in `tools/lint-web.mjs`, and the first hid the second. It collected `referencedKeys` by scanning **every** file under `src/` including `catalog.ts`, where each key appears as `'key': {` — so every key referenced itself, `referencedKeys` was identically `catalogKeys`, and the run reported "435 catalog key(s), 435 referenced" whatever the tree contained. Excluding the catalog surfaced **124** findings, of which 109 were false: the scan accepted single quotes only, so `label="upload.cancel"` — the commonest form there is — did not count. With both fixed, 15 real orphans: eleven per-feature state keys the shared components replaced, and four dead keys from unbuilt treatments withdrawn in `ENC-815`/`ENC-817`. A third change teaches it about `` t(`library.status.${v}`) ``, whose static prefix is a real reference to every key under it — counting only literals reported four live keys as dead and would have shipped a raw key into the peek panel. Watched to fail on a probe key. **This is `ENC-543`'s shape inside the tool that exists to catch `ENC-543`** |
| ENC-868 | The library screen still has no checkboxes, no selection bar, no filter chips and no saved views | P2 | TODO | What still diverges from the prototype after the design system landed, stated so it is not mistaken for done. Three groups. **Client-side and buildable now**: the checkbox column and the floating selection bar (`specs/library.md §4A(a)`, `§5`) — `capabilities` is already on every listing row, so the intersection the bar needs is an AND over booleans the server sent, not a re-derivation. **Waiting on the server**: saved-view pills need `savedViews[]`, the filter chip row needs a filter contract, the status column needs `status {code, tone}`, the Modified column's avatars need `modifiedBy`, and grouping by a metadata facet needs the group buckets — `specs/library.md`'s "Backend required" lists all of them. **Waiting on a decision**: the peek panel's Activity tab is blocked on `docs/17 §13 Q24`, because `audit_events` is hash-chained and deliberately not a user feed. The design system does not close any of these; it means each is now a component call rather than a stylesheet |

| ENC-017 | `security.txt` + PGP key published at casualoffice.org | P2 | TODO | Drafted at [`.well-known/security.txt`](.well-known/security.txt) and deliberately left unparseable: `Expires` and `Encryption` are `<REPLACE_ME>`, so an RFC 9116 reader rejects the file outright rather than half-trusting it if it is ever deployed unfinished. Confirmed still open — `https://casualoffice.org/.well-known/security.txt` 404s today while `SECURITY.md` tells reporters to fetch a PGP key from it. **Needs from the repo owner:** (1) confirmation that `security@casualoffice.org` reaches a monitored mailbox — the domain has Cloudflare Email Routing MX records so mail reaches *something*, but routing is per-address and not visible from here, and the address was transcribed from `SECURITY.md`/`README.md`, not chosen; (2) a PGP key, generated and published at a stable URL — no key material belongs in this repository (rule 11), which is why the field takes a URL; (3) an `Expires` date and the person who renews it. The GitHub advisory URL is on the file already because it is the one contact that can be verified from here. Publication is a step on whatever serves the apex domain — it is not this repository |
| ENC-018 | Confirm legal entity name on the LICENSE copyright line | P2 | TODO | Left untouched on purpose. A placeholder is the right answer in a draft `security.txt` and the wrong one in a copyright notice: "Copyright 2026 REPLACE_ME" is a defective grant, worse than the trade name already there, and Apache-2.0 §4 obliges downstream redistributors to carry whatever this line says. **Needs from the repo owner:** the registered entity name and its jurisdiction, or a decision that copyright is held personally. Two files carry the string and must move together — `LICENSE` line 189 and `README.md` §License |
| ENC-019 | Development roadmap: milestones, gates, sequencing, risks | P1 | DONE | Requested 2026-08-18 · `ROADMAP.md` |
| ENC-020 | Product rename Vault → Enclave; ID prefix `VLT-` → `ENC-` | P1 | DONE | Requested 2026-08-18. HashiCorp Vault references deliberately preserved |
| ENC-021 | Rename the working directory `services/vault` → `services/enclave` | P2 | BLOCKED | Assessed 2026-08-20 and the caution in the original note is aimed at the wrong risk. **Nothing in the repository depends on the path:** `git grep services/vault` matches this row and nothing else, the directory being renamed *is* the repository root so git tracks no name for it, the remote is `git@github.com:CasualOffice/enclave.git` and is unaffected by what the checkout is called locally, and CI runs from `$GITHUB_WORKSPACE` and never names it. What a rename does break is untracked local state: the 29 GB `target/` tree, whose artefacts and `.d` files carry absolute paths, forcing a full rebuild, and the working directory of every editor, shell and agent session currently open on the old path — including, if an agent were to run it, its own. **Blocked on the repo owner**, because it cannot be done from inside the tree and must be done with nothing running: `mv services/vault services/enclave` from `melp/`, with no repository change of any kind |
| ENC-022 | Initialize git repository, initial history, remote on `CasualOffice/enclave` | P1 | DONE | Requested 2026-08-18 · branch `main`, private |
| ENC-023 | M0 implementation plan (`plans/M0-FOUNDATIONS.md`) | P1 | DONE | Requested 2026-08-18 · task-level breakdown for Phase 0 |

Counts include completed items in their priority column. Update this table whenever a row's status or
priority changes; a stale rollup is worse than none — this one read "Phase 1: 0 done" while
twenty-four of its rows were merged, which is how it was noticed. It had drifted again by 2026-08-20,
nine rows behind, for the ordinary reason: the rows that get added in a hurry are the ones found
mid-task, and the table is a second edit. Recounted from the rows.

Derived from the rows themselves, not maintained by hand: every `ENC-` row in `§3` and `§4`,
deduplicated by ID with `§3` winning on status because it is the fresher of the two. A row that
appears only in `§3` belongs to the phase in flight when it was raised — Phase 0 below `ENC-119`,
Phase 1 from there up, and that is where `§2.3`'s ranges come from rather than the other way round.

| 2026-08-29 | **`ENC-698` — search stops calling every index unhealthy.** `crates/api/src/routes/search.rs` handed `Retrieval::decide` a hardcoded `VectorStore::Unreachable`, so every response carried `degraded: true`. It was *true* — `ApiState` held no vector index, no Milvus configuration and nothing that could build one — and that is exactly why it needed closing deliberately: when a corpus existed nothing about the route would have changed, and a healthy index would have kept being reported as a degraded search, which is the failure `docs/09 §10`'s header exists to prevent, inverted. The fix is `ApiState::vector`, an `Option<VectorRetrieval>` that binds a `VectorIndex` to the `Embedder` that can form a query for it — **both halves or neither**, because `candidates` takes an embedding and an API replica with a Milvus endpoint and no mounted model can probe the store and never read it. Three findings outlast the feature. **The composition had to move, not be copied**: `milvus_config` lived in `crates/worker/src/main.rs` and was the only copy, so `crates/api` could not reach it — two spellings would drift silently in both directions (a census counting a different collection from the one the pass writes reads as a coverage gauge that never rises; an API on a different endpoint reads as a search that finds nothing in a full store), so it is now `MilvusConfig::from_operator_config`, beside the type it builds, where `enclave_storage::S3Config::from_operator_config` already puts the same relationship. **The dense path is the one where the post-filter is the only control there is**: `crates/search/src/vector.rs` emits no `acl_tokens` or `barrier_tokens` clause because nothing computes them, so `PostFilter::confirm` is doing *all* of the access control on that path rather than confirming any of it — which is why the new route test proposes a **same-tenant**, granted-library, same-folder, same-owner file the caller may not read, so that no tenancy mechanism can be holding the property and the `file.metadata_read` resolution is the only layer left. Forcing `may_see = true` puts that document and its body on the wire. And **`ENC-695`'s literal `0` was a live trigger disarmed**, not a placeholder: with a reachable store it would have said *invalidation is keeping up* about a tenant whose denylist had overflowed. `denylist::in_force` supplies it, and the test that makes it load-bearing suppresses past the limit while the store reports `Available`. Its tenant predicate is asserted over the harness's **superuser** connection on purpose — deleting `WHERE tenant_id = $1` leaves the app-role test green, which is `docs/12 §1.2`'s recurring shape, and fails only where `FORCE ROW LEVEL SECURITY` is bypassed and the statement is all that remains. Five rows logged rather than absorbed: `ENC-890`–`ENC-894`. |

| 2026-08-28 | **The checksum an upload declares is now verified by the object store, and one it did not verify is refused** (`ENC-820`, closing `ENC-821`). Reproduced first: `sha256` of all zeroes over a real object answered `202` and persisted `0000…` on an immutable column. The design was already right — `complete` compares the client's digest against the store's — and **both ends of that comparison were missing**: nothing put the digest on the pre-signed `PUT`, so the provider computed none; and `head` never sent `ChecksumMode::ENABLED`, so a digest a provider *had* computed would have come back `None` anyway. Fixing either alone changes nothing observable, which is worth knowing before someone re-reads the row and reaches for the one-line fix. **The judgement asked for was what remains when a provider cannot confirm**, and the answer here is refuse — not because refusing is always right, but because the other two options were unavailable: recording the evidence level on the row needs a migration, and making it a deployment policy needs a `crates/config` key, both outside this row's ownership. That is a constraint, not a principle, and `ENC-829` is where the constraint gets revisited. What made refusing *acceptable* rather than merely available is that `file_versions.checksum_sha256` is `NOT NULL` and immutable: there is no way to write "this is the client's word" on the row, so the real choice was between a verified digest and no version — and a digest nobody checked is worse than an absent one, because absent reads as unknown and stored reads as proof. **`ChecksumEvidence` was deleted rather than given a consumer.** The type was honest and nothing acted on it; adding a caller would leave the next caller free to forget. `VerifiedContent` now cannot be constructed unless the provider confirmed, so "the digest was checked" is something the compiler knows. **The cost is stated, not buried**: uploads above 16 MiB are refused (`ENC-829`, P1, blocks M1's 5 GB criterion), because no S3 multipart checksum is the whole-object digest and MinIO rejects AWS's `FULL_OBJECT` type — probed, not assumed. Blast radius today is zero only because `ENC-827` already records that the client cannot do multipart. **The client is broken until `ENC-832` lands**, logged P0: the checksum header is signed, so a `PUT` without it fails the signature check, and `web/**` was outside this row's ownership. Every test was watched to fail by name before it was believed — eight breaks across the signed header, `ChecksumMode`, the multipart refusal, the malformed-digest refusal, `verify`'s new arm, the service's error mapping, the required `sha256` and the header map — and the one that matters runs against real MinIO, because a mock would only assert that the mock agreed with whoever wrote it. Two smaller findings logged rather than fixed in passing: `ENC-830` (a missing required field answers `field: "body"`) and `ENC-831` (`QUOTA_EXCEEDED` carries neither the quota nor the limit, so several rows in this file describe a response field that does not exist) |

| 2026-08-27 | **Sync becomes a subsystem** (`ENC-731`–`ENC-734`, delivering the first half of `ENC-311`). `crates/sync/src/lib.rs` was five lines and `docs/05-API.md §13` documented four endpoints that did not exist. **The hard part was the cursor and it is not a naming problem.** `docs/04 §15` documents `sync_cursors.cursor` as *"a monotonic change sequence"* and nothing in the schema produced one, so the choice was open. A timestamp is the obvious answer and is wrong under concurrent commits — two transactions read the clock in one order and commit in the other, and a client that polls between them stores a cursor above a change that has not landed and never sees it again, with every row in the database correct. A PostgreSQL `SEQUENCE` fails identically one layer down, and is the more dangerous answer because it *looks* like it is handing out order: `nextval` does not lock, does not roll back, and returns before its caller commits. What `migrations/0023` uses instead is a **transactional counter row** whose incrementing `UPDATE` holds a row lock to commit, so allocation order is commit order exactly and the visible sequence is always a contiguous prefix. The cost — per-scope write serialisation — is written into the migration rather than left to be discovered. The feed is appended by a **trigger on `files`**, on the same completeness argument RLS makes about tenant predicates: a crate that forgets to append does not fail, it produces a device quietly missing a file. **The other decision `docs/10` does not make** is the boundary of its own tombstone rule: `§4` requires an ineligible file to appear as a `TOMBSTONE` with a reason rather than be silently omitted, and a tombstone carries a *path* — so emitting one per file would hand a caller with no grant the complete contents listing of a library they may not browse. A file the caller cannot `file.metadata_read` is therefore omitted, and only a file they can see becomes a tombstone. **On the wipe**, what it guarantees is stated in the handler rather than implied: it stamps the request, moves the device to `WIPING`, and — the half that acts without the client — refuses every subsequent delta and reservation from that commit. `wiped_at` is stamped only by the device's own acknowledgement, so a machine that never comes back stays `WIPING` for ever, which the response says plainly through `wipeOutstanding`. Thirteen sites, `ENC-606`, not fixed here because the fix needs an audit sink on `ApiState` and `crates/api/src/main.rs` belonged to another session — acknowledged in the gate with the tracker row that owns it, printed on every run, and a fourteenth site fails the build. |
| 2026-08-22 | M4 step 3: DLP modes, and `SecurityFacts` threaded through the chain (`ENC-582`). The step that makes steps 1 and 2 do something, and the design work was almost entirely about making two failures *unwriteable* rather than merely tested against. **D28**: nothing that computes a verdict is told which mode is running — `RuleSet` has no mode field and `evaluate` takes no mode argument — so a `SIMULATION`/`ENFORCE` divergence needs a signature change before it needs a branch; and every record's "what would enforcement have done" is the *same* `DlpMode::effect` call with `Enforce`, so a simulation cannot report a decision enforcement would not have taken. **D26**: the engine gathers the snapshot once, before the first stage, and the DLP trait receives a `&FactsSnapshot` — there is no provider on it to call twice. Three things came out differently from the plan. **`facts_unavailable` and the DLP mode are different questions**, which the design does not say and which had to be decided: the tenant policy decides what an evaluation *concludes* without facts, the mode decides whether a conclusion is *acted on*, so a missing-facts denial is recorded under the three non-enforcing modes and refused under `ENFORCE` — because a tenant that has not enabled blocking has not asked DLP to block, and a mode that blocked anyway is one nobody can roll out, which is `§2`'s whole subject. **Two escalations were asking about inputs `require` was never given**, and they turned out to be one defect with two faces: `ENC-588`'s already-external share, and `ENC-591`, which nobody had logged — the `RESTRICTED` escalation took its rank from the caller, the only caller is the DLP stage, and it had nothing to pass when the scan had not run, so the rule was asked about `None` in exactly the case it exists for. Both are now `ResourceState` on the snapshot, gathered once, with no parameter to forget. **Three handlers were dropping obligations in release**: `me`, container browse and version history asserted `obligations.is_empty()` with `debug_assert!`, which the shipping binary compiles out — D29's third outcome, and `ENC-544` again. `Obligations::require_none` is a check that ships. Twelve deliberate breaks watched to fail by name. **One failed nothing**, and it is the finding worth keeping: stamping every observation `MONITOR` left all eleven mode tests green, because `MONITOR` and `SIMULATION` differ in *nothing* except that label — and the label is what `docs/06 §9`'s simulate-before-enforce gate reads, so the gate would have been answering from a field that lied while the behavioural suite stayed clean. An assertion was added and then watched to fail. What is **not** done and is logged rather than implied: nothing constructs `ModedDlp` in a running deployment and no provider reads `security_facts`, because `crates/api/src/main.rs` and `migrations/` were both outside this task's ownership (`ENC-594`). |
| 2026-08-22 | M4 step 5: the storage quota switched on (`ENC-589`). `ENC-584` shipped a correct charging statement that nothing called, which is the exact state `plans/M4-GOVERNANCE.md §2` is written against, and closing it turned out to be one question rather than three: **what does the counter mean?** The reconciliation job defines truth as `SUM(file_versions.size_bytes)`, so the charge has to sit where that row is inserted — anywhere else and the first nightly pass reads the charge as drift and subtracts it, making enforcement depend on when the job last ran. That settles the multipart/resumable question the task framed as a toss-up: not at initiation, not at completion, at commit. The reserve-time check stays, demoted to what `docs/05 §8` actually asks for — a bandwidth courtesy — and `Preflight` was given no admitting variant so it cannot quietly become the enforcement. The consequence is written into the code and asserted in a test rather than left in a comment: two sessions that each fit the same headroom are both issued, and the tenant is bounded at commit. The signature change is the real wiring — `VersionService::commit` takes `&mut TenantScoped` because `charge_storage` does, so a caller cannot hold the charge outside the transaction it bounds. Six breaks watched to fail. The one worth recording is the one that **did not**: moving the charge below the version insert failed only the source-order unit test, with all 24 database tests still green, because both orders roll back together — the ordering is a statement about what a caller is told and which lock is taken first, and it is unprovable behaviourally, which is why it is asserted against the source. The release half is honestly not done: nothing in this workspace destroys stored bytes, so `release_storage` stays on `unwired_report.py` under `ENC-597` rather than being given a call inside a function that refuses before reaching it — an edit that would have moved it from visibly unwired to invisibly unwired, which is the distinction that report exists for. |
| 2026-08-22 | M4 step 4: storage quotas (`ENC-584`). The enforcement was the easy half — `migrations/0008`'s shape applied to bytes — and the plan was right that the reconciliation job is the hard part. The answer is that **nothing writes an absolute figure**: the counter and the measured truth are read in one statement so they share a snapshot, and the difference is applied as `used_bytes = used_bytes + drift`, so charges committing during the pass keep their effect and nothing is ever locked out. Every absolute variant has the window somewhere. Two design pressures showed up only once the `CHECK` existed: a tenant can be legitimately over its limit, so the constraint needs an explicitly-recorded escape (`overshoot_bytes`) or the nightly job fails on that tenant forever; and a release that could abort would fail a *delete* on an accounting bug, which is D31's hostage situation by another road, so it saturates. Seven breaks watched to fail. Two findings worth the milestone's own rule: my source-scanning unit tests were wrong on first run — `split_once(" WHERE ")` keeps the `RETURNING` list, which names `limit_bytes` — and they failed *against themselves*, which is `docs/12 §1.2`'s warning landing for the third time in this repo; and removing the limit from the charging statement did **not** fail the exit-criterion test by its own assertion first, it failed because the `CHECK` aborted the transaction, so the backstop is doing more of the work than the assertion is. That is recorded rather than smoothed over, and a break the constraint cannot see was added to prove the assertion separately. |

Q16's structured-detector answer is expressed as an *absence*: `CandidateClass` is a closed enum of character classes and `validate(&Candidate) -> Verdict` has nowhere to put a pattern, so the custom-pattern request enterprise buyers open with cannot be served here by accident. Ten mutations watched to fail by name. The one that mattered was a defect of mine the tests found: the first tokeniser absorbed spaces and offered only the joined run, so it detected `4111 1111 1111 1111` and **missed an IBAN inside a sentence** — the clever case passing while the common one failed, which is the shape a green suite hides best. One mutation deliberately did **not** fail the test whose control it should have exercised, and is recorded rather than papered over: stopping the scan at the first match per candidate class leaves `a_scan_becomes_facts_that_carry_counts_and_no_content` green, because its two identifiers are in different classes — `every_instance_is_counted_not_merely_the_first` is the test that holds that property, and it fails. |

The fix in both cases is to separate the fetch from the verdict so they fail in different steps with different words, and to retry only the fetch: an assertion that gets three attempts is a machine for turning an intermittent security failure into a green build, and each retry site says so in a comment so nobody widens it later. `ENC-504`'s visible half is the same idea one layer in — the remedy `ENC-172` wrote was correct, on `Display`, and invisible, because `.expect()` prints `Debug`. `HarnessError` now renders its message and source chain; the checksum gate itself is untouched, and the test asserts `{:?}` rather than `{}` because asserting `Display` is exactly what let it stay broken. The client address is now resolved by walking `X-Forwarded-For` **right to left**, believing a hop only while the address being stepped past is itself inside a configured trusted network; the peer's `hops` is the budget and anything beyond it is discarded, never merged. The exit criterion — a forged header from an untrusted peer is ignored — is an assertion about an absence, so it is never asserted alone: the same resolver, the same header and the same configuration are run a second time with the peer genuinely trusted and the header *must* be honoured, which is what stops it passing against a resolver that ignores the header unconditionally or never runs. Two findings worth recording. `via_trusted_proxy` was documented as "the peer was a configured proxy", which is a fact about our deployment that no policy can act on; it now means "this address was relayed to us rather than observed", so a trusted proxy sending no header yields `false`. And zone membership had to move onto the *resolved* address — computing it from the peer would put every request behind a load balancer inside the load balancer's zone, which is the trusted one, so the mistake would have been silently permissive rather than noisy. Q19's separate rule set is a type separation, not a convention: `MachineCondition` has no `PostureBelow`, so the escape clause a single rule set needs on every posture rule has nothing to attach to. `system` is governed by the machine set because `crates/auth/src/claims.rs` maps a `typ: "system"` claim onto it — an exemption would have been reachable by anyone holding such a token — and the price, named rather than papered over, is that a machine allowlist omitting loopback refuses in-process work loudly. ---

## 7. If you are an agent working in this repo

- Read `§2` before doing anything. The no-pivoting rule is the one most likely to be broken by
  helpfulness.
- Check `§3` for what is in flight. If something is `WIP` and you were asked for something else, log
  the new request per `§2.2`, report what you logged and its priority, and continue the in-flight
  item.
- Assign priority yourself using `§1` — do not ask which priority unless the rubric genuinely does not
  decide it.
- Update the row's status when you start and when you finish, and update `§5` and `§6` in the same
  edit. The tracker being current *is* part of the task.
- If the work you were asked for is already a row, say so and use that ID rather than opening a
  duplicate.
- **Scratch files go in a directory of your own**, never a shared path like `/tmp/<module>.rs.bak`.
  Two sessions once picked the same name during a deliberate violation and one restored a `text.rs`
  from the wrong crate over the other's (`ENC-512`). Derive the directory from something unique to
  your session and keep every backup, patch and intermediate file under it.
- **`cargo fmt --all` edits every session's files, not just yours.** With parallel sessions running,
  prefer `cargo fmt -p <your-crate>`. Three sessions have now reported formatting another's tree
  mid-edit; none caused damage, and all three were right to flag it rather than hope.
- **Do not run a cleanup while other sessions are in flight.** Deleting `/tmp/*.bak` to reclaim disk
  is a change to somebody else's working tree if they are mid-edit. Same for pruning Docker images
  or `target/`. If disk is genuinely short, say so and let the sessions finish.
